The discrepancy arises from the Fact Find recording him as age 56 while the report states 55. Given the Fact Find completion date of 06/08/2025 is after his 06/01/1970 birthday, he would have turned 55 on 06/01/2025 and would be 55 at the Fact Find date of 06/08/2025. The Age field showing 56 in [P4] appears to be an error or forward-projection in the Fact Find system. However, the report's assertion of age 55 is more plausibly correct if the report date is before 06/01/2026. The tolerance guard permits age to differ by 1 year where fact find predates report, but here the Fact Find shows a later age (56) than the report (55), which is the reverse direction.   [G1.3] Employment details are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Client (Anthony) employment: (1) Retired from factory role after 35 years; (2) Worked part-time as driver for autistic children earning approximately £890 net per month but has since stopped working altogether (Unemployed); (3) Spouse (Yvette) works full-time as Medical Case Handler earning £28,000 gross per annum; (4) Spouse intending to reduce hours to 3 days per week from next year with intended retirement date 2026-12-16. Report states in contributions section: 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' without specifying what those earnings are.
    File:     Fact Find shows Anthony's employment: Net Basic Monthly Income £890.00, Total Gross Annual Earnings £14,000.00, listed as 'Employed' with occupation 'Driver'. Income changes section states 'No' expected changes. Cashflow documents show Anthony's DB Pension income of £22,000 in first year of plan and Employment income of £0 in the same year. Notes indicate: 'Retired, gets DB income, but works part-time. When will he stop? Defo 60?' and query about whether he quit before eligible for workplace scheme. For Yvette, Fact Find does not provide employment details but cashflow shows Yvette Employment income of £16,800 in first year, consistent with gross salary figure if part-time (28,000 × 60% = £16,800).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts Anthony is currently 'Unemployed' (stopped working as driver) but Fact Find records employment status as 'Employed' with occupation 'Driver' and gross annual earnings £14,000
      - Report states 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but Fact Find shows £0 employment income in cashflow first year, contradicting assertion of current earnings
      - Fact Find shows £890 net monthly basic income (£10,680 gross annually) but total gross annual earnings listed as £14,000, discrepancy of £3,320
      - Report references Yvette's £28,000 gross annual salary but does not specify whether this is current full-time or the intended part-time reduced amount; cashflow shows £16,800 employment income (60% of £28,000), suggesting part-time basis already applied
      - Report states Yvette 'intending on reducing her hours to 3 days per week from next year' with retirement date '2026-12-16' but Fact Find fact-finding date is 06/08/2025, making 'from next year' ambiguous as to implementation date
    Anthony's employment status presents internal inconsistency within the suitability report itself. The report extracts show four separate employment records: (1) Retired status from factory role (clearly historical), (2) Unemployed status from part-time driving role (which 'has since stopped'), (3) and (4) two records for Yvette. The canonical model lists the driving role with 'Unemployed' status, which contradicts the Fact Find entry showing 'Employed' as current status with ongoing occupation 'Driver' and active monthly income of £890. The Fact Find shows Basic Income entry with frequency noted and end date 06/08/2025, suggesting active income recorded at fact-find completion. However, the notes query 'When will he stop? Defo 60?' indicating uncertainty about continuation. The cashflow projection shows Anthony Employment income of £0 in year 1 (2026), which contradicts both the report's assertion of 'current earnings sufficient to support contribution' and the Fact Find's recorded £890 monthly income, unless the contribution is supported by pension income (£22,000 DB) rather than employment earnings. The Fact Find shows total gross annual earnings of £14,000 against recorded monthly basic income of £890 (£10,680 annualised), a £3,320 gap unexplained in the file. For Yvette, the report cites £28,000 gross but the first-year cashflow shows employment income of £16,800 (60% reduction), suggesting the part-time transition has already been reflected in projections. The retirement date of '2026-12-16' in the report appears to be an extraction artefact (formatted as a date) from the phrase 'reducing her hours from next year', which is qualitative, not a stated retirement date. The guard concerning intended retirement age notes to prefer most recent dated evidence; the Fact Find (06/08/2025) predates the suitability report and cashflow documents (dated 16/12/2025), so the cashflow's projection of £16,800 income represents the adviser's most recent understanding. The ambiguity in the canonical model ('current earnings are sufficient') combined with zero employment income in projections, coupled with the discrepancy between Fact Find employment status ('Employed') and extracted employment status ('Unemployed'), creates material inconsistency about whether Anthony has active employment income available to support pension contributions under tax rules (which typically require current earnings to claim relief).

  [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: Anthony receives a defined benefit pension from Aon of £22,000 gross per annum (approximately £1,700 net per month). Yvette works full-time as a Medical Case Handler earning £28,000 gross per annum, intending to reduce to 3 days per week from next year at pro-rata £16,800 gross annually. Current regular expenditure is approximately £2,493 per month (essential costs), plus £800 per month discretionary spending, plus £5,000 annually on holidays. This totals £3,710 monthly (£2,493 + £800 + £417). The report states total monthly net income is £4,900 and monthly surplus or deficit is £1,190. However, the report also states in cashflow modelling that 'Your current expenditure is approximately £2,206 per month' and total disposable income is £383.33 monthly. State Pension of £11,973 per annum expected from age 67 in 2037. Employment income 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but no current earnings are specified for Anthony after he stopped driving work.
    File:     The Fact Find [P1] records: Total Net Monthly Income £2,590.00; Total Monthly Expenditure £2,206.67; Total Monthly Disposable Income £383.33. Anthony's Basic Income is listed as £890.00 net monthly (part-time driving, which has since stopped per the report). Anthony's Private Pension Aon Final Salary is £1,833.00 gross monthly (£1,700.00 net monthly). Total Gross Annual Earnings stated as £14,000.00. The Cashflow Current Situation [P2] shows 'Money In' of £38,800 annually for 2025-2029, then £22,000 for 2030-2032, then £34,451 from 2033 onwards. Yvette's reduced income calculation [P9] shows £16,800.00 gross annually pro-rata, £1,301.30 net monthly. Fact Find [P21] quote confirms Yvette earns £28,000 gross per annum and Anthony receives £22,000 gross DB pension plus previously earned £890 net monthly from driving (now stopped). State Pension of £11,973 per annum confirmed [P21]. Cashflow assumptions [P13] show 0% surplus income percentage saved.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Anthony's total monthly net income contribution is £1,700 (DB pension only, as employment income has stopped), but Fact Find [P1] lists £2,999.67 total for Anthony including the £890 monthly employment income that is no longer active
      - Report asserts current total monthly expenditure is £3,710 (sum of £2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + £417 holidays), but simultaneously states in cashflow modelling that current expenditure is £2,206 per month—a difference of £1,504 monthly or 68%
      - Report states total monthly net income is £4,900, but Fact Find [P1] records total net monthly income as £2,590.00—a difference of £2,310 or 89%
      - Report states monthly surplus is £1,190, but Fact Find [P1] records disposable income as £383.33—a difference of £806.67 or 210%
      - Report's monthly expenditure figure of £2,206 used in cashflow (page 8) does not reconcile with stated actual expenditure of £3,710 (page 2) derived from £2,493 + £800 + £417
      - Report's total monthly income of £4,900 cannot be derived from stated components: £1,700 (Anthony DB pension) + estimated Yvette net of £1,800-2,100 (if full-time £28,000) = £3,500-3,800 net, not £4,900
      - Cashflow 'Money In' [P2] shows £38,800 annually for 2025-2029 but report components would give approximately £32,400 annually (£22,000 DB + £10,400 Yvette net equivalent), a difference of £6,400 or 20%
      - Cashflow transitions to £22,000 'Money In' from 2030 onward [P2], aligning with Anthony's DB pension alone, implying Yvette's income ceases or is not modelled—but report does not state Yvette stops working
      - Report references 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but specifies no current earnings for Anthony after stopping driving work—internally contradictory
    The requirement is to establish whether income and expenditure figures in the suitability report are consistent with evidence and with each other. Material discrepancies exist on both internal consistency and external corroboration. (1) INTERNAL CONTRADICTION ON EXPENDITURE: The report states two distinct total monthly expenditure figures—£3,710 on page 2 (sum of stated components: £2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + £5,000÷12=£417 holidays) and £2,493 elsewhere, versus £2,206 on page 8 (cashflow assumptions). The 68% variance between £3,710 and £2,206 is not explained by rounding or presentation; they address the same question and cannot both be correct. The report is internally inconsistent on a material figure used for affordability assessment. (2) INCOME MISMATCH WITH FACT FIND: Report's stated income components are £1,700 net monthly (Anthony DB pension) + estimated Yvette net approximately £1,800-2,100 if £28,000 gross, yielding ~£3,500-3,800 monthly. The report asserts total monthly net income of £4,900 but provides no source for the additional £1,100-1,400. The Fact Find [P1] records £2,590 total net monthly income, which is £2,310 below the report's assertion. The discrepancy cannot be explained by rounding (it is 89% of the Fact Find figure) or by inclusion of asset drawdowns, which would not be 'income'. (3) SURPLUS/DISPOSABLE INCOME MISMATCH: Report infers monthly surplus of £1,190 (income £4,900 less expenditure £3,710). Fact Find [P1] records total monthly disposable income as £383.33, a 210% variance. This is a material difference in the affordability assessment. (4) CASHFLOW INCOME RECONCILIATION: The cashflow [P2] shows 'Money In' of £38,800 annually (2025-2029). The stated income sources are: Anthony DB pension £22,000 + Yvette employment £28,000 = £50,000 gross. After tax (approximately estimated at £1,400 Anthony net, £2,100 Yvette net = £3,500 net = £42,000 annually for two earners is reasonable), the £38,800 figure could represent approximately this magnitude, but the report provides no explicit reconciliation showing how it derives £38,800 from stated gross incomes and tax. The Fact Find total of £2,590 monthly (£31,080 annually) differs materially. (5) CESSATION OF ANTHONY'S EMPLOYMENT INCOME: The report states Anthony stopped driving work 'and have since stopped working altogether'. His employment income ceased. However, the Fact Find [P1] still lists his income with £890 net monthly employment income recorded, though annotated as ended 06/08/2025. The report's statement in the contributions section that 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' contradicts the narrative that he is no longer working and creates ambiguity about whether any employment income is modelled. (6) YVETTE'S TRANSITION: The cashflow [P2] shows 'Money In' dropping to £22,000 from 2030 onwards. This aligns with only Anthony's DB pension continuing, implying Yvette's employment income ceases or drops to zero in modelling. However, the report does not explicitly state Yvette stops working; it states she 'intends on reducing her hours to 3 days per week from next year' (2026). The transition in 2030 (age 64/68) may reflect a retirement event not stated in the narrative. The £16,800 pro-rata calculation [P9] for 3 days per week is corroborated, but its use in cashflow is not explained. (7) EXPENDITURE REDUCTION ASSUMPTION: The cashflow modelling [P13] states '0% of any in-year surplus will be saved' but does not explicitly address how the £2,206 expenditure modelling assumption differs from the £3,710 stated actual expenditure on page 2. A reduction of £1,504 monthly (43% decrease) is a material assumption that should be disclosed and justified. (8) GROSS VS NET GUARD: The report mixes gross and net figures without always being clear on basis. The DB pension is stated as £22,000 gross and £1,700 net monthly—this conversion is approximately correct (£22,000÷12=£1,833 gross monthly; net ~93% = £1,700). However, Yvette's £28,000 gross is stated without a specific net equivalent in the report narrative, only estimated. The Fact Find provides no net figure for Yvette's current earnings. (9) STATEMENT ABOUT EARNINGS SUFFICIENCY: The report states 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but does not specify what 'current earnings' are after Anthony stopped work. This is either (a) referring only to Yvette's income, in which case it should say so, or (b) referring to income that contradicts the earlier statement that Anthony is no longer working. The ambiguity is a compliance risk. (10) RECOMMENDATIONS RELIANCE: The requirement is about completeness and consistency of income/expenditure data. The recommendation to contribute £3,600 (extracted from context as the advised contribution) depends on affordability derived from surplus income. If surplus is £1,190 monthly (report figure) rather than £383.33 (Fact Find figure), the recommendation's affordability basis is materially different. The report does not reconcile these figures or acknowledge the discrepancy. CONCLUSION: The report contains internal contradictions (two expenditure totals, two surplus figures) and external contradictions with the Fact Find (income total, disposable income, underlying earnings). Some variance is explained by the transition from current (with Anthony earning £890/month driving, which has stopped) to modelled scenarios (post-2026 with Yvette reduced hours, or post-2030 retirement). However, the report does not make these assumptions explicit and does not reconcile its stated current figures with the Fact Find's record. A material portion of the discrepancy remains unexplained.

  [G1.5] Assets and liabilities are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts the following assets: Main Residence 27 Reigate Road, Urmston, Manchester valued at £400,000 (mortgage-free); £100,000 inheritance from late father's estate (due this month); £40,000 Chartered savings account; £20,000 Cash ISA (client C1); £20,000 Cash ISA with Moneybox (client C2 - Yvette); £10,000 emergency fund.       - Report states total assets of £669,092 but fact find [P18] header shows Anthony Barnes total of £590,000 — a difference of £79,092, matching the pension value discrepancy.

      - Report liquid assets total £190,000 (£100k + £40k + £20k + £20k + £10k) but cashflow [P6] shows opening liquid assets and investments total £142,326, and [P9] current situation shows opening liquid assets & accessible pensions £225,018 — neither matches £190,000.
      - Fact find [P18] does not separately itemise a 'Personal Pension' value in the assets table; pensions are listed in a separate 'Defined Contribution Pensions' section in [P6] at £82,692, not included in the £590,000 total.
      - Report references 'Aviva ISA' in [P6] cashflow as '£20,000 ISA Stocks & Shares' but [P18] lists '£20,000 MoneyBox Cash ISA' — asset description and provider mismatch.
    The report asserts a total net asset value of £669,092 comprised of three components: Main Residence (£400,000), Cash & Savings (£190,000), and Personal Pension (£79,092). The evidence from the fact find and cashflow documents shows internal inconsistencies that bear on the accuracy of the reported position. The property value of £400,000 is corroborated by [P1] and [P18]. The main liabilities are absent, which is corroborated. However, the liquid assets and pension values do not reconcile consistently across documents. The pension value stated in the report (£79,092) contradicts the opening value in the cashflow [P6] (£82,692), a £3,600 discrepancy. The liquid assets reported as £190,000 (inheritance £100k + savings £40k + ISAs £20k + £20k + emergency £10k) do not match the cashflow opening figures: [P6] shows £142,326 in savings & investments and £82,692 in pensions (total £225,018), or [P9] shows £225,018 total liquid assets & accessible pensions. The report's decomposition of liquid assets as £190,000 appears to exclude the inheritance of £100,000 in the opening position but includes it in narrative, which may reflect timing (inheritance 'due this month'). However, this timing distinction is not explicitly clarified in the report. The pension value discrepancy between £79,092 and £82,692 is material and unexplained. The ISA description varies between 'Aviva ISA' (stocks & shares) in [P6] and 'MoneyBox Cash ISA' in [P18], suggesting either a data capture error or asset repositioning not noted in the report. These are data quality issues affecting the completeness and internal consistency of the assets schedule.

  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The extraction report identifies three internal inconsistencies: (1) Platform charge stated as 0.32% in existing arrangements table but 0.54% in pension switch charges table (0.54% appears to be platform 0.32% plus weighted fund charges 0.22%); (2) Total liquid assets stated as £190,000 with components listed as £100,000 inheritance + £40,000 Chartered savings + £20,000 client Cash ISA + £20,000 Yvette Cash ISA + £10,000 emergency fund = £190,000, with potential ambiguity over Moneybox ISA reference; (3) Existing pension holdings have combined risk level of 6 (LifeStrategy 60% = risk 5, LifeStrategy 80% = risk 7), client agreed risk profile is 4, recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II is risk level 4 aligning with client profile, but switch charges table shows '+0.08%' increase in total recurring charge (from 0.54% to 0.62%) despite switch rationale citing 'Lower Costs', with new platform charge 0.21% (0.11% reduction) but advice fee of 1% per annum added.
    File:     This requirement is assessed from the report alone — it compares the report against itself. There is no supporting document to corroborate it. The extraction metadata itself reports these inconsistencies as observations of what the report contains, not evidence from external documents.
    Discrepancies:
      - Platform charge basis differs: 0.32% stated in existing arrangements context versus 0.54% stated as 'Current Annual Recurring Charge of Existing Plan' in switch comparison — unclear whether 0.54% is total recurring charge (platform plus fund charges) or platform charge alone
      - Liquid assets components sum to £190,000 as stated but ambiguity exists around whether Moneybox ISA reference creates a distinct cash holding or is another name for an already-counted holding
      - Charge increase of 0.08% (0.54% to 0.62% total recurring) contradicts the stated switch rationale of 'Lower Costs', and the interplay between platform charge reduction (0.32% to 0.21%), fund charges, and added advice fee (1% per annum) is not clearly reconciled in the report's narrative
    The extraction process identified three areas where the report's own statements require clarification. First, on platform charges: the report states 0.32% in one table and 0.54% in another. The extraction observes that 0.54% may be platform (0.32%) plus weighted fund charges (0.22%), suggesting the tables use different scope definitions without explicit statement. This is a labelling and disclosure issue within the report itself. Second, on liquid assets: the components sum correctly to the stated total of £190,000. The ambiguity flagged is whether a 'Moneybox ISA' reference elsewhere in the report denotes a separate holding or refers to one of the two £20,000 Cash ISAs already counted. The extraction notes this creates potential confusion but the arithmetic itself reconciles. Third, on charges and recommendation rationale: the report recommends a switch citing 'Lower Costs', yet the total recurring charge increases by 0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62%). The platform charge decreases from 0.32% to 0.21%, a reduction of 0.11%, but this is more than offset by fund charges and the addition of a 1% per annum advice fee. The report does not clearly explain why, given this net increase, the switch is justified on cost grounds. The derived figures confirm that £63.00 at 0.08% implies a fund value of £78,750.00, which reconciles to the stated Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd current value. The inconsistency is not arithmetic but narrative: the recommendation's cost justification is not supported by the charge figures presented.

  [G1.11] Facts used in calculations, cashflow modelling, projections and assumptions match the facts stated in the report narrative
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report states cashflow modelling was performed (wasCashflowModelled = true) with assumptions disclosed including: General expenditure inflation 2%, Savings inflation 1.25%, State pension inflation 2.5%, Property value growth 4.7%, Lending growth 4%, Aviva Platform Investments growth 4.5%, Mid growth rate 2.94%, Surplus income treatment 0%, Current expenditure £2,206 per month, Retirement age 60, and Yvette's retirement from 2027 onwards.     File:     The cashflow models in the evidence pack [P6], [P10], [P9] show inflation assumptions of: GENERAL 2%, STATE PENSION 2.5%, PROPERTY 4.7%, LENDING 4%, matching those in the report. The expenditure continuation on death is stated as 80% reduction for both Anthony and Yvette in [P6], [P8], [P10]. The Surplus Income Percentage Saved is confirmed as 0% in [P6], [P8], [P10]. The retirement age of 60 appears in [P18] but as 'Retirement age for projections' context, not the baseline timeline. The cashflow models show Anthony's state pension age as 67 and death events at age 104 in baseline, age 70 in 2040 scenario [P8]. Yvette's retirement timing references show she is 59 years old, not projected to retire from 2027 (when client age 60) but her work is shown as 3 days per week at £28,000 gross per annum. The high inflation stress test uses 6% [P21] but the report assumption extraction references 6% as a scenario, not a disclosed assumption.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report assumes Savings inflation 1.25% but no cashflow model in the evidence discloses this assumption
      - Report assumes Aviva Platform Investments growth 4.5% but no cashflow model discloses this separate assumption
      - Report states current expenditure £2,206 per month but this figure does not appear verbatim in any retrieved passage; nearest match is £2,074 in cashflow data
      - Report states Yvette's retirement timing 'From 2027 onwards (when client reaches age 60)' but evidence shows she is 59 years old and working 3 days per week, with no explicit retirement event at that date in the timelines
      - Report projects retirement age 60 for client but [P8] and [P10] show client born 06/01/1970, making age 60 in 2030, not 2027; state pension age shown as 67 in 2037
      - Report assumes mid-growth rate 2.94% which appears in [P18] but this is labeled 'Like for Like' comparison context, not a cashflow inflation assumption
      - Report quotes page numbers for assumptions (e.g., page 24 for inflation, page 23 for surplus income, page 13 for current expenditure) but the actual cashflow modelling documents show these on different structure without page numbers as described
    The report's extracted assumptions show several stated but undisclosed or unverified values. The core inflation assumptions (General 2%, State Pension 2.5%, Property 4.7%, Lending 4%) are corroborated by the cashflow models in [P6], [P8], [P10]. The Surplus income treatment of 0% and expenditure reduction on death are also confirmed. However, the Savings inflation rate of 1.25% and Aviva Platform Investments growth of 4.5% do not appear in any retrieved cashflow document, suggesting either they were not fed into the models or they are assumptions applied elsewhere (possibly in fund projections rather than household cashflow). The current expenditure figure of £2,206 per month appears in the report's narrative section [per the extraction quote from page 13] but does not appear in the cashflow tables retrieved; the closest figure in cashflow data is £2,074. The retirement age assumption states age 60, but the client birth date 06/01/1970 from [P8] and [P10] would place age 60 in 2030, not aligning with the stated 2027 Yvette retirement timing. The page number references (24, 23, 13, 22, etc.) in the provenance fields cannot be verified against the cashflow documents which do not use traditional page numbering. These discrepancies suggest either extraction ambiguity or modelling outputs that do not fully reconcile with the narrative facts as stated.

  [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts six objectives in priority order: (1) Ensure investments match agreed Cautious Balanced risk level (Risk Level 4); (2) Invest inheritance tax-efficiently using a GIA and consider transferring Cash ISA to Stocks & Shares ISA; (3) Provide flexibility and growth to support desired retirement income of approximately £2,200 net per month; (4) Simplify administration by keeping pension, ISA and GIA on one Aviva Platform with ongoing professional oversight; (5) Take advantage of pension annual allowance with contribution of £3,600 gross (£2,880 net) in 2025/26 tax year; (6) Do not wish to move Chartered savings account at this time. All are marked as client-stated with priority ranks 1–6.
    File:     The Fact Find [P3] records retirement goals and objectives notes stating: 'Review his Aviva pension and look to improve upon it. Client will complete ATRQ in his own time. Wants to be fully retired by 60. Min income in retirement is £2,200 net per month. Also looking at investment advice on £60k of his Dad's property money coming in this month, and the £40k cash he currently has so as to avoid paying tax on interest. He has already used his full ISA allowance this year, so has his wife. Potentially interested in transferring his cash ISA to S&S ISA but would have to be really worth his while - not dead set on doing this.' [P15] and [P16] contain adviser notes: 'About to receive circa £100k but will use £40k on kitchen/bathroom. Has another £40k in high interest savings, but only look at investing the £60k. Possibly move ISAs – not likely though.' and 'Retired, gets DB income, but works part-time. When will he stop? Defo 60?'. The Risk Profile Report [P10] states 'Agreed risk level 5' in one section but also confirms 'Risk profile 4' in the table of contents. [P18] records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both investment target and agreed risk level discussions. The Suitability Report itself [P12] states the recommended fund 'demonstrates lower volatility than your current mix of Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% equity funds, which have a combined risk level of 6' and that the client agreed Risk Level 4.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report objective 1 (risk level alignment to Risk Level 4): Evidence shows agreed risk level recorded as both 5 [P10] and 4 [P10 table]; the Risk Profile Report [P18] records no notes on how risk level was agreed
      - Report objective 2 (tax-efficient investment using GIA and ISA transfer): Fact Find [P3] states client is 'Potentially interested in transferring his cash ISA to S&S ISA but would have to be really worth his while - not dead set on doing this', and adviser notes [P15] say 'Possibly move ISAs – not likely though'. The report presents this as a firm objective; evidence shows it was contingent and uncertain
      - Report objective 3 (£2,200 net per month retirement income): This figure is evidenced in [P3]. However, the employment status and ability to contribute is unclear: [P15] and [P16] note client is 'Retired, gets DB income, but works part-time' and 'quit before he could' enrol in workplace scheme. The extraction summary flagged ambiguity at /financialPosition/income/0/amount stating 'Current employment income appears to be zero, but report states contributions section that current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level without specifying what those earnings are'
      - Report objective 4 (simplify by consolidating on Aviva Platform): Not explicitly evidenced in fact find or needs analysis documents
      - Report objective 5 (£3,600 gross pension contribution in 2025/26): Report asserts this; [P15] records 'We need to assess earnings for this year, and pension contributions for this year. Does he have a P60/P45? Possible pension contribution can be done if he's still working – none.' The adviser notes indicate this was not confirmed at time of fact find
      - Report objective 6 (do not move Chartered savings): Extraction summary notes this appears in the canonical objectives list but extraction ambiguity states 'OBJ6 should be classified under constraints rather than objectives' and 'appears as a fact in the Your current situation section rather than as a stated objective'. Not listed as an objective in [P3] Fact Find objectives section
    The report asserts six client objectives with priority ranking 1–6, all marked as client-stated. The evidence base allows partial corroboration but reveals material inconsistencies and gaps. Objective 1 (Risk Level 4 alignment) is supported by the agreed profile evident in [P12], but the Risk Profile Report [P10] shows conflicting records: a table cites 'Agreed risk level 5' while the contents page shows 'Risk profile 4', and [P18] records no discussion notes on how the risk level was agreed—a control gap given that risk is fundamental to suitability. Objective 2 (tax-efficient investment and ISA transfer) is partially supported: [P3] confirms the inheritance investment need and the £2,200 target; however, the ISA transfer is recorded in [P3] as conditional ('would have to be really worth his while - not dead set on doing this') and in [P15] as unlikely ('Possibly move ISAs – not likely though'). The report presents this as a primary objective without qualification; the evidence shows client reluctance. Objective 3 (retirement income target of £2,200 net per month) is clearly evidenced in [P3]. However, the capacity to achieve it via the recommended £3,600 contribution is undermined by ambiguity over current earnings. The extraction summary flags that 'current employment income appears to be zero' after the client 'quit before he could' enrol in a workplace scheme, yet the report claims in the contributions section that 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' without stating what those earnings are. [P15] and [P16] confirm uncertainty: adviser notes ask 'When will he stop? Defo 60?' regarding part-time work and note he has 'got DB income, but works part-time'. This is a material inconsistency affecting objective achievability. Objective 4 (consolidate on Aviva Platform for simplification) is not explicitly recorded in the Fact Find objectives section [P3] or in the adviser notes [P15]–[P16]. It appears to be an adviser-proposed solution rather than a stated client objective. Objective 5 (£3,600 gross pension contribution in 2025/26) cannot be reconciled with the employment status evidence. The adviser notes [P15] state 'Possible pension contribution can be done if he's still working – none' and query whether he has a P60/P45. The contribution recommendation rests on unstated or absent current earnings. Objective 6 (do not move Chartered savings) is marked as a client objective in the canonical model but the extraction summary correctly notes it appears in the Fact Find as a stated fact ('Do not wish to move Chartered savings account at this time' is a constraint, not a primary objective). The prioritisation of this as objective 6 is questionable. Guard: Advisers do paraphrase objectives; the substance of objectives 1–3 and 5 are substantively supported by evidence despite wording variation. The guard does not excuse the conditional framing of objective 2 being presented as firm, nor the absence of earnings evidence for objective 5, nor the conflicting risk-level records for objective 1. Guard: Objective 4 (platform consolidation) is not present in the evidence and the recommendation is not shown to bear on an evidenced client need; it remains unsupported. The overall picture is that core objectives around retirement income, risk tolerance and inheritance planning are evidenced but that the supporting detail—employment status, current earnings, actual client commitment to an ISA transfer, and the derivation of the specific contribution amount—contains material gaps and inconsistencies that undermine confidence in the suitability assessment. The fact that the Risk Profile Report records no discussion notes on risk level agreement, combined with conflicting records of whether it is 4 or 5, creates a control gap affecting objective 1. The absence of clear confirmation of current earnings undermines the feasibility of objective 5. The conditional nature of the ISA transfer in evidence versus its presentation as a firm objective in the report is a disclosure gap.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states life expectancy for a 55-year-old male (the client Anthony) is approximately 84 years old, and for a 59-year-old female (Yvette) is 87. The report projects funds will last well beyond average life expectancy. The pension switch analysis uses retirement age 60 as the comparison basis for projected values. The report references target age 60 in the Fact Find. The cashflow projections show retirement events at age 67 for Anthony (in 2037) and age 67 for Yvette (in 2033), with death events at ages 104 and 107 respectively.
    File:     The evidence shows multiple retirement ages across documents. The Fact Find [P23] states target age 60. The pension switch report [P6] uses retirement age 60 for the like-for-like comparison of projected values. Cashflow scenarios [P8], [P10], [P12], [P13], [P14], [P18] consistently show: Anthony born 06/01/1970 (age 55 in 2025), retirement event 'Retire' age 67 in 2037; Yvette retirement event 'Retire' age 67 in 2033. Anthony's state pension age is 67, Yvette's state pension age is 67. Death events are projected at age 104 for Anthony (2074) and ages ranging from 107 to 108 for Yvette depending on scenario. The risk profile questionnaire [P3] confirms client understanding of pension planning is 'Very little understanding/knowledge'. No document evidences a client statement that retirement target age is 60, nor clarifies whether age 60 is an aspiration, a pension access date, or a planning assumption separate from state pension age 67.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report life expectancy for female stated as 87; nearest matching figure in evidence is 85 in [P17] (Yvette death age in scenario), difference of 2 years (2.3%)
      - Retirement age 60 used in pension switch analysis [P6] and referenced in Fact Find [P23] as target age; cashflow scenarios [P8], [P10], [P12], [P13], [P14], [P18] show retirement events at age 67, not age 60
      - Report asserts funds projected to last beyond life expectancy but does not distinguish between: (a) capital sufficiency to age 84/87 or beyond; (b) income adequacy from state pension + investment drawdown from age 60 vs age 67; (c) the planning assumption on which projections are based
    The evidence reveals a material inconsistency in investment and planning horizons. The pension switch analysis [P6] explicitly compares projected values 'at retirement age 60'. The Fact Find [P23] lists 'Target Age: 60'. However, every cashflow projection in the file [P8], [P10], [P12], [P13], [P14], [P18] models retirement events at age 67 for both clients, not age 60. Age 67 is also their state pension age. The distinction matters: a 7-year gap between a pension access or interim withdrawal age (60) and full retirement or state pension commencement (67) creates different investment horizons for different tranches of capital and different income requirements. The report states that 'based on these statistics and your robust financial position, your funds are projected to last well beyond average life expectancy' [P22 content reflected in life expectancy assertion]. However, no document in the file shows the underlying cashflow or projection model that produced this conclusion or explains which life expectancy assumption (84, 87, 104, 107) was used as the basis. The stated life expectancy of 87 for the female cannot be confirmed — the closest figure in the evidence is 85 (Yvette death age in one scenario [P17]). The report's life expectancy statement appears to derive from ONS general population statistics rather than client-specific modelling; the actual planning horizon used in the financial projections is to ages 104/107+, which contradicts the reliance on ages 84/87 for the adequacy conclusion. The client's understanding of pension planning is recorded as 'Very little understanding/knowledge' [P3], yet the report does not evidence explicit discussion of whether age 60 represents a planning assumption, a pension crystallization point, or the actual intended retirement date. The vulnerability overlay is relevant: limited pension knowledge combined with an unstated assumption about retirement timing (60 vs 67) and unclarity about whether funds are modelled to cover a 47-year horizon (to age 104) or a 32-year horizon (to age 87) represents a material gap between the stated and evidenced planning basis.

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts two access requirements: (1) Planned access of approximately £40,000 from inheritance for renovation of main residence, required from 2025-12-16, funded from 'Inheritance from late father's estate' [P19]; (2) Regular drawdown access to pension funds in retirement via Flexi Access Drawdown from Aviva Pension Portfolio [P24]. The report also states an emergency fund of £10,000 is held and 'You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital, either immediately or on short notice, to meet any unexpected emergencies' [P19].
    File:     The Fact Find [P18] confirms an Emergency Fund of £10,000 with no shortfall. The cashflow scenarios [P1-P16] model regular expenditure and withdrawals across multiple life scenarios, showing income and outflows reconciled through accessible pensions and liquid assets. Scenario tables [P2, P11] show annual cashflow from 2049-2062 with regular money in/out and in-year surplus/deficit tracking. The cashflow modeller documentation [P3-P10] defines liquid assets, accessible pensions, and withdrawal types (Required withdrawal Investments; Required withdrawal Pensions). The suitability report [P21] confirms 'The model assumes you will utilise liquid assets as required to support your desired lifestyle in retirement before accessing your investments' and references input of Aviva Platform Pension £79,092 plus £3,600 contribution, Aviva Investment Portfolio (GIA) £57,120, and Aviva ISA Portfolio £20,000. Timeline in [P4] shows client (Anthony) 'Retire' age 67 in 2037, confirming the retirement horizon. However, no specific evidence document explicitly states or evidences the £40,000 renovation requirement by date 2025-12-16, nor does any evidence document independently confirm the inheritance amount, timing, or intended use.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states a planned £40,000 renovation expenditure required from 2025-12-16 funded from inheritance, but no evidence document independently corroborates this specific amount, date, or planned use.
      - The report states the client has confirmed sufficient liquid capital for emergencies, but no signed client confirmation letter or communication is presented in the evidence to independently verify this statement.
      - The report names Flexi Access Drawdown as the client's stated preference for pension access, with supporting statement in [P24] that 'You understand the pension freedoms and prefer the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown', but no independent client document (e.g., signed letter, meeting notes, or response to adviser) is present to evidence the client made this statement.
    The comparison is performed because the access requirements can be evaluated against the evidence available. The emergency fund requirement is met: both report and evidence (Fact Find [P18]) confirm £10,000 held with no shortfall. Regular pension drawdown in retirement is supported by the cashflow modelling which assumes utilisation of liquid assets and accessible pensions in retirement, consistent with the Flexi Access Drawdown mechanism described. However, three material pieces of evidence are absent from the file: (1) no independent corroboration of the £40,000 renovation need, the date 2025-12-16, or the source as inheritance; (2) no standalone client confirmation of sufficient emergency liquidity; (3) no client-authored or independently documented statement of preference for Flexi Access Drawdown. The renovation amount and date appear in the report's own extract from itself ([P19] quotes the suitability report page 2), not in a separate Fact Find, meeting notes, or client letter. The statement 'You have confirmed' in [P19] is an assertion the report makes about what the client said, not evidence of what the client said. Under the rule that a consistency requirement is met when the report's assertion is corroborated by evidence, and that the report cannot corroborate itself, these three statements stand unsupported. The cashflow evidence shows the plan models drawdown and liquid asset utilisation consistent with a scenario where £40,000 is deployed for renovation (scenarios show liquid asset drawdowns and planned expenditure), but does not independently state the £40,000 renovation figure or confirm it is client-authorised. The access requirements themselves (emergency, planned renovation, regular pension drawdown) are clearly identified by the requirement definition. The material issue is that the client-authored basis for two of the three access requirements is not evidenced by any document other than the report itself.

      · Suitability Report - Barnes.md [I] (P19)

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts five foreseeable changes: (1) Yvette's intended retirement when client reaches age 60 (2030-12-16), with loss of £16,800 gross employment income; (2) Client received £100,000 inheritance from late father's estate due this month (2025-12-16); (3) Yvette's reduction to 3 days per week employment from next year (2026-01-01), with loss of £11,200 gross income; (4) Both clients become eligible for State Pension (2037-12-16), with combined income of £23,946 gross; (5) Pension IHT rule change from 6 April 2027 affecting DC pensions and death benefits.
    File:     The case file contains multiple financial projections and scenarios. Passages [P6], [P8] state Yvette's 'Retire' event at age 67 in 2033, not age 60 in 2030. Passages [P1], [P2], [P3], [P7], [P19], [P23] show cashflow projections with liquid assets of £142,326 in 2025 consistent with £100,000 inheritance received. Passages [P2], [P8] reference State Pension of £11,973 from age 67 (one individual, not both). Passage [P10] notes 'About to receive circa £100k but will use £40k on kitchen/bathroom' and states 'Both used ISA allowance' but no explicit evidence of Yvette's hours reduction or its implementation date. Passages [P6], [P8], [P15], [P16], [P17] define State Pension age for Anthony as 67 (born 1970, reaching SPA 2037) and Yvette as 67 (born 1966, reaching SPA 2033), not a combined 2037 event. Passage [P9] correctly states the 6 April 2027 IHT rule change. No passage explicitly confirms Yvette will reduce to 3 days per week from 2026-01-01.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Yvette's retirement at age 60 (2030-12-16); file shows Yvette's retirement event at age 67 (2033) in cashflow scenarios [P6], [P8]
      - Report asserts financial impact of Yvette's retirement as loss of £16,800 gross; no supporting calculation or employment income figure in evidence for this amount
      - Report states Yvette's hours reduction to 3 days per week from next year (2026-01-01) with £11,200 loss; file contains no explicit confirmation of this change or its timing
      - Report states both clients become eligible for State Pension in 2037-12-16 with combined income £23,946; file shows State Pension age 67 for both (Anthony 2037, Yvette 2033), with individual rate £11,973, not combined at same date
      - Report cites £100,000 inheritance due 'this month'; cashflow [P10] states 'About to receive circa £100k but will use £40k on kitchen/bathroom', suggesting only £60k net available for investment, not £100k invested
    The report identifies five foreseeable changes. Examination against file reveals material discrepancies on three: (1) Yvette's retirement age and date conflict directly with cashflow projections [P6], [P8] which show Yvette's retirement event at age 67 in 2033, not age 60 in 2030. The report's statement that 'Yvette plans to fully retire when you turn 60' appears unsupported; the scenarios model retirement at state pension age 67. (2) Yvette's hours reduction is stated in the report as 'intending on reducing her hours to 3 days per week from next year' but no passage confirms this intention, the timing (2026-01-01), or the £11,200 income impact. Notes [P10], [P11] contain uncertainty ('When will he stop? Defo 60?') but no resolution in the file. (3) State Pension eligibility: the report states 'Both clients become eligible for State Pension' on 2037-12-16, but passages [P6], [P8], [P15], [P16], [P17] show Anthony's SPA as 67 (reaching 2037) and Yvette's as 67 (reaching 2033). These are not concurrent events, yet the report presents them as a single foreseeable change in 2037 with combined income £23,946 (= £11,973 × 2). The cashflow evidence shows Yvette accessing State Pension four years earlier. (4) The £100,000 inheritance is corroborated by passages [P9], [P10] but passage [P10] states £40,000 will be used on kitchen/bathroom, leaving £60,000 available for investment. The report does not reconcile this allocation with its recommendation to invest £100,000. (5) The 6 April 2027 IHT rule change is correctly stated and supported by passage [P9]. On the vulnerability overlay: Passage [P24] records 'Client did not have time to complete an ATRQ today, so have sent him an email version to complete himself on Defaqto.' This indicates communication support needs or time pressure that may have affected the completeness of goals and life-change elicitation, but no specific vulnerability driver or characteristic is recorded that would alter the substance of this finding.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report identifies six client objectives with priority ranks 1–6. Priority 1 involves ensuring investments match a Cautious Balanced risk level. Priority 2 involves investing inheritance tax-efficiently via a GIA and considering transferring Cash ISAs. Priority 3 involves reviewing the existing Aviva Platform pension. Priority 4 involves being fully retired by age 60. Priority 5 is unstated in the extracted model. Priority 6 involves not wishing to move the Chartered savings account. The report states one targetAmount of £3,600 gross (basis: Gross), but no targetDate is extracted. The report confirms both clients are in good health with surplus income each month after regular and discretionary expenditure. A cashflow analysis is presented showing projected income sufficient to meet expenditure needs through to age 100, with no shortfall periods indicated by red bars. The report states retirement at age 67 (state pension age) as the formal retirement event, though Priority 4 objective claims full retirement by age 60. The report references planned renovations costing circa £40,000 from the inheritance and confirms sufficient liquid capital for emergencies.
    File:     Evidence documents multiple objectives in varying contexts. The Policy Research document [P18] lists four client objectives: (1) Review existing Aviva Platform pension for suitability; (2) Has £100k incoming, doesn't want to pay income tax on interest, wants to invest for growth and tax-efficiency; (3) Wants to be fully retired by age 60, wants a plan for that; (4) Not an overly risky investor. The Fact Find [P20] notes 'Client did not have time to complete an ATRQ today, so have sent him an email version to complete himself'. The Suitability Report [P3] states: 'You recently retired from a factory role after 35 years of shift work. You did start work part-time driving autistic children to colleges, earning approximately £890 net per month however, you decided that this was not suited to you and have since stopped working altogether.' Income is stated as £22,000 gross per annum from defined benefit pension plus Yvette's £28,000 gross (intending to reduce to 3 days per week next year). Current expenditure is £2,493 per month essential plus £800 discretionary plus £5,000 annually holidays. The report [P3] confirms 'surplus income each month, which you currently save or spend as required.' Cashflow projections [P11] show opening liquid assets of £142,326 (including £40k Chartered, £20k ISA, £20k Wife's Moneybox Cash ISA, £10k emergency fund, £52,326 GIA). The report [P4] states 'Your current regular expenditure is approximately £2,493 per month... leaves you with surplus income each month.' Cashflow events [P14] and [P15] confirm 'Retire' event at age 67 in 2037 (not age 60). The cashflow analysis [P6] states 'your projected income throughout your lifetime is sufficient to meet your expenditure needs... through to age 100. Your current surplus income, combined with your investment growth and pension arrangements, provides a solid foundation.' No explicit statement of Priority objective 5 appears in evidence. The Fact Find [P20] records no accessibility requirement beyond 'No Requirement' for accessible format.
    Discrepancies:
      - Retirement age: Report Priority 4 objective states 'fully retired by age 60'; cashflow events [P14][P15] show formal 'Retire' event at age 67 in 2037 (when Anthony is 67, not 60). This is a 7-year discrepancy between stated goal and planned event.
      - TargetAmount basis and amount: Report asserts one targetAmount of £3,600 gross but no targetDate is extracted; context suggests this is an annual pension contribution figure, not a standalone goal amount with measurable outcome or date.
      - Priority 5 objective: Extracted model shows priorityRank 5 but no corresponding objective text; no evidence document lists a fifth objective.
      - Employment income ambiguity: Extraction notes 'Client's employment status and earnings unclear'; report states client 'have since stopped working altogether' and 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' without specifying what current earnings are (zero or non-zero).
    The critical discrepancy is between Priority Objective 4 ('fully retired by age 60') and the cashflow timeline, which shows a formal 'Retire' event at age 67 in 2037 [P14][P15]. At age 55 (current), Anthony would reach age 60 in 2030 and age 67 in 2037. The stated objective is to be fully retired by age 60; the planning event occurs at age 67. This is a 7-year conflict between goal and plan. The cashflow analysis [P6][P11] proceeds on the assumption that the Retire event occurs at age 67, not age 60, without acknowledging or reconciling this gap. The report does not explain why, if full retirement at age 60 is a priority, the financial plan models retirement at age 67. The report [P3] confirms 'surplus income each month' after £2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + £5,000 annual holidays, and [P6] projects no shortfall through age 100, but this projection assumes retirement at 67, not 60. If retirement at 60 is the true objective, the sufficiency of income and assets to support that earlier retirement date is not demonstrated. The targetAmount of £3,600 appears to be an annual gross pension contribution (referenced in [P2]: 'recommended contribution level' and in the opening paragraph context of pension and ISA planning), but no target date or completion date for this contribution is stated. Priority 5 is extracted but undefined; this appears to be a data capture error rather than an omission in the report itself, as the six objectives listed in [P4] under 'Your Objectives' do not explicitly number themselves. The Policy Research document [P18] lists four objectives (review pension, tax-efficient investment of inheritance, full retirement by 60, cautious risk profile); the Suitability Report [P4] proceeds with these and adds the constraint 'Do not wish to move Chartered savings account' (stated in [P3] as a fact, not explicitly as an objective). The Fact Find [P20] records no accessibility or communication requirement beyond 'No Requirement' for accessible format. No vulnerability drivers, support needs or reasonable adjustments are noted in the evidence that would materially affect the feasibility or timeline of the stated objectives. The core finding is structural: a stated goal (retire by 60) is in temporal conflict with the modelled plan (retire at 67). The plan was not stress-tested against the stated early retirement objective, and the report does not acknowledge or explain this divergence.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states three recommendations linked to objectives as follows: Recommendation 1 (Personal Pension Plan - Aviva Pension Portfolio) is linked to OBJ1, OBJ4, OBJ5. Recommendation 2 (Aviva Investment Portfolio GIA) is linked to OBJ2, OBJ3, OBJ4. Recommendation 3 (Aviva ISA Portfolio) is linked to OBJ2, OBJ3, OBJ4. The report identifies objectives including: (OBJ1) Ensure investments match Cautious Balanced risk level; (OBJ2) Invest inheritance tax-efficiently using GIA; (OBJ3) Transfer Cash ISA to Aviva; (OBJ4) Build pension fund for good standard of living in retirement; (OBJ5) Do not wish to move Chartered savings account. The report states retirement age 67, state pension from age 67, minimum pension access age 55 (rising to 57 from April 2028), and projects pension values at age 60 of £74,000 (low), £83,300 (mid), £93,400 (high) in real terms. Time horizon to age 60 is approximately 5 years. The report confirms client has adequate liquid assets (£190,000 including £100,000 inheritance due this month), surplus monthly income of approximately £1,200 net, and an emergency fund of £10,000.
    File:     The evidence shows: Client (Anthony) aged 55, married to Yvette aged 59. Anthony recently retired from factory role after 35 years; briefly worked part-time driving (£890 net/month) but has stopped; receives defined benefit pension from Aon of £22,000 gross per annum (£1,700 net/month). Yvette works full-time earning £28,000 gross per annum, intending to reduce to 3 days per week. Current expenditure approximately £2,206-£2,493 per month (essential plus discretionary). Liquid assets £190,000: £100,000 inheritance (due this month), £40,000 Chartered savings, £20,000 each Cash ISAs, £10,000 emergency fund. Property valued £400,000 (mortgage-free). Client confirmed surplus income and adequate liquid capital for emergencies. Cashflow projections from multiple scenarios (Current Situation, Market Crash, Tony Predeceases 2040, Yvette Predeceases 2040) show asset paths through to age 104. State pension £11,973 per annum from age 67. Pension minimum age 55. Aviva Pension Portfolio shows uncrystallised fund value £82,692 with 4.5% expected return p.a., 0.63% ongoing charges, plus 1% ongoing financial planning fee. Recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II rated risk level 4 (matching client's agreed profile). Fund projections at age 60: low growth £74,000, mid growth £83,300, high growth £93,400 (real terms). Pension drawdown options described: flexi-access drawdown (preferred by client per report), self-select phased income drawdown, capped drawdown. Death benefits: tax-free to beneficiaries if death before 75; taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate if after 75. IHT change from April 2027 means most unused defined contribution pensions will be included in taxable estate. No evidence of any stated objectives beyond those in the report.
    Discrepancies:
      - The extraction metadata notes ambiguity regarding employment income: report states 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but current employment income appears to be zero (client is fully retired). The basis for this sufficiency claim is not evident in the file.
      - The report projects pension values at age 60, but the stated retirement age is 67, not 60. The five-year cashflow scenarios in the evidence extend to age 67 and beyond, but the pension illustration focuses on age 60 projections only.
      - Objective OBJ5 ('Do not wish to move Chartered savings account at this time') appears in the recommendation linkage but the extraction metadata notes this is stated as a fact in 'Your current situation' section rather than as a primary objective. Its classification as a linked objective is ambiguous.
      - The report's linkage of Recommendation 2 (GIA) and Recommendation 3 (ISA) to OBJ4 ('Build pension fund for good standard of living in retirement') is unclear: a GIA and ISA are not pension vehicles and do not directly build a pension fund, though they may support overall retirement savings.
    The report asserts three recommendations explicitly linked to five objectives. The evidence file contains complete cashflow analysis, pension projections, and client circumstances documentation that supports the recommendation structure. However, several aspects warrant scrutiny: (1) Time horizon alignment: The stated retirement age is 67 (evidenced in cashflow scenarios and the report's own text stating 'Retire age 67 in 2033'), yet pension projections are illustrated at age 60. This is a five-year discrepancy. The critical yield and reduction in yield analyses in the pension switch report use age 60 as the projection point, but this is not the client's retirement target. This introduces a timing misalignment between the stated goal (retire at 67, receive state pension from 67) and the investment horizon used to justify the recommendation. (2) Objective linkage specificity: OBJ1 (risk alignment) is directly supported by evidence: the existing holdings (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% risk 5, 80% risk 7, combined average risk 6) exceed the agreed profile (risk 4); the recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II is rated risk 4. OBJ2 and OBJ3 (tax-efficient use of inheritance via GIA and ISA transfer) are stated in the report and supported by the asset allocation shown in the file. OBJ4 (build pension fund for retirement) is supported by projections, though the GIA and ISA recommendations are investment vehicles rather than pension-specific tools—they support the overall retirement savings goal but do not build the pension fund per se. OBJ5 (retain Chartered savings account) is satisfied by absence of any recommendation to move it. (3) Access needs and term: The report confirms client has surplus income (£1,200 net per month after expenditure), liquid assets of £190,000, and a £10,000 emergency fund. No ad-hoc access requirements beyond normal retirement are disclosed. The pension wrapper allows access from age 55 (or 57 from April 2028), which is before the stated retirement age of 67, providing flexibility. The GIA and ISA are fully liquid. (4) Foreseeable life changes: The report notes Yvette's intention to reduce hours to 3 days per week 'from next year', which is reflected in the cashflow assumption (£28,000 pro-rata for 3 days). Multiple life scenarios are modelled (Market Crash, spouse predeceases, Yvette predeceases) showing robustness. (5) The critical issue is the age 60 projection target: the report uses age 60 throughout the pension switching analysis and illustrations, but this predates the stated retirement age of 67 by 7 years. This creates a mismatch between the time horizon used to assess suitability (5 years to age 60) and the actual retirement planning horizon (12 years to age 67). The pension switch report shows 'Effect of switching' as £0 or 0.0% decrease, implying the new solution performs identically to the existing one at age 60—but age 60 is not a stated withdrawal date. The analysis does not address why age 60 is used as the critical comparison point when the client will not access the pension until age 55 at earliest (or preferably not until 67 when state pension begins). This is a weakness in justification: the recommendation is appropriate for the stated goal (retire at 67 with good standard of living) on the evidence of cashflow, but the suitability comparison pivots on an intermediate age not aligned to the stated goal.

  [G2.8] Target retirement income stated in the report is consistent with the evidence and with the figures the plan is modelled on
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Target retirement income of £2,200 net per month, monthly frequency. Current expenditure is stated as approximately £2,206 per month. Retirement age for projections is 60. Assumptions include general expenditure inflation of 2%, savings inflation of 1.25%, state pension inflation of 2.5%, property value growth of 4.7%, lending growth of 4%, Aviva Platform Investments growth of 4.5%, mid-growth rate of 2.94%, and that 0% of any in-year surplus will be saved within the plan. Total net monthly income is derived as £4,900. Yvette's retirement timing assumed from 2027 onwards (when client reaches age 60). Current expenditure is £2,206 per month with no expected changes. Earnings are stated as sufficient to support recommended contribution level.
    File:     Fact Find (P1, P5) records target retirement income as £2,200 net per month, monthly frequency, target age 60. Fact Find (P4) records calculated total monthly household expenditure as £2,206.67, total net monthly income as £2,590.00, and total monthly disposable income as £383.33. Fact Find (P15) shows employment income of £890 net monthly and DB pension income of £1,700 net monthly (£2,999.67 total from Anthony), with no statement of Yvette's income in the income table shown. Notes (P2, P3) indicate client works part-time, asks 'When will he stop? Defo 60?' and records 'quit before he could' enrol in workplace scheme. Cashflow projections (P19-P23) show modelled assets and income streams over time. Pension projections (P7, P8) assume growth of 2.7% annually based on 4.8% example growth rate less 2% inflation. Pension switch report (P9, P13, P14) uses mid-growth rate of 2.94%, retirement age 60, and shows existing pension projected value £86,800 and new solution £86,700 at mid-growth. Report states assumptions on inflation (2%, 2.5%, 4.7%, 4%, 4.5%) in needs analysis section (P17 reference in canonical model).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report derives total net monthly income as £4,900 but Fact Find (P4) records total net monthly income as £2,590.00 — a difference of £2,310.
      - Report states earnings are sufficient to support recommended contribution level (canonical model ambiguity notes) but Fact Find records employment income of only £890 net monthly from part-time work, with no current employment income stated for Yvette in the visible income table (P15), and notes (P2, P3) express uncertainty about whether client is still working and when he will stop.
      - Report assumes Yvette's retirement timing from 2027 onwards but Fact Find (P15) shows Yvette earns £28,000 gross per annum pro-rata 3 days per week, and notes (P3) ask 'When will he stop? Defo 60?' suggesting employment status and continuation are uncertain.
      - Report assumes savings inflation of 1.25% but figure matching shows nearest corroboration is 1.5% in passages — not matched exactly.
      - Report assumes property value growth of 4.7% but nearest corroboration in passages is 4.72% (P20) — not matched exactly but within 0.4%.
      - Current expenditure stated in report as approximately £2,206 per month matches Fact Find (P4) figure of £2,206.67 but the report states this as current expenditure with no expected changes in foreseeable future, while Fact Find (P4) shows this is calculated total household expenditure at a point in time, not explicitly tied to the target retirement income goal.
      - Report states page reference 13 for 'Your current expenditure is approximately £2,206 per month' but Fact Find (P4) shows this is recorded in expenditure details table, not necessarily on page 13.
    The requirement tests consistency between the target retirement income stated (£2,200 net monthly) and evidence supporting it. The £2,200 figure is directly corroborated in Fact Find (P1, P5) as a minimum income requirement in retirement. Current expenditure of £2,206.67 (P4) approximately matches the stated £2,206. However, a material discrepancy arises in total net monthly income: the report derives £4,900 but the Fact Find shows £2,590. The canonical model notes an ambiguity here, stating 'current earnings are sufficient to support recommended contribution level' without specifying what those earnings are, while noting the client is retired from factory work after 35 years and briefly worked part-time driving (£890 net/month) which has since stopped. The Fact Find shows current net income of £2,590 (£890 employment + £1,700 DB pension), not £4,900. The £2,200 target income appears aligned with stated current expenditure of ~£2,206, but without clarity on current earnings or Yvette's income contribution to household totals, the derivation of £4,900 total household income cannot be validated against the evidence. The notes (P2, P3) express material uncertainty about employment continuation ('When will he stop? Defo 60?'), which contradicts any assumption of stable current earnings. Inflation assumptions are stated in the report (2%, 1.25%, 2.5%, 4.7%, 4%, 4.5%) with the 1.25% and 4.7% figures not exactly matched in passages (nearest are 1.5% and 4.72%), though these are stated as assumptions in the needs analysis section. The retirement age of 60 is clearly evidenced (P1). The guard on expressed targets in today's terms versus modelled escalation does not apply — the £2,200 is stated as current/today's money target. The guard on defined benefit pension inclusion is relevant: the report does not explicitly state whether £2,200 includes or excludes the £1,700 DB pension already being received. If the target is independent of DB pension, it represents additional need; if inclusive, it is already being exceeded by DB alone plus employment. This basis is not stated and is a material omission. The comparison can be performed on the stated target (£2,200) versus evidence of stated target (corroborated in P1, P5), but the derivation of total household income (£4,900) and employment earning assumptions cannot be reconciled with the Fact Find evidence.

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G3.2, G3.3, G3.4, G3.5, G3.6, G3.7, G3.8, G3.9, G3.10). Report asserts CFL rating of 'High' in canonical model but states 'Medium-High' in narrative section [P11], creating an internal label mismatch. Report asserts CFL rating of High/Medium-High but agrees risk level of only 4 (Cautious Balanced); the two are not aligned on the same scale or basis. Report grounds CFL in guaranteed income and significant liquid funds, but the term 'High' is not quantified or defined against any reference scale, unlike the ATR which is explicitly mapped to risk level 4 on a defined 1–7 scale [P13]. 8 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts a capacity for loss rating of 'High' (recorded as 'Medium-High' in the narrative section [P11]). The assessment basis rests on three grounds: (1) 'Good level of guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions' with only 'a small period' shortfall when Yvette retires until her State Pension begins [P11]; (2) 'Significant funds to cover this, providing greater resilience to investment volatility' [P11]; (3) Client 'confirmed sufficient liquid capital, either immediately or on short notice, to meet any unexpected emergencies' [P11].
    File:     The Risk Profile Report [P13] records the natural risk level as 4 (Cautious Balanced) based on the ATR questionnaire completed 09/08/2025. The Capacity for Loss Questionnaire section [P1] states 'No additional notes were recorded'. Cashflow modelling [P14] shows the client has liquid assets and accessible pensions of £225,018 in 2025, with income of £38,800 p/a initially, falling to £22,000 p/a from 2030 (when Tony's employment income ceases), then rising to £34,451 in 2033 when Yvette's State Pension begins. The cashflow projections show deficits from 2030–2032 (£7,101, £7,052, £7,002 respectively, drawing from liquid assets), restoring to surplus from 2033 onwards. Under the Market Crash scenario [P15], liquid assets remain above £168,000 even in the crash stress test by 2058. The client's ATR questionnaire responses [P2] show 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares and 'Very little understanding / knowledge' of pension planning and income in retirement, though the client has invested in stocks and shares by themselves and in pensions through a financial adviser. The suitability report narrative [P11] states the agreed risk level is 4 (Cautious Balanced), not elevated to match the CFL rating of High.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts CFL rating of 'High' in canonical model but states 'Medium-High' in narrative section [P11], creating an internal label mismatch
      - Report asserts CFL rating of High/Medium-High but agrees risk level of only 4 (Cautious Balanced); the two are not aligned on the same scale or basis
      - Report grounds CFL in guaranteed income and significant liquid funds, but the term 'High' is not quantified or defined against any reference scale, unlike the ATR which is explicitly mapped to risk level 4 on a defined 1–7 scale [P13]
      - Cashflow shows a material shortfall period from 2030–2032 (years when Tony is age 60–62, before his State Pension at 67), contradicting the claim of only 'a small period' shortfall
      - The assessment basis provided ('good' guaranteed income, 'significant' funds, 'sufficient' liquid capital) restates asset position rather than measuring capacity to absorb loss; no quantified loss tolerance (e.g. maximum acceptable draw-down, loss threshold in £ or %) is stated
      - Report does not reference any CFL questionnaire findings, despite P1 record of Capacity for Loss Questionnaire responses being available; the assessment appears unsupported by documented questionnaire outcome
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Capacity for loss scale definition — the report uses the label 'High' but never defines what High, Medium-High, or other CFL ratings mean on any numerical or descriptive scale, CFL questionnaire responses or scoring — P1 states 'No additional notes were recorded' but the underlying questionnaire data is not provided; the basis for the CFL rating cannot be traced to any questionnaire outcome, Quantified loss tolerance — no maximum acceptable percentage or absolute loss threshold is stated (e.g. 'client can tolerate loss of £X' or 'loss of up to Y% of portfolio'), Basis for reconciliation between ATR rating (4, Cautious Balanced) and CFL rating (High) — the report does not explain why a cautious attitude to risk is paired with a high capacity for loss, or whether these are independent assessments, Explicit scale mapping for CFL — unlike ATR which is mapped to risk levels 1–7, CFL uses a different label set (High/Medium-High) with no stated mapping to portfolio risk level or investment term
    The report asserts a capacity for loss rating but provides no evidence it was derived from a structured assessment. The Capacity for Loss Questionnaire record [P1] shows no findings recorded. The assessment text in [P11] restates the client's financial position (good pension income, significant liquid funds, sufficient emergency capital) rather than deriving a loss tolerance from it. This is a weak assessment: income and asset level are inputs to capacity for loss, not proof of capacity itself. The report does not quantify what loss the client can actually absorb — neither as a percentage of portfolio value, nor as an absolute figure, nor as a time-to-recovery measure. The label 'High' appears without definition or scale. Critically, the canonical model records CFL as 'High', but the suitability report narrative [P11] states 'Medium-High', creating an internal inconsistency in how the rating is reported. The guard 'Capacity for loss restated as a consequence of attitude to risk' applies here: the report notes that 'Having agreed your attitude to investment risk, we discussed how this could impact on potential losses to your investment, and you confirmed this is within an acceptable tolerance based on your capacity to suffer any losses' [P11]. This phrasing suggests CFL is being derived from or aligned with ATR rather than assessed independently. The field /riskAssessment/perClient[]/capacityForLoss/isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr is set to true in the canonical model, but the narrative text does not support independent assessment — it shows the CFL discussion following from ATR agreement. The cashflow evidence [P14] does show a modest shortfall period (3 years: 2030–2032), not the lengthy deficit that would challenge capacity. However, this supporting evidence is not referenced in the report's CFL rationale, indicating the cashflow modelling was performed but not used to justify the CFL rating. The ATR questionnaire [P2] shows very limited investment knowledge (no understanding of stocks/shares, very little of pensions), yet CFL is rated High — these are not obviously reconciled. No override or uplift from ATR to CFL is explained. The comparison cannot be completed: the CFL rating is asserted but neither the questionnaire outcome nor a quantified loss tolerance is provided in the evidence, so the assertion cannot be corroborated or contradicted.
