  [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts in the Capacity for Loss section [P10]: 'you confirmed this is within an acceptable tolerance based on your capacity to suffer any losses.' The report states capacity for loss is 'Medium-High' based on: (1) good level of guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions; (2) only a shortfall for a small period when Yvette retires until her State Pension begins; (3) significant funds to cover this, providing greater resilience to investment volatility. The agreed risk level is 4 (Cautious Balanced) on a scale of 1-10. The report describes this as: 'you tend to see yourself as quite a cautious person and are inclined to view risk negatively rather than as a source of opportunity' and 'are inclined to look for safer investments, although you may consider taking some risk in exchange for increased potential returns.' [P20]
    File:     Evidence supports multiple elements: (1) Capacity for Loss Questionnaire responses [P17] show the client answered questions on capacity for loss; no additional notes recorded [P22]. (2) Risk profile documentation [P12, P21] confirms agreed risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced) based on natural ATR questionnaire score completed 09/08/2025, with projections discussed and agreed. (3) Cashflow analysis [P13, P16] models retirement income including DB pension (£22,000), Yvette's employment (£16,800), showing liquid assets over time. (4) Notes [P23, C24] record 'Only ATR 4. Expected growth is 4.5%, they already get this on moneybox ISAs.' (5) Research reports [P2, P3, P6, P7] confirm risk level 4 Cautious Balanced mapping to multi-asset portfolio with moderate capital appreciation target. However, no document explicitly quantifies 'tolerance for loss' as a separate numerical or categorical measure distinct from capacity for loss or attitude to risk. The term 'Medium-High' capacity for loss appears only in the suitability report [P10]; no supporting document uses this label.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states capacity for loss is 'Medium-High' but no evidence document uses, corroborates or justifies this specific label. The Capacity for Loss Questionnaire [P17] records responses but the summary records 'No additional notes were recorded' [P22], providing no narrative justification for a 'Medium-High' rating.
      - The report's statement 'you confirmed this is within an acceptable tolerance based on your capacity to suffer any losses' [P10] is not corroborated by any passage; the questionnaire responses are recorded but not cited to confirm client confirmation.
      - No document quantifies tolerance for loss numerically or categorically (e.g., 'can tolerate X% loss' or 'tolerance rating Y'). Capacity for loss is asserted as 'Medium-High' without definition of the scale or threshold.
      - The report derives capacity for loss reasoning from cashflow and income adequacy ('good level of guaranteed income', 'significant funds to cover shortfall') but the Capacity for Loss Questionnaire section [P22] shows 'No additional notes were recorded' regarding capacity-specific discussion or rationale.
    The report asserts that tolerance for loss is 'within acceptable tolerance' and quantifies capacity for loss as 'Medium-High', but evidence is incomplete and partially circular. The Capacity for Loss Questionnaire was completed [P17] but the follow-up discussion section records 'No additional notes were recorded' [P22], meaning no documented discussion of the questionnaire results or capacity assessment. The report's rationale for 'Medium-High' capacity (guaranteed income, residual shortfall period, significant available funds) is logically consistent with the cashflow modelling [P13, P16] but is not explicitly linked to any questionnaire response or formal assessment criterion. The agreed risk level 4 is well-evidenced across multiple research reports [P2, P6, P7, P12, P21] and matched to the Cautious Balanced profile; this is internally consistent. However, the requirement asks whether tolerance for loss is 'consistent with and supported by evidence.' The guard notes that many firms do not quantify tolerance for loss separately, and absence is a documentation gap, not a contradiction. Here, the report does assert a 'Medium-High' capacity rating and uses it to support the conclusion that losses are within acceptable tolerance, but provides no documentary source for the rating and no evidence that 'tolerance for loss' was discussed or measured separately from attitude to risk and capacity. The distinction between 'attitude to risk' (questionnaire-derived, risk level 4) and 'capacity for loss' (financial analysis-derived, Medium-High) is conceptually sound, but the Medium-High rating lacks evidentiary support. No document defines the scale (what constitutes Medium-High vs High vs Low), so comparison cannot establish whether Medium-High aligns with a risk level 4 or whether it matches the client's confirmed acceptance.

  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an investment term of 5 years in the canonical model field /riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears. The report uses this figure as a basis for discussing risk level and projections with the client. The report describes the process: 'In the discussion we had, you were shown a projection, based initially on your natural risk level, investment amount, target and desired time horizon. We then discussed this projection and altered any factor, including the risk level, until we agreed that the right balance had been achieved.' [P1] However, no explicit statement of the 5-year term appears in the risk profile narrative itself; the term is embedded in the model only.
    File:     The evidence shows a substantially longer time horizon than 5 years. Cashflow projections extend to age 103/107 (approximately 48 years from the current point in 2025, when the client is aged 55/59). [P4] and [P6] model financial positions through 2074, reflecting a multi-decade planning horizon. The GIA research report and Personal Pension Plan documents all reference the discussion process and agreed risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced), but do not quantify the specific time horizon used to justify this level. The only explicit time horizon stated in supporting documents is in the Aviva Key Features: 'you should view such investments as medium to long term (typically more than five years)' [P13]—which describes a minimum or general threshold, not the specific horizon used for this client's risk assessment. The cashflow analysis and accumulated asset projections suggest the adviser contemplated a multi-decade horizon for the overall financial plan, not a 5-year investment term.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts a 5-year investment term in the canonical model, but the cashflow evidence models outcomes to age 103/107 (48+ years), suggesting a much longer planning horizon was the true basis for discussions.
      - No explicit statement appears in the report's own narrative linking the 5-year figure to the agreed risk level of 4, despite the canonical model extracting it as the investmentTermYears.
      - The evidence describes a generic 'medium to long term (typically more than five years)' threshold in product documentation [P13], which does not reconcile clearly with a specific 5-year term.
    The comparison can be performed because both sides provide time horizon information, but they are on different bases and the report does not reconcile them. The canonical model records 5 years as the investmentTermYears; this appears to be a single-goal or recommendation-specific horizon extracted from the report's structure. However, the evidence shows that the client's overall financial plan extends 48+ years (to age 103/107), with cashflow modelling and asset accumulation projections running through 2074. The guard states: 'Money left invested through retirement can legitimately have a longer horizon than the retirement date itself—but the report must say so.' The report does not explicitly explain why a 5-year term was selected for the risk assessment while the cashflow analysis spans 48 years, nor does it state that funds are to remain invested beyond the 5-year point. The 5-year figure appears in the model but not in the risk narrative. The cashflow evidence and multi-decade projections [P4][P6] indicate the adviser contemplated a much longer investment horizon in structuring the overall plan, yet this is not reconciled with or explained in relation to the stated 5-year term. The Aviva Key Features describe investments as 'medium to long term (typically more than five years)' [P13], which acknowledges a horizon longer than 5 years is contemplated but does not reconcile it with the specific 5-year figure asserted in the model. This creates ambiguity: either the 5-year term is incomplete or mismatches the actual planning basis, or the report has failed to explain how the 5-year term relates to a 48-year cashflow horizon. The agreed risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) is consistently documented across all three recommendation documents [P1][P2][P8][P9][P10][P12], and the risk profile narrative in each [P1][P8][P9][P10] describes the process of agreeing risk 'considering your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return for your goal' after showing projections 'based initially on your natural risk level, investment amount, target and desired time horizon.' The time horizon input to that discussion is stated in the model as 5 years, but the evidence on file does not record or justify why 5 years was selected when the financial plan extends 48 years. Without explicit statement that funds are held for the full 48 years or explanation of the mismatch, the 5-year figure cannot be verified as the true basis for the risk assessment against the objective horizon.

  [G3.5] Investment strategy and asset allocation
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report asserts a target risk rating of 4 and recommends two funds at 100% allocation each: (1) Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II at 100% allocation with risk rating 4 and ongoing charge figure 0.41%; (2) CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc at 100% allocation with risk rating 4 and ongoing charge figure 0.29%. The investment strategy type is stated as MultiAssetFund. The report does not extract an explicit assetAllocation breakdown showing equity/bond/cash percentages for the recommended portfolio as a whole.
    File:     The evidence file contains multiple supporting documents: (P10, P11) confirm the client's agreed risk level is 4 and describe a Cautious Balanced profile. (P4) states the recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II 'provides more consistent performance aligned with your Risk Level 4 profile' and has 'lower volatility than your current mix of Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% equity funds, which have a combined risk level of 6'. (P8) lists Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II (GBP) at 100% allocation with risk rating 4 and weighted AMC 0.41%. (P6, P9, P24) confirm CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc carries risk rating 4. (P1, P17) provide detailed asset allocation breakdowns for the Aviva fund showing 41.7% Equities, 33.8% Bonds, 5.4% Alternative Assets, 1.3% Cash, 2.0% Gold, and 9.1% Other as at 31 October 2025. (P5, P9, P14) all state 'The client's agreed risk level is 4. The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' (P24) shows a data comparison of the two recommended funds both rated risk 4 on the Defaqto scale.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report extracts two funds each listed at 100% allocation (Aviva at 100% and CT Universal at 100%), which if both recommended would total 200%. The evidence does not clarify whether both funds are equally recommended or whether the 100% allocations refer to separate tranches (e.g., one for pension, one for ISA/GIA). Multiple research reports (P8 for Pension, P9 for GIA, P14 for Pension) appear to focus on different segments but the canonical model does not distinguish between them.
      - The report states Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II ongoing charge figure as 0.41%. The figure 0.41% is corroborated by passages P24 (AMC 0.41%) but P7 and P13 in research reports show the same fund with ongoing charges of 0.41% in the pension switching context. However, the CT fund is listed in the report as 0.29% and this is corroborated by P13 (Ongoing Charges 0.29%). No discrepancy on these individual figures.
      - The report does not extract an explicit overall asset allocation for the recommended strategy (e.g., X% equities, Y% bonds), though the underlying funds carry implicit allocations detailed in the evidence. The evidence shows the Aviva fund's detailed breakdown but the report's canonical model path /solution/investmentStrategy/assetAllocation[] is absent, making comparison of recommended asset allocation against ATR/CFL incomplete at the portfolio level.
    The comparison can be performed on the elements present. The agreed risk rating of 4 is consistently supported across multiple passages (P10, P11, P5, P9, P14). Both recommended funds carry risk rating 4 according to the evidence (Aviva in P8, P24; CT Universal in P6, P9, P24), matching the client's agreed risk level on the Defaqto risk scale. The Aviva fund's detailed asset allocation (P1, P17) shows a diversified multi-asset structure (41.7% equities, 33.8% bonds, alternatives, cash, gold) consistent with a risk level 4, Cautious Balanced profile. The report's statement that the recommended Aviva fund 'provides more consistent performance aligned with your Risk Level 4 profile' is supported by P4, which compares it favourably against the existing Vanguard holdings (combined risk 6, outside the agreed profile). The ongoing charge figures (0.41% for Aviva, 0.29% for CT) are accurately stated and corroborated. However, a material ambiguity exists: the canonical model lists two separate funds each at 100% allocation, which would imply 200% total if both are equally weighted recommendations. The evidence suggests these may refer to different wrappers (pension, ISA, GIA) based on the research reports' structure (P8 Pension Plan, P9 GIA), but this distinction is not explicit in the suitability report's extracted model. The investment strategy type 'MultiAssetFund' is appropriate for both recommended funds. The report does not extract an overall portfolio-level asset allocation breakdown, though this is less critical since both funds are independently rated at level 4 and the evidence confirms the Aviva fund's implicit allocations support the Cautious Balanced profile. No override of the agreed risk rating is apparent, and no upward override from a questionnaire result is evidenced. The strategy risk (both funds at level 4) does not exceed the agreed capacity for loss (level 4). The comparison between strategy and agreed profile is supported, though the presentation of two 100% allocations creates a presentation ambiguity that does not itself contradict the underlying suitability—it reflects a multi-wrapper recommendation structure not clearly delineated in the extraction.

  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an agreed risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) on a 1–10 scale. The recommended investment strategy targets risk rating 4. The client's capacity for loss is rated as 'High' or 'Medium-High' depending on which passage is consulted. The recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II is mapped to risk level 4, stated to align with the agreed profile.
    File:     The case file evidences agreed risk level 4 repeatedly across multiple research and review reports (P1, P3, P4, P12, P15 all confirm '4 Cautious Balanced'). The Attitude to Risk Questionnaire completed 09/08/2025 (P24) shows the client's natural risk profile. The existing pension holdings (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% at risk 5, Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% at risk 7) are stated in P10 to have a weighted average volatility that 'maps to a risk level of 6 which is outside the client's agreed risk level'. P2 confirms this: '10 year future volatility for the existing holdings based on the current asset allocation is 12.34. This volatility maps to a risk level of 6 which is outside the client's agreed risk level.' P3 states 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' Capacity for loss is asserted in P11 (from the suitability report itself, category [I]) as 'Medium-High', with reasoning: 'You have a good level of guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions' and 'significant funds to cover this, providing you with greater resilience to investment volatility.' P1 and P24 (category [D]) show the risk questionnaire and capacity discussion but record 'No additional notes were recorded' in capacity for loss discussion.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts capacity for loss is 'High' (canonical model path /riskAssessment/perClient[]/capacityForLoss/rating), but P11 states it as 'Medium-High'. These are different labels on what appears to be the same assessment.
      - Report states agreed risk level 4 consistently; file states agreed risk level 4 consistently. No numeric discrepancy on the agreed profile itself.
      - Existing holdings risk level: report states 6 (P23, extracted as /existingArrangements[]/riskRating); file confirms 6 in P10 and P14. No discrepancy on existing rating.
      - Recommended fund risk level: report asserts strategy targets 4; file confirms in P3 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4'. No numeric discrepancy on recommended rating.
    The comparison can be performed. All figures are present and numeric. On the primary requirement—whether the recommended strategy exceeds the agreed risk profile—the evidence is consistent: agreed risk is 4, recommended strategy is mapped to 4, and no exceedance occurs. The suitability report itself (P11) provides the reasoning for capacity for loss: guaranteed income plus liquid assets. The file does not contradict this, nor does it support an alternative rating; P24 records no additional notes on capacity discussion. The recommended strategy (risk 4) does not exceed the agreed risk level (4) and therefore complies with the requirement on its face. The alignment of the new recommended fund (Multi-Asset Plus II, risk 4) with the agreed profile (4) is evidenced in P3. The strategy is not a violation of either the agreed risk rating or stated capacity for loss. The labelling inconsistency between 'High' and 'Medium-High' for capacity is an internal report discrepancy but does not affect whether the strategy exceeds the profile; it relates to how much loss the client can absorb, and the strategy remains at the agreed level regardless.

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) Agreed risk profile is 4 on a 1-10 scale, label 'Cautious Balanced', assessed on 2025-08-09 via risk profiling questionnaire.   Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) Client's agreed risk profile is 4 on a 1-10 scale, labeled 'Cautious Balanced'. (2) Capacity for loss is rated 'Medium-High', supported by good guaranteed income from pensions, significant liquid funds (£190,000), and confirmed liquid capital for emergencies. (3) Investment term is 5 years. (4) Recommended strategy is a Multi-Asset Fund with 100% allocation to two funds: Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II (risk rating 4, 0.41% OCF) and CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc (risk rating 4, 0.29% OCF). (5) Both recommended funds are stated as risk rating 4, matching the agreed profile. (6) The funds reflect the client's risk profile and are managed to remain within risk tolerance. (7) The strategy provides diversification, blend of active/passive, competitive charges, and strong peer performance. (8) Projections at age 60 (5-year term): Low growth £74,000, Mid growth £83,300, High growth £93,400 in real terms.
    File:     The evidence corroborates most elements: (1) Risk profile questionnaire completed 9 August 2025, agreed as 4 on 1-10 scale, 'Cautious Balanced' [P17]. (2) Capacity for loss assessed as 'Medium-High' [extracted], supported by pension income guarantees, significant liquid funds, and emergency liquidity confirmed [P18 suitability report]. (3) Both recommended funds carry SRRI/risk rating 4: Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II risk rating 4 [P4, P5, P14]; CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc risk rating 4 [P1, P15]. (4) Investment term is 5 years, consistent with pension contribution horizon and age 60 projection target [P14, P17]. (5) Existing holdings show Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity (risk 5) and 80% Equity (risk 7), combined weighted average risk approximately 6, which exceeds agreed profile of 4 [P9, P18]. (6) Research reports confirm agreed risk level 4 and that recommended portfolio risk mapping is 4, matching agreed level [P4, P7]. (7) Fund asset allocations are detailed: Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II contains 33.43% Global Fixed Income, 28.95% North America Equity, 6.99% Europe Equity, 4.58% UK Corporate, 3.58% UK Government Bonds, with cash and other holdings totaling 100% [P14]. CT Universal MAP Cautious shown as risk 4 in KIID with medium volatility historically [P1]. (8) Performance data shows both recommended funds have outperformed benchmarks and maintained risk alignment [P3, P4, P18].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states recommended funds are risk rating 4; evidence confirms both are SRRI/risk rating 4 — no discrepancy in fund risk ratings themselves. However, report's assertion that these funds 'align with' and 'reflect' the client's profile requires checking against whether the portfolio as constructed meets the profile. Existing holdings (combined Vanguard 60%/80%) show risk profile of approximately 6, which is higher than agreed profile of 4. The recommendation to switch is justified by this mismatch, not a discrepancy in the final recommendation. Report states recommended funds have OCF of 0.41% and 0.29% respectively; evidence from research reports and fund factsheets confirms these figures [P8, P14]. No discrepancy on charges stated. Report projects values at age 60 in real terms under three growth scenarios; no independent projection evidence in file to contradict these figures. Projections are internally consistent with stated term of 5 years and current age implied (approximately 55). Report asserts capacity for loss is 'Medium-High' with rationale of pension guarantees and significant liquid funds; evidence in P18 and extracted data support this. No contradiction on capacity for loss rating itself. Report does not explicitly quantify 'tolerance for loss' as a separate figure (e.g., maximum acceptable drawdown percentage); this is absence rather than discrepancy.
    The report asserts that recommended funds align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss, and investment term. Evidence supports each element independently: (1) Attitude to risk: Client's agreed profile is 4 (Cautious Balanced) from questionnaire on 9 August 2025 [P17]. Both recommended funds carry risk rating 4 on the SRRI scale, as confirmed by Defaqto research [P4: 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level']. This is internally consistent. (2) Capacity for loss: Assessed as Medium-High with clear rationale: guaranteed pension income, liquid assets of £190,000, and confirmed emergency liquidity [extracted, P18]. No evidence contradicts this assessment. The capacity rating supports a risk 4 allocation for a 5-year term. (3) Investment term: 5-year horizon is stated consistently across documents [P14, P17]. CT Universal MAP Cautious KIID states 'this fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years' [P1]. This is not a contradiction but a boundary condition: the fund is acceptable at the 5-year point but implies heightened volatility risk if withdrawal occurs before 5 years. The report does not address this boundary explicitly. (4) Portfolio construction: Report recommends 100% allocation split between two funds of the same risk rating (4). No evidence suggests this violates the profile; both funds are risk 4 and managed to maintain this rating. Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II asset allocation shows 33.43% Global Fixed Income, 28.95% North America Equity, with smaller holdings across other regions and asset classes, consistent with a cautious-balanced approach [P14]. (5) Existing holdings context: Report contrasts current holdings (Vanguard 60%/80%, combined risk ~6) against recommended funds (both risk 4), and demonstrates why the switch improves alignment [P18]. The switch rationale is supported. The comparison performed in P18 shows 'the recommended Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II provides more consistent performance aligned with your Risk Level 4 profile. The fund demonstrates lower volatility than your current mix of Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% equity funds, which have a combined risk level of 6.' This is accurate and well-reasoned. (6) Missing quantification of tolerance for loss: Report asserts capacity for loss qualitatively but does not model a specific large-loss scenario (e.g., maximum acceptable loss in pounds or percentage over 5 years). This is absence of detail, not contradiction. COBS 9.2.2R requires assessment of capacity for loss but does not mandate scenario modelling in the narrative; it is implicit in the risk rating system [P6, P7]. (7) Fund suitability at boundary: The 5-year investment term aligns exactly with the CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund recommendation horizon [P1]. This is appropriate, though the KIID's wording suggests caution at shorter horizons. The report does not note this as a consideration but does not violate it. (8) Consistency check: Risk profile (4), capacity for loss (Medium-High), term (5 years), and recommended funds (both risk 4, diversified, low-cost, matched to CIP framework) are mutually aligned. No evidence shows contradiction. Performance data [P3, P4, P18] confirms both recommended funds have performed competitively while maintaining appropriate volatility for risk level 4. Conclusion: All three dimensions (ATR, CFL, term) are evidenced, matched to the portfolio, and internally consistent. No material contradiction exists.

    Report:   The report asserts: (1) Attitude to Risk: agreed risk rating of 4 on a 1-10 scale ('Cautious Balanced'), assessed via risk profiling questionnaire on 9 August 2025, with no override applied (reconciliation shows 'Unchanged', fromCode 4 to toCode 4). (6) Strategy Risk Alignment: funds stated as 'reflect your risk profile and are managed to remain within this risk tolerance', with rationale citing risk profile alignment, diversification, cost efficiency, simplicity, competitive charges, peer performance, and CIP monitoring.
    File:     The file evidences: (1) Attitude to Risk: Risk profile questionnaire dated 9 August 2025 confirms natural risk level 4 'Cautious Balanced' [P1, P17]. Agreed risk level also 4 [P1, P2, P4, P6]. Questionnaire responses show client answered 'No understanding/knowledge' to stocks and shares understanding, 'Very little understanding' to pension planning, and selected 'Balanced' as investment approach; on £20k fall tolerance, selected 'More than £2,500' loss [P16, P19]. (2) Capacity for Loss: Risk profile report records 'No additional notes were recorded' in the capacity for loss section [P8, P19], despite a Capacity for Loss questionnaire being completed. (3) Tolerance for Loss: No quantified tolerance or loss scenario modelling on file. (4) Investment Term: Risk profile projection shows 5-year investment period [P17].     Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts capacity for loss as 'Medium-High' but file shows capacity for loss questionnaire with 'No additional notes recorded', no supporting capacity assessment, and no evidence justifying the upgrade from 'Medium-High' to 'High'.
      - Report states funds 'are managed to remain within this risk tolerance' but research reports and file provide no evidence of active management protocols to maintain risk 4 during market volatility.

    Attitude to Risk is fully supported: questionnaire completed 9 August 2025, natural risk level 4 agreed with no override, scale identified (1-10), methodology documented. No contradiction. Capacity for Loss presents a material gap: the report asserts 'Medium-High' capacity supported by three specific client circumstances (pension income, available funds, liquid capital confirmation). The risk profile report confirms a Capacity for Loss questionnaire was completed but records 'No additional notes were recorded' [P8, P19], providing no independent assessment or questionnaire-derived rating. The three bases the suitability report cites are present in the suitability report itself [from canonical model provenance] but the file contains no standalone capacity for loss assessment document or questionnaire results. This is internal consistency within the report, not corroboration by external evidence. The file is silent on capacity for loss methodology and quantification. Tolerance for Loss is not quantified on file; the report states it is 'within acceptable tolerance' but no loss scenario, percentage decline tolerance, or stress test is documented. Investment Term is evidenced: 5-year projection period confirmed in risk profile [P17]. The research reports recommend Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II [P6] in the pension switching report and CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc [P3] in the GIA research report. The file does not clarify which is the primary recommendation or how they are allocated. Both are confirmed as risk level 4. However, the Personal Pension Plan review report [P4, P5] identifies a critical mismatch: the existing holdings (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% at risk 5 and 80% at risk 7) have a combined volatility of 12.34, mapping to risk level 6, which is 'outside the client's agreed risk level' of 4. The suitability report does not disclose this existing-to-agreed mismatch or explain whether the recommended switch corrects it. The research reports confirm the new portfolio (recommended Aviva fund) maps to risk 4 [P12], but the suitability report does not reference this correction or justify why an existing risk-6 portfolio required switching to risk 4. The report's statement that funds 'are managed to remain within this risk tolerance' lacks support: no investment mandate, fund documentation, or risk management protocol in the file evidences active management to maintain the risk level. Standard risk warnings in fund factsheets are boilerplate [P24] and do not constitute management evidence. No vulnerability indicators from the evidence materially affect this assessment; the client's limited investment knowledge (noted in questionnaire responses [P16]) supports the Cautious Balanced classification but does not alter the gaps identified above.

      [G3.11] No Issue
        - The report describes the existing arrangement as having a risk level of 6 and states it will be switched to a new fund designed for risk level 4, indicating acknowledgment of misalignment in the current state. The arrangement is not being retained as-is; it is being switched. The adviceAction states 'RetainAndSwitchFunds,' meaning the arrangement (platform/wrapper) is retained but the underlying funds are replaced.

        - The exact risk rating of the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II is not explicitly stated in the evidence provided, though the report claims it is designed for risk level 4 investors and [P10] confirms 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level.'

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). The report characterizes the client's knowledge as 'limited or moderate' while the risk profile questionnaire records 'Very little understanding / knowledge' as the client's self-assessment of overall investing experience (P12). 'Very little' is materially lower than 'limited or moderate'. The report does not cite or reflect the direct questionnaire responses showing 'No understanding / knowledge' for stocks and shares (P1) and Unit Trusts (P12), nor the 'Very little understanding / knowledge' for pension planning (P1), instead presenting a generalized characterization. Report asserts 'fair degree of understanding' of stocks and shares; Risk Profile Report questionnaire response records 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares. 4 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts the client's stated knowledge level is 'Limited'. The report states in the suitability narrative (P22): 'You have limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand, but you are suited to a Cautious Balanced approach to investing.'
    File:     The risk profile questionnaire (P1, P12) records the client's responses to direct knowledge questions completed on 09/08/2025. On the investor experience question 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself?' the response is 'Very little understanding / knowledge' (P12). Specific investment knowledge responses include: 'No understanding / knowledge' for stocks and shares understanding (P1); 'Very little understanding / knowledge' for pension planning (P1); 'No understanding / knowledge' for Unit Trusts (P12); 'A fair degree of understanding / knowledge' for savings accounts and cash ISAs (P12). The client has invested in current/savings accounts by himself (P1), stocks and shares by himself (P1), and pension products through a financial adviser (P1). The fact find (P6) records advisor notes: 'Only ATR 4. Expected growth is 4.5%, they already get this on moneybox ISA's.' and notes the client checks valuations annually (P1).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report characterizes the client's knowledge as 'limited or moderate' while the risk profile questionnaire records 'Very little understanding / knowledge' as the client's self-assessment of overall investing experience (P12). 'Very little' is materially lower than 'limited or moderate'.
      - The report does not cite or reflect the direct questionnaire responses showing 'No understanding / knowledge' for stocks and shares (P1) and Unit Trusts (P12), nor the 'Very little understanding / knowledge' for pension planning (P1), instead presenting a generalized characterization.
    The questionnaire responses (completed 09/08/2025, category D) are the formal recorded assessment of the client's knowledge at the time of advice. They show specific products where the client has no understanding (stocks and shares, Unit Trusts, EIS/VCTs) and very little understanding of pension planning. The report's characterization of 'limited or moderate experience' is a softer description than what the questionnaire records. The client does report some practical experience: investing in savings accounts and stocks by himself, and pension products through an adviser (P1). The client checks valuations annually (P1), indicating some engagement. However, the report's stated level does not fully reflect the depth of assessed knowledge gaps shown in the questionnaire. The questionnaire is the primary evidence of knowledge assessment and predates the suitability advice. The risk profile report (P2, P4) explains that 'If you have limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand then you may be suited to a Cautious Balanced approach' — this is the descriptor for risk level 4, not an independent assessment of the client's knowledge. The report has reused this descriptor language rather than precisely stating what the questionnaire found. The discrepancy is material because the questionnaire shows 'very little' understanding of investing overall, while the report states 'limited or moderate.' No documentation explains how the advisor moved from 'very little' to 'limited or moderate,' or whether advisor discussion at the meeting altered the assessment.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts the following product experience: Cash accounts (hasHeld: true, understanding described as 'fair degree of understanding'); Savings accounts (hasHeld: true, 'fair degree of understanding'); Cash ISAs (hasHeld: true, 'fair degree of understanding'); Unit trusts (hasHeld: false, 'no understanding'); Stocks and shares (hasHeld: true, 'understanding' but also stated 'no understanding' in another quote); Enterprise investment schemes (hasHeld: false, 'no understanding'); Pension planning (hasHeld: true, 'invested through a financial adviser and check valuation annually'). The report states: 'You have a fair degree of understanding of cash accounts, savings accounts, and Cash ISAs' and 'You have invested in current accounts, savings, Cash ISAs, and stocks and shares within the last 5 years, with stocks and shares investments made by yourself.' It also states 'no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes' and 'You have also invested in pension planning through a financial adviser and check the valuation of your investment and retirement portfolio on an annual basis.'
    File:     The Risk Profile Report dated 11 August 2025 records the following responses to the knowledge questionnaire: Do you understand how stocks and shares work: 'No understanding / knowledge'; Do you understand how Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts work: 'No understanding / knowledge'; Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works: 'Very little understanding / knowledge'; Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings within last 5 years: 'Yes, by myself'; Have you invested in Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts within last 5 years: 'No'; Have you invested in stocks and shares within last 5 years: 'Yes, by myself'; Have you invested in Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts within last 5 years: 'No'; Have you invested in pension planning within last 5 years: 'Yes, through a financial adviser'; Do you check the valuation of your investment and retirement portfolio on an annual basis: 'Yes, by myself'.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts 'fair degree of understanding' of stocks and shares; Risk Profile Report questionnaire response records 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares
      - Report asserts client 'has invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years' (consistent with file); however, understanding of stocks and shares is contradicted: report narrative suggests understanding exists but questionnaire records none
      - Report states 'no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes' but also earlier asserts understanding of stocks and shares, creating internal contradiction within the report itself
      - Risk Profile Report records understanding of pension planning as 'Very little understanding / knowledge' whereas report asserts client has invested in pension planning and checks valuation, implying more than very little understanding
    The comparison can be performed because both the report and the Risk Profile Report questionnaire are in the evidence pack. A critical inconsistency exists: the suitability report contains contradictory statements about stocks and shares knowledge. One passage states 'You have a fair degree of understanding of cash accounts, savings accounts, and Cash ISAs' and separately 'You have invested in current accounts, savings, Cash ISAs, and stocks and shares within the last 5 years, with stocks and shares investments made by yourself' — which might suggest competence. However, another passage in the same report states 'no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes'. The Risk Profile Report questionnaire responses, dated 09/08/2025 and signed off before the suitability report advice, definitively record: 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge.' This is unambiguous and contemporary evidence. The extraction noted this ambiguity in the canonical model. The report's knowledge narrative does not align with what the client recorded in the questionnaire. For pension planning, the questionnaire records 'Very little understanding / knowledge' but the report narrative suggests the client checks valuations annually, which implies familiarity rather than 'very little'. However, the questionnaire specifically asks about understanding of pension planning and income in retirement, which the client rated as 'Very little' — a direct answer to the knowledge question. The report acknowledges pension experience via adviser but conflates experience (holding a product) with understanding (comprehension of how it works and associated risks). The guard on this comparison is that 'Plans held do not prove understanding — do not treat plan ownership as evidence of knowledge. The investor-experience questionnaire asks separately about shares, unit trusts, tax-advantaged schemes and pensions. A client recorded as having no understanding of pension planning is the material answer where the recommendation is a pension, whatever the other rows say.' Applying this guard: the client's very little understanding of pension planning is what matters, not the fact of holding a pension. The recommended product is the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II within a pension wrapper, described in the risk profile as risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced). The report recommends a pension product to a client who records 'Very little understanding' of pension planning and 'No understanding' of stocks and shares. The Multi-Asset Plus Fund II is a multi-asset fund with equity exposure, which requires understanding of stock market risk. The questionnaire record shows the client does not have this understanding.

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts three recommended products: (1) Aviva Personal Pension (SIPP) — classified as 'Complex' with drivers including flexi-access drawdown, multiple fund choice (over 6,900 funds), tax relief and annual allowance management, death benefits, and inheritance tax treatment from April 2027. (2) Aviva Platform ISA (Stocks & Shares) — classified as 'NonComplex' with drivers including standard tax-free wrapper, £20,000 annual limit, and fund selection within managed proposition. (3) Aviva Platform GIA (General Investment Account) — classified as 'NonComplex' with drivers including standard non-tax-wrapped account, £3,000 CGT allowance, fund selection within managed proposition, and Bed & ISA facility. The report specifies the recommended fund across the pension is Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II.
    File:     The evidence confirms product literature exists for all three recommended products: Aviva Pension Key Features (P14, P21) describes flexi-access drawdown, phased income drawdown, capped drawdown options, death benefits before age 75 (tax-free lump sum, drawdown, or dependents annuity), and government actuary limits. Aviva ISA Key Features (P10, P18) describes a standard stocks and shares ISA with tax-free investment wrapper, minimum £50/month or £1,000 single payment, fund switching restrictions during deferment, and minimum holdings of £250 per portfolio and £50 per fund. Aviva GIA Key Features (P2, P6, P24) describes standard non-tax-wrapped investment account, automatic withdrawal facility, fund selection, and clarifies that initial and ongoing costs are embedded in fund prices. The Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II Factsheet (P11, P17) confirms risk rating of 4, includes risk warnings A (General), B (Foreign Exchange Risk), C (Emerging Markets), E (Fixed Interest), and F (Derivatives). Research reports (P7, P8, P13, P16) confirm risk level 4 mapping and portfolio composition. The pension illustration (P19) shows 100% investment in Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II S14 with 0.21% Aviva charge.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states SIPP complexity drivers include 'Flexi-access drawdown with discretionary withdrawal options' but evidence shows multiple drawdown types available (flexi-access, phased income drawdown, capped drawdown) without specification of which applies to this client's recommended arrangement.
      - Report states product complexity drivers include 'Multiple fund choice (over 6,900 funds available)' but pension illustration (P19) shows 100% invested in single fund Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II S14; no evidence of client exercising discretionary fund choice or that 6,900+ fund choice is actually available to this client on recommended product.
      - Report claims complexity driver 'Inheritance tax treatment from April 2027' but no evidence in any Key Features document or illustration explaining how April 2027 inheritance tax rules apply to this specific SIPP or affecting recommended product complexity.
      - Report classifies ISA as 'NonComplex' but evidence (P18) shows structured products available within ISA Portfolio with restrictions (no partial withdrawals, fixed-term, potential loss of original investment) — these features not disclosed in report's complexity description.
      - Report's complexity assessment for GIA omits mention of structured products available on the platform (P6, P24) which carry fixed-term risk and potential capital loss, contrary to standard GIA description.
    The report describes the Aviva Personal Pension (SIPP) as complex with multiple drivers, which is consistent with general SIPP characteristics and supported by Key Features literature confirming flexi-access drawdown, death benefits, and tax treatment complexity. However, three complexity drivers lack corroboration: (1) The '6,900 funds available' claim contradicts the pension illustration (P19) which shows 100% allocation to a single fund with no evidence of actual discretionary choice being available or exercised; this appears to be a generic platform capability statement rather than a feature of the recommended solution. (2) The 'Inheritance tax treatment from April 2027' is stated as a complexity driver but no document in the evidence explains this rule, its relevance to the client, or how it affects SIPP complexity; this appears to be unsupported. (3) Drawdown complexity drivers are stated generally without confirmation that the client was advised on which specific drawdown option (flexi-access, phased income, or capped drawdown) applies to their recommended arrangement. The ISA and GIA classifications as 'NonComplex' are supported for core features (tax wrapper, standard investment account structure). However, the report's complexity assessment omits reference to structured products available within both ISA and GIA portfolios (evidenced in P6, P24, P18), which carry material features (fixed terms, no partial withdrawal, potential capital loss) typically considered in complexity assessment. For ISA, P18 explicitly warns: 'Structured products don't allow partial withdrawals because they are fixed-term investments...You may not get back the amount you originally invested.' This feature is material to complexity but absent from the report's NonComplex classification rationale. The recommended fund itself (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II) is confirmed in evidence (P9, P11, P13, P16, P17, P19) as a mainstream multi-asset fund with risk rating 4, which is not inherently complex. Risk warnings A, B, C, E, F documented in the factsheet (P17) are consistent with a multi-asset fund profile and do not escalate complexity beyond expected for the risk level. The evidence does not indicate whether client comprehension of SIPP complexity features was tested or documented, as required where complex products are recommended to retail clients. The ambiguity in the extraction metadata regarding whether Multi-Asset Plus II represents a switch or rename of existing Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings cannot be resolved from the available evidence and does not directly affect complexity assessment, but it does create uncertainty about whether the complexity profile changes materially from existing arrangements.

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states the client has 'Limited' knowledge and experience [P5]: 'you describe yourself as having very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing. You have a fair degree of understanding of cash accounts, savings accounts, and Cash ISAs, but no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes.' The report identifies three recommended products with complexity ratings: [Complex, NonComplex, NonComplex]. The report recommends a Cautious Balanced portfolio (risk level 4) mapped to multi-asset funds suitable for clients with 'limited or moderate experience of investments' [P6]. The report states the recommended funds are 'CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc (GBP)' for GIA and ISA [P18, P20], and an Aviva insured fund for the pension [P8]. The report concludes these align with the client's agreed risk profile and experience level.
    File:     The fact find [P21] records the client 'did not have time to complete an ATRQ today, so have sent him an email version to complete himself on Defaqto.' The Attitude to Risk Questionnaire completed on 09/08/2025 [P4, P16] shows: 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares; 'Very little understanding / knowledge' of pension planning; but 'Yes, by myself' for investments in current accounts, savings, Cash ISAs, and stocks and shares within the last 5 years; 'Yes, by myself' for checking valuations annually. The risk profile description [P7] states: 'If you have limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand then you may be suited to a Cautious Balanced approach.' The suitability report [P5] confirms this assessment internally: 'you tend to see yourself as quite a cautious person and are inclined to view risk negatively rather than as a source of opportunity. You have limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand, but you are suited to a Cautious Balanced approach to investing.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The complexity rating array [Complex, NonComplex, NonComplex] in the canonical model is inconsistent with the narrative that all three recommended products (pension, GIA, ISA) are mapped to the same risk level 4 Cautious Balanced approach. No passage identifies which product is rated Complex.
      - The report asserts client understanding but provides no evidence in category C (client comprehension records) that the client demonstrated understanding of the recommended multi-asset fund structure, asset allocation methodology, or platform mechanics beyond acknowledging the risk level.
      - The fact find records the ATR questionnaire was not completed in person but sent by email for self-completion [P21], yet the report treats the resulting responses as establishing the client's knowledge baseline without noting this limitation.
    The report's stated knowledge level ('Limited') is corroborated by the questionnaire responses in the file [P4, P16]: the client records 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares and 'Very little understanding / knowledge' of pension planning. The risk profile precondition is satisfied: the file documents 'limited or moderate experience of investments' and the client 'do[es] not find financial matters particularly easy to understand' [P7], matching the descriptor for risk level 4. The recommended Cautious Balanced portfolio is aligned with this profile. However, two concerns arise. First, the canonical complexity ratings show [Complex, NonComplex, NonComplex], but no passage identifies which product is Complex or explains why it warrants that rating given the client's Limited knowledge. The Cautious Balanced description in the research reports [P18, P20] describes the funds as 'risk rated' multi-asset investments suitable for this profile, but neither passage addresses product complexity per se—complexity and risk rating are distinct dimensions. Second, the fact find [P21] records the ATR questionnaire was not completed face-to-face but 'sent by email' for self-completion, introducing a methodological concern about the reliability of self-assessed knowledge responses. The report does not acknowledge or address this limitation. Third, no category C evidence (client comprehension records) is present in the pack demonstrating the client understood the mechanics of the recommended multi-asset structure, the platform operation, asset allocation, or how the funds work. For a client with stated 'very little understanding' and 'no understanding' of unit trusts and stocks/shares [P5], comprehension corroboration is material where any product is complex. The guard 'You confirmed you understand' is an adviser assertion; it does not corroborate understanding without C-category evidence. The report's internal consistency is sound (the Cautious Balanced description is applied consistently [P5, P6, P7]), but the file evidence does not support a finding that the client's knowledge has been established beyond the questionnaire responses, particularly given the email completion method and the absence of comprehension records.

  [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report states that the client 'describes very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' and that the client 'check[s] the valuation of your investment and retirement portfolio on an annual basis'. The report also records that the client 'have limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand, but you are suited to a Cautious Balanced approach to investing' with an agreed risk profile of 4 on a scale of 1-10.

Meeting notes [P8] state 'only ATR 4' and 'Expected growth is 4.5%, they already get this on moneybox ISA's'. The risk profile report [P19] records 'No additional notes were recorded' under both 'Investment target' and 'Agreed risk level' sections, with no documented client questions or evidence of comprehension discussion. The case file contains no record of client questions about the recommended fund, its features, risks or complexity, nor any documented evidence that the client demonstrated understanding of the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II or its characteristics.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the client has 'very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' yet recommends a switch to a multi-asset fund requiring evaluation of switching costs, fund complexity and ongoing management. No record exists of how this comprehension gap was addressed or bridged.
      - The report describes annual portfolio review frequency as evidence of knowledge, but the fact find and notes show the client was unable to complete the attitude to risk questionnaire in person and had to be sent an email version, suggesting limited financial engagement.
      - The risk profile report records 'No additional notes were recorded' for investment target and agreed risk level discussions, creating a gap between the stated knowledge assessment and documented evidence of comprehension.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Record of client questions or dialogue about the recommended fund's features, risks and complexity, Documentation of how the adviser established that a client with 'very little understanding or knowledge' could evaluate the risks and features of the Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II, Evidence of client comprehension of the fund's characteristics, investment approach, or suitability relative to the existing Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings, Notes or evidence from the risk profiling discussion showing how risk level 4 alignment was explained and understood by the client, Any discussion or evidence of client understanding of the switch rationale, charges comparison, or performance requirements
    The comparison cannot be completed because the evidence required to establish the client's demonstrated comprehension of the recommended product is absent from the case file. COBS 9.2.1R and 9A.2.1R require that the suitability report evidence the client can evaluate risks and features of complex products. The report correctly identifies the client as having 'very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' and assigns risk profile 4, suitable for a 'Cautious Balanced approach'. However, no evidence exists that the client's limited knowledge was bridged through explanation or that comprehension was verified before the recommendation was made. The risk profile report [P19] explicitly records 'No additional notes were recorded' for investment target and agreed risk level, indicating no substantive discussion of comprehension is documented. The fact find [P10] shows the client could not complete the attitude-to-risk questionnaire in person and required an email version, suggesting limited financial literacy and engagement. The meeting notes [P8] record the ATR as '4' and reference existing ISA growth expectations, but contain no dialogue about fund selection, complexity evaluation or risk feature comprehension. Where a product is recommended to a client recorded as having very limited investment knowledge, the advisor must document evidence that the client understood its features and risks. The absence of such evidence is a material gap. The report's assertion that the client checks valuations annually is insufficient to evidence comprehension of a fund switch decision involving charge comparisons (0.08% increase, £63 annually), asset allocation changes, and ongoing professional oversight costs (1% fee). The regulatory requirement under COBS 9.2.1R and 9A.2.1R turns on demonstrated comprehension by questions or other evidence; internal consistency of the report's own statements does not satisfy this. No document in the case file records client questions or any evidence the client evaluated or understood the recommended product's complexity.

  [G4.6] Existing holdings evidencing investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts the client has: fair degree of understanding of cash accounts, savings accounts, and Cash ISAs; prior investment in current accounts, savings, Cash ISAs, and stocks and shares within the last 5 years (stocks and shares by himself); prior investment in pension planning through a financial adviser; checks pension valuation annually. Current holdings in SIPP: Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity (35.02%), Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (64.95%), Cash (0.03%). No understanding of unit trusts, enterprise investment schemes. Holdings show weighted average risk profile of approximately 6 (60% fund at risk 5, 80% fund at risk 7).
    File:     Transaction history [P9], [P11] shows holdings of Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity with regular monthly transactions dating back to at least January 2018, evidencing continuous ownership and active management over 7+ years. Client report [P3] shows opening value £59,017.42 as of 24/11/2022, closing value £79,092.32 as of 23/11/2025, with 34.02% cumulative return, evidencing performance tracking. Fact Find [P2], [P4] records employer pension scheme eligibility (member status unclear but listed as not currently a member) and client's stated objectives to 'Review his Aviva pension and look to improve upon it' and desire to 'be fully retired by 60' with minimum retirement income £2,200 net monthly. No record in the file of stocks and shares holdings outside the pension, nor detail of when those were acquired or how they were managed. No evidence of other product types (unit trusts, enterprise investment schemes) in holdings. Fact Find shows client 'Wants to be fully retired by 60' and is reviewing pension arrangements, consistent with annual monitoring claim.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims client invested in 'stocks and shares within the last 5 years, with stocks and shares investments made by yourself' but the file records only Vanguard LifeStrategy funds (which are multi-asset funds containing equity exposure, not direct stocks and shares holdings) within the pension, with no evidence of self-directed stock picking outside the pension arrangement.
      - Report states client has 'no understanding of unit trusts' but Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% funds are themselves unit trusts or ICVC structures, which the client has held for 7+ years. A client holding these funds for over seven years without understanding the product structure is internally inconsistent with the demonstrated experience of holding them.
      - Report asserts client 'check[s] the valuation of your investment and retirement portfolio on an annual basis' but transaction history and client report provide no evidence of systematic annual review dates or adviser-documented annual check points; client report shows quarterly statements are sent [P23] but no record of client-initiated reviews.
    The comparison can be performed as holdings records, transaction history, and client report are on file. Three material inconsistencies arise: First, the report's claim of self-directed stocks and shares investment cannot be corroborated from the file. The holdings shown are Vanguard LifeStrategy funds held within a pension wrapper; these are managed funds, not direct equity investments. No evidence exists of self-directed stock selection or holdings outside the pension. This represents either an overstatement of the client's demonstrated investment experience or evidence of a different investment history not recorded in the case file. Second, the report characterises the client as having 'no understanding of unit trusts' while simultaneously recording 7+ years of continuous holdings in Vanguard LifeStrategy funds, which are unit trust vehicles. This is a direct internal contradiction: a client cannot hold a product type without understanding for over seven years without some implicit experience of that product structure, even if passive. The guard recognises that holding a product does not prove understanding of its mechanics, but claiming simultaneous non-understanding of a product the client has held continuously for 7+ years is implausible. Third, the annual valuation check is stated but the file records quarterly statements [P23] being sent automatically, with no documented evidence of client-initiated annual reviews or adviser-recorded annual monitoring touchpoints. The Fact Find [P4] records that the client 'check[s]' his portfolio annually, which may be self-initiated, but no check-in records or review dates appear in the transaction history or adviser notes to corroborate the pattern claimed. The holdings themselves are consistent with a risk profile of 6 (blended 60/80 allocation), which aligns with the report's later statement that the client's risk profile is 4 and a switch to risk 4 is warranted — but this is not a contradiction; it is context for the suitability of the recommendation. The client's status as an existing pension investor with holdings of 7+ years duration does evidence some pension and investment experience, and the Fact Find confirms pension review and planning is on the agenda. However, the characterisation of that experience is internally contradicted and overstated relative to the file.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report identifies three recommended products with complexity ratings: (1) a pension (Complex) with complexity drivers including flexi-access drawdown, discretionary withdrawal options, multiple fund choice (over 6,900 funds), tax relief calculations, annual allowance management, death benefits, beneficiary designation, and inheritance tax treatment from April 2027; (2) a GIA (NonComplex) with standard tax-free investment wrapper, annual CGT allowance, fund selection within managed proposition, and Bed & ISA facility; (3) an ISA (NonComplex) with standard investment account, annual CGT allowance, fund selection within managed proposition. The report provides rationale for the recommendations including: risk alignment (current funds risk level 6 do not match agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile 4; recommended fund designed for risk level 4); lower costs (platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21%); professional oversight and monitoring; tax efficiency (pension contribution of £3,600 gross utilising annual allowance; GIA allowing CGT allowance planning; Bed & ISA facility for gradual tax-efficient transfers); and platform suitability. The report does not explicitly state the client's knowledge level or prior investment experience relevant to managing complex products like flexi-access drawdown.
    File:     The Fact Find indicates the client 'is about to receive circa £100k but will use £40k on kitchen/bathroom' with another £40k in high interest savings, and notes state 'Only ATR 4' (Attitude to Risk 4). Adviser notes record: 'Has he been enrolled into a workplace scheme yet? Defo opting out? – quit before he could. We need to assess earnings for this year, and pension contributions for this year. Does he have a P60/P45? Possible pension contribution can be done if he's still working. – none. Aviva appears to be only pension and no contributions made.' The notes also state 'Retired, gets DB income, but works part-time. When will he stop? Defo 60?' The Risk Profile Report records 'No additional notes were recorded' under both the investment target section and agreed risk level section. The Fact Find notes 'Client did not have time to complete an ATRQ today, so have sent him an email version to complete himself on Defaqto.' No formal assessment of the client's investment knowledge, experience with complex products, understanding of pension flexibility features, drawdown mechanics, or comprehension of annual allowance/inheritance tax implications is recorded in the file. The evidence shows the client has an existing Aviva pension but the notes indicate no prior contributions were made to it ('Aviva appears to be only pension and no contributions made'). There is no record of discussion or confirmation of the client's understanding of flexi-access drawdown, discretionary withdrawals, tax relief calculations, or beneficiary designation requirements.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report describes complexity drivers for pension including 'flexi-access drawdown with discretionary withdrawal options,' 'tax relief calculations and annual allowance management,' 'death benefits and beneficiary designation requirements,' and 'inheritance tax treatment from April 2027,' but the file contains no evidence of the client's prior experience with or understanding of these features
      - Report marks pension as 'Complex' but provides no explicit statement of the client's knowledge level or investment experience to support suitability of complex product recommendation
      - Rationale provided focuses on risk alignment and cost reduction but does not address the client's capability to understand or manage the complexity drivers of flexi-access drawdown and discretionary withdrawal mechanics
      - Risk Profile Report records 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Agreed risk level' section, suggesting no documented discussion of how risk profile translates to product complexity suitability
      - Fact Find notes indicate client 'did not have time to complete an ATRQ today' but no evidence provided that client subsequently completed it or that results were reviewed to assess knowledge/experience
    The report classifies the recommended pension as 'Complex' and identifies twelve specific complexity drivers, several of which require specialist knowledge: flexi-access drawdown with discretionary withdrawal options, tax relief calculations, annual allowance management, death benefits and beneficiary designations, and inheritance tax treatment from 2027. COBS 9.2.1R and 9.2.2R require that complex products be recommended only where the adviser has assessed the client as having the knowledge and experience to understand the risks involved. The file demonstrates the client's agreed risk rating is 4 (Cautious Balanced), derived from a questionnaire the client did not complete in person during the advice meeting but was sent by email afterwards. The Risk Profile Report contains no additional notes explaining how the client's knowledge or experience was assessed, and records 'No additional notes were recorded' under the agreed risk level section. The Fact Find records the client is 'Retired, gets DB income, but works part-time' and is about to receive a £100k inheritance. No prior pension or investment experience is documented except reference to an existing Aviva pension with 'no contributions made.' The adviser's notes indicate uncertainty about the client's employment status and pension eligibility ('Has he been enrolled into a workplace scheme yet? Defo opting out?') rather than a clear assessment of knowledge and experience. The rationale for the pension recommendation emphasizes risk alignment (current funds risk 6 vs. agreed risk 4) and cost savings (0.32% to 0.21% platform charge) but does not justify why a client recorded as having an ATR of 4 and no apparent prior experience with complex drawdown products should be recommended a product featuring discretionary withdrawals, tax relief calculations, and inheritance tax treatment management. The report provides general rationale about the suitability of the pension and GIA platforms but does not explain how these complexity drivers are suitable for the client's demonstrated knowledge and experience. The absence of documented discussion, client comprehension check, or knowledge assessment specific to the complex features represents a gap between what the report asserts (that rationale is 'adequately justified') and what the file evidences (no such justification at the complexity-driver level). The GIA and ISA recommendations are marked NonComplex and their rationale (tax efficiency through CGT planning and Bed & ISA) is proportionate to a novice investor. The pension complexity, however, stands unsupported by evidence of client knowledge or prior experience.
