CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.7, G5.8). Report asserts initial commitment of £80,012 but figure cannot be reconciled: £79,092.32 (pension transfer per [P12]) + £57,100 (GIA per [P21], not £57,120) + £20,000 (ISA) + £3,600 (pension contribution per [P12]) = £159,792.32 gross, less £4,794.37 fee (per [P19]) = £155,005.93 net invested, not £80,012. The £80,012 figure appears to represent only the new client cash outlay (£57,120 + £20,000 + £3,600 = £80,720 before fee consideration, close to £80,012) but this conflates transfer value with new client commitment. Report states ongoing commitment of £1,558 annually as 1% platform fee, but this is internally inconsistent: if £1,558 = 1% then portfolio value would be £155,800. However, the actual initial total to be invested is £159,792 (£79,092 pension + £57,100 GIA + £20,000 ISA + £3,600 contribution). At £159,792, a 1% ongoing fee would be £1,598, not £1,558. The £1,558 figure appears to exclude the ISA and/or misstate the base. Report asserts 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' (£3,600 gross per [P12]) but the file shows Anthony's employment income is zero—he retired from factory role after 35 years and part-time driving income (£890 net/month previously) has since stopped. The basis on which current earnings support the contribution is not evidenced and contradicts the cashflow data. 23 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts initial commitment of £80,012 net from client (derived as £57,120 GIA + £20,000 ISA + £3,600 net pension contribution after £4,794 advice fee). Report states ongoing commitment of £1,558 annually (1% platform fee on approximately £155,800 total portfolio value). Report asserts affordability on four bases: (1) inheritance funds can cover initial investment without affecting lifestyle; (2) guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions is resilient; (3) significant liquid funds provide resilience to volatility; (4) current earnings are sufficient to support the £3,600 gross contribution. Report also states 'We are unsure of your earnings this tax year, as you retired and then only worked part-time' but concludes earnings are sufficient without specifying current earnings amount or basis.
    File:     Cashflow evidence [P1] shows combined household income of £38,800 in 2025 (Anthony DB Pension £22,000 + Yvette Employment £16,800), with no employment income from Anthony stated. Expenditure is £29,915, yielding £8,885 annual surplus (0% saved per assumptions [P5]). Liquid assets before recommendation are stated as £190,000 comprising inheritance £100,000 (due this month), Chartered savings £40,000, two Cash ISAs £20,000 each, and emergency fund £10,000. After recommended investments are deployed, liquid assets at 2025 in cashflow table [P1] show £225,018. Ongoing adviser charge of 1% is stated in [P14] and [P19] as calculated on portfolio value, estimated at £47.60 monthly in [P19]. Report's own illustration [P21] shows GIA investment of £57,100 annually with combined charges reducing value. Report's illustration [P22] shows pension investment with charges applied. Cashflow projections [P1] through [P11] show consistent affordability across scenarios with no funding stress. No current employment income for Anthony is evidenced in the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts initial commitment of £80,012 but figure cannot be reconciled: £79,092.32 (pension transfer per [P12]) + £57,100 (GIA per [P21], not £57,120) + £20,000 (ISA) + £3,600 (pension contribution per [P12]) = £159,792.32 gross, less £4,794.37 fee (per [P19]) = £155,005.93 net invested, not £80,012. The £80,012 figure appears to represent only the new client cash outlay (£57,120 + £20,000 + £3,600 = £80,720 before fee consideration, close to £80,012) but this conflates transfer value with new client commitment.
      - Report states ongoing commitment of £1,558 annually as 1% platform fee, but this is internally inconsistent: if £1,558 = 1% then portfolio value would be £155,800. However, the actual initial total to be invested is £159,792 (£79,092 pension + £57,100 GIA + £20,000 ISA + £3,600 contribution). At £159,792, a 1% ongoing fee would be £1,598, not £1,558. The £1,558 figure appears to exclude the ISA and/or misstate the base.
      - Report asserts 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' (£3,600 gross per [P12]) but the file shows Anthony's employment income is zero—he retired from factory role after 35 years and part-time driving income (£890 net/month previously) has since stopped. The basis on which current earnings support the contribution is not evidenced and contradicts the cashflow data.
      - Liquid assets at start of 2025 in cashflow [P1] are £225,018, not £190,000. The £190,000 figure in the report is the pre-recommendation position. The report does not clarify whether affordability is assessed against pre- or post-deployment liquidity.
      - Cashflow table [P1] shows 2025 surplus of £0 with income of £38,800 and expenditure of £29,915. This £8,885 in-year surplus is offset to zero by the surplus income percentage saved assumption of 0%, per [P5]. This is consistent with the report's claim that income covers baseline expenditure, but the mechanism by which the annual £1,558 ongoing fee is paid from this zero surplus is not explained.
    The report asserts affordability on the basis of (1) inheritance funds covering the upfront investment, (2) guaranteed State and Private Pension income covering baseline expenditure, (3) significant liquid reserves providing resilience, and (4) earnings supporting contributions. The evidence largely supports points (1)–(3): inheritance of £100,000 due this month is documented [P3], [P4]; combined guaranteed income of £38,800 (State Pension from age 67, DB Pension £22,000, Yvette employment £16,800) is documented; and liquid assets of £190,000 pre-recommendation and £225,018 post-recommendation are shown [P1]. However, point (4)—that current earnings support the £3,600 contribution—cannot be supported: Anthony's employment income is zero post-retirement; his part-time driving income (£890 net/month) has ceased per extraction note; and the report itself states 'we are unsure of your earnings this tax year'. The £3,600 gross contribution (£2,880 net per [P12]) cannot be funded from current earnings; it must be funded from inheritance, savings or pension drawdown, and the affordability assessment should not conflate this with earnings-supported contribution capacity. The initial commitment figure of £80,012 does not reconcile to any logical sum of the components: the raw outlay (£57,120 + £20,000 + £3,600 = £80,720) includes no fee deduction yet the canonical note says it is 'net of advice fee', and £4,794 reduction yields £80,720 − £4,794 = £75,926 or £80,720 − (portion of fee allocated to initial deployment) which does not yield £80,012 without further undocumented allocation. The ongoing commitment of £1,558 represents 1% of approximately £155,800, but the actual total to be invested is £159,792. The discrepancy (£1,558 vs. ~£1,598 at 1% of actual total) suggests either the fee base is misstated or the rate differs from the stated 1%. Cashflow projections [P1] through [P11] demonstrate that the baseline household income (£38,800 reducing to £22,000 when Yvette retires) covers expenditure even in stress scenarios (market crash, predecease), and the £100,000 inheritance is available to front-load the investment. The affordability of the initial capital deployment is therefore supported by evidence. However, the affordability of the ongoing £1,558 annual fee from a zero in-year surplus, after fees are deducted from the cash account per [P19] and [P22], relies on the assumption that the investment portfolio itself will generate sufficient income or that the client's liquid reserves (now £225,018 post-deployment) can absorb the ongoing charge without drawing into allocated emergency funds. This is supported by the cashflow projections which show no stress even with £10,000 emergency fund in place. Consistency fails on three counts: (1) the earnings support claim is unsupported and contradicts evidence; (2) the initial commitment figure cannot be reconciled; and (3) the ongoing fee basis appears misstated. These are material because the report relies on 'current earnings are sufficient' as an affordability pillar but provides no earnings to support that claim.

  [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

(6) No quantified definition of 'sufficient' emergency fund requirement is found in any document.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts client 'confirmed sufficient liquid capital' but provides no benchmark or definition of sufficiency for the emergency fund amount. Fact Find [P11] records required amount (£10,000) matching held amount (£10,000), but this appears as a reconciliation of what is already held, not an independent calculation of need.
      - Report states renovation spending 'circa £40,000...on renovations' and provides date 2025-12-16, but the actual required date for the expenditure is not explicitly stated in the report beyond 'this month' reference to inheritance receipt.
      - Cashflow analysis [P3] lists emergency fund (£10,000) as eighth in liquidation sequence, after Chartered Savings (£40,000), Aviva ISA (£20,000), Wife's MoneyBox ISA (£20,000), and Aviva GIA (£52,326), indicating the emergency fund is protected from routine drawdown. However, the report does not explain this ordering or confirm that the fund will remain accessible and untouched under the recommendation.
      - Report references 'Flexi Access Drawdown' as access method but does not quantify what income level is anticipated, whether the drawdown is designed to sustain the emergency fund, or whether any recommendation (such as the fund switch to Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II) affects drawdown capacity or access timing.
      - Fact Find shows £383.33 monthly disposable income after all expenditure. If all recommended contributions (£3,600 gross annually = £300/month net approximately) come from this disposable income, only £83.33/month remains unallocated. The report does not reconcile the contribution commitment against this tight monthly surplus or explain how the emergency fund is maintained if disposable income is committed to pension contributions.
    The report asserts that the emergency fund of £10,000 is sufficient and that the client has confirmed retention of sufficient liquid capital. The fact find corroborates the £10,000 amount but does not establish the basis for sufficiency independently. The matching of 'held amount' (£10,000) to 'required amount' (£10,000) in [P11] represents internal consistency within the fact find, not external support. No passage explains what period of expenditure, contingency or specific need the £10,000 is intended to cover. The monthly disposable income of £383.33 (after all regular expenditure) is tight and does not visibly accommodate both pension contributions and building reserves. The cashflow models [P1], [P5], [P7] project stability over decades and show no shortfalls, suggesting the combination of liquid assets, pensions and income is sufficient in aggregate, but they do not isolate the emergency fund or explain how it is preserved. The Aviva platform documents confirm that cash accounts are accessible and that withdrawal procedures exist, satisfying the mechanical requirement for liquidity. The planned £40,000 renovation is identified as a foreseeable need funded from inheritance, and the inheritance itself is recorded as available 'this month' (December 2025). The renovation is ring-fenced from the emergency fund in the liquidation schedule. However, the suitability report does not explain how the recommendation (pension contributions and fund switch) interacts with the emergency fund commitment or monthly income, and does not quantify the required contribution in detail or confirm that it can be sustained without depleting the emergency fund or disposable income margin. The report relies on a general statement of client confirmation of sufficiency rather than a specific quantified analysis. This represents an assertion without detailed supporting workings visible in the evidence provided. The gap is not a numerical mismatch, but a missing link between the sufficiency claim and the inputs (monthly surplus, contribution commitment, fund access) that would justify it.

  [G5.4] Initial and ongoing commitments are unaffordable based on income, expenditure and cashflow
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts initial commitment of £80,012 and ongoing commitment of £1,558 per annum. Report states affordability is supported by: (1) 'This is an amount you can comfortably afford from your inheritance funds without affecting your current lifestyle'; (2) 'You have a good level of guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions'; (3) 'You also have significant funds to cover this, providing you with greater resilience to investment volatility'; (4) 'Your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level'. Report infers monthly surplus of £1,190 based on net monthly income of £2,590 and monthly expenditure of £2,206.67.
    File:     Fact Find establishes: net monthly income £2,590 (consisting of employed basic income £890 net plus private pension £1,700 net); total monthly expenditure £2,206.67; disposable income £383.33 per month. Employment income description shows £1,166.67 gross / £890 net, marked as 'Employed Monthly' with end date 06/08/2025, indicating this employment has terminated or will terminate. Private pension (Aon Final Salary) £1,833 gross / £1,700 net has no end date. Emergency fund set at £10,000. Liquid assets total £190,000 comprising £100,000 inheritance (due this month), £40,000 Chartered savings, £20,000 client Cash ISA, £20,000 Yvette Cash ISA, £10,000 emergency fund. Fact Find states 'Do you expect any changes in your income in the foreseeable future?: No'. Cashflow projections across three scenarios (Market Crash, Tony Predeceases, Yvette Predeceases) show liquid assets remain substantial throughout retirement (£144,867 to £197,715 by 2057 depending on scenario). Ongoing adviser charge documented as 1% per annum on platform value.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report's inferred monthly surplus of £1,190 contradicts Fact Find's calculated disposable income of £383.33 per month — a difference of £806.67 monthly
      - Report asserts 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but Fact Find shows employment income (£890 net) ended 06/08/2025, making current earnings zero
      - Report's initial commitment figure of £80,012 does not match extracted figures: pension transfer £79,092 + GIA £57,120 + ISA £20,000 + pension contribution £3,600 = £159,812 gross, or after £4,794 advice fee = £155,018, not £80,012
      - Ongoing commitment of £1,558 asserted but nearest evidenced figure is £1,700 monthly pension income (9.1% away); no document corroborates £1,558 as annual commitment or monthly figure
      - Report claims affordability from 'current earnings' but evidence shows no current employment income post-06/08/2025
    The Fact Find documents monthly net income of £2,590 and monthly expenditure of £2,206.67, yielding disposable income of £383.33 per month. The report infers a monthly surplus of £1,190, which is 3.1 times the evidenced disposable income. This discrepancy is material: the report's affordability case rests on both income sufficiency and liquid asset availability, but the cashflow foundation differs significantly between the two documents. The employment income line in the Fact Find (£890 net monthly, end date 06/08/2025) indicates this income source has ceased. The report states 'Your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' (£3,600 gross annually or £300 monthly), but provides no specification of what those current earnings are. Given that the Fact Find records only pension income (£1,700 net) as ongoing, the statement about current earnings supporting contributions is unsupported by documented income. However, the report's affordability assessment does not rely primarily on monthly earnings: it relies on the £100,000 inheritance funds to cover initial investment (£80,012) and the significant liquid asset base (£190,000 total) to provide resilience. The ongoing commitment of £1,558 per annum is stated as advice fee at 1% of platform value. Cashflow projections across all three scenarios show adequate liquid assets throughout retirement, with the inheritance absorbed into the investment base. The emergency fund (£10,000) is preserved in projections. Initial commitment affordability from inheritance is evidenced and not contradicted. Ongoing commitment affordability depends on (1) whether ongoing fee of £1,558 can be met from disposable income (£383.33 monthly = £4,600 annually, which exceeds £1,558), or (2) whether it is charged against investment returns as stated. The report's vulnerability overlay notes the client is post-retirement from full-time employment; no vulnerability indicators are flagged that would materially affect this assessment. The stated basis of affordability—inheritance funds for initial investment and pension income plus liquid reserves for ongoing resilience—is supported by the evidence for the inheritance-funded portion, though the statement about 'current earnings' is not.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts in the liquidityAndAccessNeeds section that emergency fund liquidity is funded from two sources: 'Inheritance from late father's estate' and 'Aviva Pension Portfolio via Flexi Access Drawdown'. The report does not quantify an emergency fund shortfall. The canonical model shows the emergencyFund/shortfall path is absent from the extracted document.
    File:     The cashflow documents [P1, P2, P3] consistently identify a 'Cash Emergency Fund £10,000' as a separate liquid asset, listed as LIQUIDATED 8TH in the liquidation order across three scenarios (Current Situation, Market Crash, Yvette Predeceases 2040). The emergency fund is held in a Cash Account at 1.25% expected return p/a and passes to Yvette on death of Anthony. The cashflow tables [P11, P12, P15] show opening liquid assets including accessible pensions remain positive throughout the projection (never fall below £120,499 in the Higher Inflation scenario through to 2044). The surplus account is LIQUIDATED 1ST showing £0 opening balance across all scenarios [P1, P2, P3]. No scenario shows the £10,000 emergency fund being drawn down to meet shortfalls; instead, required withdrawals are stated as zero across all shown years.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states in liquidityAndAccessNeeds that emergency fund is funded from 'Inheritance from late father's estate' and 'Aviva Pension Portfolio via Flexi Access Drawdown', but the cashflow documents show the £10,000 emergency fund is a pre-existing cash holding, separate from the inheritance which is shown as £100,000 due 'this month' [P1]. The inheritance is not described as funding the emergency reserve but as a separate liquid asset component.
      - The report does not quantify the emergency fund amount or shortfall, whilst the file documents consistently specify £10,000 as the emergency fund. The absence of a quantified shortfall in the report against a documented £10,000 fund suggests the report may not have assessed whether this amount is adequate to the client's needs, though the file shows it is retained throughout the plan.
    The emergency fund is not depleted by the recommendation. The cashflow documents [P1, P2, P3] show the £10,000 emergency fund remains in place throughout the plan and is listed as LIQUIDATED 8TH, meaning it is a last resort before required pension withdrawals—a logical sequencing. The fund is held in liquid form (Cash Account at 1.25% return) and passes to Yvette on Anthony's death, confirming it remains accessible during the client's lifetime. The report's citation of 'Aviva Pension Portfolio via Flexi Access Drawdown' as a source of emergency liquidity is consistent with the client's age (55, so minimum pension age of 55 applies) and the fund's availability. However, the report contains no quantified assessment of whether £10,000 is sufficient. The file does not evidence a needs analysis that calculates the client's emergency expenditure requirement—for example, three to six months of regular outgoings. The annual expenditure shown in [P5] is £26,480 (£9,600 discretionary + regular expenses); a £10,000 emergency fund would cover approximately 4.6 months at that rate, which could be adequate but is never justified in the report. The report's statement that emergency liquidity is funded from inheritance and pension drawdown is materially inaccurate on the inheritance point: the £100,000 inheritance is a separate asset [P1], not a funding source for the emergency reserve. The £10,000 fund exists independently. The report does not explain why the emergency reserve is not supplemented or whether its size relative to expenditure has been considered. This is a disclosure shortfall—the report asserts retention of liquid capital and emergency access but does not show the working that supports 'adequate' provision.

  [G5.6] Liquidity mismatch with access needs

    Report:   The suitability report extracts show `/financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[]/requiredFrom: ["2025-12-16"]` and `/solution/recommendedPlans[]/wrapper: ["SIPP", "GIA", "ISA"]`.       - Report does not specify whether the SIPP recommendation involves contributions (capital lock-in until age 55, already reached), planned withdrawals at 2025-12-16, or a transfer of existing pension. Without this, the wrapper compatibility with 2025-12-16 access cannot be assessed.
      - Report does not quantify the recommended contribution level or specify its affordability mechanics beyond stating current earnings 'are sufficient'.

    The requirement asks whether affordability is 'fully supported by, and consistent with, the evidence provided' from a financial resilience and liquidity perspective, and specifically whether recommended wrappers are compatible with stated access needs. The comparison cannot be completed because the report's core assertion — that there is a liquidity need dated 2025-12-16 — lacks contextual support in the file. No document explains what this date represents, what sum is required, or why.   [G5.7] State Pension entitlement and other anticipated income sources
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts four income sources: (1) defined benefit pension from Aon of £22,000 gross per annum (stated as actual, modality Actual); (2) Yvette's full-time employment as Medical Case Handler earning £28,000 gross per annum (stated as actual, modality Actual); (3) Yvette's intended pro-rata employment at 60% (£16,800 gross per annum, derived as 60% of £28,000 for 3 days per week, modality Intended); and (4) State Pension of £11,973 per annum from age 67 (stated as projected, modality Projected). The report states on page 2: 'You also receive a defined benefit pension from Aon of £22,000 gross per annum', 'Yvette works full-time as a Medical Case Handler, earning £28,000 gross per annum', and 'You both expect to receive the full State Pension of £11,973 per annum from age 67'. Current monthly net income is stated as £2,590.00 with monthly expenditure of £2,206.67, yielding disposable monthly income of £383.33. The report confirms 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' (referenced in ambiguity note regarding employment status). State Pension Age is 67 for both clients.
    File:     The cashflow documents corroborate: (1) DB Pension of £22,000 to Anthony [P9]; (2) Yvette's salary shown as £16,800 p/a until 2030 with note 'retire in 2030' [P1]; (3) State Pension of £11,973 (inflated by 2.5%) from age 67 [P1, P3]. The Fact Find [P5] shows Total Net Monthly Income of £2,590.00 and Total Monthly Expenditure of £2,206.67, yielding Total Monthly Disposable Income of £383.33. However, the Fact Find records Yvette's full-time employment but does not explicitly state £28,000 gross; the figure appears derived from the 60% pro-rata calculation shown in cashflow as £16,800. Anthony's employment income is listed as 'No' for Money Purchase schemes and no current employment income is documented post-retirement from factory role after 35 years. The cashflow documents show Yvette's current income as £16,800 p/a (60% of £28,000) from 2025, not the full £28,000. Anthony's State Pension Age is confirmed as 67 [P2]. Emergency fund is stated as £10,000 with required amount £10,000, yielding zero shortfall [P5].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Yvette's current employment income as £28,000 gross per annum (full-time); cashflow documents show £16,800 p/a as her actual current income until 2030, labelled as 60% pro-rata for 3 days per week
      - Report income reconciliation shows two separate employment income entries (£28,000 and £16,800) suggesting both are current; evidence shows £16,800 is the pro-rata amount reflecting Yvette's part-time working arrangement, not an additional income stream
      - Ambiguity regarding Anthony's current employment status: report states 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but no current employment income is documented in Fact Find post-retirement; cashflow shows zero employment income for Anthony in first year of plan
      - Report's financial position table [P24] lists Personal Pension value as £79,092; canonical model figures and derived figures reference £79,074.07 based on charge calculations, a £17.93 variance
    The core consistency issue concerns Yvette's employment income. The report extracts two employment income figures: £28,000 (stated as Yvette's full-time salary) and £16,800 (derived as 60% for 3 days per week). The cashflow documents demonstrate that £16,800 is Yvette's actual current income, representing a pro-rata reduction from a notional full-time equivalent of £28,000. This is not a contradiction but rather a question of which figure represents current affordability. The Fact Find [P5] records Monthly Net Income of £2,590.00, which the report uses to calculate disposable income of £383.33 per month. This monthly figure must be based on actual current earnings (£22,000 Anthony DB + £16,800 Yvette pro-rata = £38,800, which at typical tax yields approximately £2,590 net monthly), not on the £28,000 full-time salary. Therefore, the report's statement of Yvette's income as '£28,000 gross per annum' in the income section appears to be a notional or maximum figure rather than current income used in affordability calculations. The actual affordability case rests on current surplus of £383.33 per month (£2,590 net income less £2,206.67 expenditure), supported by emergency fund of £10,000 [P5]. Cashflow projections across multiple scenarios (Yvette Predeceases, Tony Predeceases, Market Crash, Higher Inflation) all show positive in-year surplus/deficit of zero or positive through retirement, with liquid assets remaining substantial (ranging from £85,592 to £383,679 depending on scenario) [P10, P13]. State Pension of £11,973 from age 67 is consistently evidenced [P1, P3]. Regarding Anthony's employment income: the Fact Find [P4] shows no current money purchase or personal pension employer schemes with current contributions; his factory employment ended after 35 years. The reference to 'current earnings are sufficient' in the ambiguity note appears to mean his defined benefit pension and Yvette's pro-rata salary are sufficient for the £3,600 gross annual contribution (£300 monthly). The £17.93 discrepancy in pension value is immaterial and likely a rounding difference in fund valuation between report date and extraction date. Overall, the affordability case is internally consistent when Yvette's income is understood as her actual current pro-rata earnings (£16,800), not a notional full-time salary. The report's presentation of '£28,000' without clarifying it represents a conditional or notional full-time equivalent creates ambiguity but does not contradict the actual affordability figures used in calculations, which rest on documented current surplus and projected cashflow. The emergency fund is explicitly addressed and quantified at £10,000 with zero shortfall [P5]. No evidence of vulnerability drivers materially affecting this assessment is identified in the file.

  [G5.8] Planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts five foreseeable changes: (1) Yvette's intended retirement when client reaches age 60 (expected 2030-12-16, financial impact -£16,800 gross); (2) Client received £100,000 inheritance from late father's estate (due this month, 2025-12-16); (3) Yvette's reduction to 3 days per week employment (expected 2026-01-01, financial impact -£11,200 gross); (4) Both clients become eligible for State Pension (expected 2037-12-16, financial impact +£23,946 gross for both); (5) Pension inheritance tax treatment change from 6 April 2027 (no financial impact stated). The report also asserts two liquidity and access needs: (1) Renovation of main residence planned for circa £40,000 from the inheritance (due 2025-12-16, funded from inheritance); (2) Drawdown access to pension funds in retirement via Flexi Access Drawdown. The report states the client intends to spend circa £40,000 of the inheritance on renovations.
    File:     The Fact Find (P2, P14, P15) records current monthly net income of £2,590.00 comprising Anthony's employed income £890.00 net per month and DB Pension £1,700.00 net per month, plus Yvette's employment income £16,800 gross annually (derived from context as part of combined household income). The Fact Find records total monthly expenditure of £2,206.67 and disposable income of £383.33. Assets include £100,000 inheritance ("Incoming funds from sale of Dad's property"), £40,000 Chartered Savings Account, £20,000 MoneyBox Cash ISA, £20,000 Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA, and £10,000 Cash Emergency Fund. The Fact Find asks "Do you expect any changes in your expenditure in the foreseeable future?" with answer "No" (P2). The Fact Find does not record Yvette's planned reduction to 3 days per week. The Fact Find records retirement goal as "Wants to be fully retired by 60" and "Min income in retirement is £2,200 net per month". The Cashflow Current Situation (P13) shows Income Statement first year: Anthony DB Pension £22,000, Employment £0, Yvette Employment £16,800, Total Regular Income £38,800. Regular Expenditure first year totals £29,915. The cashflow projections (P3, P4, P7, P8) show year-by-year asset and income trajectories through 2074, with Yvette's employment ceasing in 2031 (age 65) in the baseline scenario. The Higher Inflation scenario (P3) shows liquid assets declining from £142,326 in 2025 to £116,424 in 2031. The Market Crash scenario (P8) shows "Returns overridden for Investments (excluding cash); Pensions for the following years: -30% in 2028". The Tony Predeceases scenario (P12) shows "Death" age 70 in 2040. Multiple scenarios show expenditure continuation at 80% on death of either spouse (P10, P11, P12).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts Yvette's intended retirement when client reaches age 60 (2030-12-16) with £16,800 gross annual impact, but cashflow projections show Yvette's employment income continuing to 2031 (age 65) in the baseline scenario, not 2030 (age 60); this is a 5-year discrepancy in timing.
      - Report asserts Yvette's reduction to 3 days per week from 2026-01-01 with £11,200 gross annual impact, but the Fact Find (P2) records answer "No" to "Do you expect any changes in your expenditure in the foreseeable future?", and does not record this income reduction anywhere in the initial fact-finding responses; Yvette's reduction appears only later in the report narrative (page 8) without corresponding update to the Fact Find.
      - Report asserts £40,000 renovation expense as a planned liquidity need funded from the £100,000 inheritance, but the Fact Find does not separately identify or record this £40,000 planned expenditure as a distinct future commitment; it is asserted in the report narrative without corroborating documentation of the client's commitment to this amount or timeline.
      - The Fact Find states current monthly combined income as £2,590.00 net (P2), but Income Statement in Cashflow (P13) shows total regular income of £38,800 annually (£3,233 monthly gross equivalent before tax/NI of £3,435), representing inconsistency in basis (net vs gross) and magnitude that affects affordability assessment.
    The report's assertion of five foreseeable changes and two liquidity needs is partially supported but reveals material inconsistencies. (1) Yvette's retirement timing: The report states Yvette plans to fully retire at client age 60 (2030), but the cashflow projections (P3, P4, P7) show Yvette's employment ceasing in 2031 (when she reaches age 65), not 2030. This is a 1-year lag that may reflect standard state pension age modelling rather than the stated client intention. The Fact Find does not record Yvette's age 60 retirement intention; only the general goal "Wants to be fully retired by 60" appears, which may refer to the client (Anthony) not Yvette. (2) Yvette's hours reduction: The report asserts Yvette "is intending on reducing her hours to 3 days per week from next year" (2026-01-01) with projected £11,200 gross annual impact. However, the Fact Find (P2) records "Do you expect any changes in your expenditure in the foreseeable future? No", with no corresponding record of income changes. This reduction appears in the report narrative (page 8) but was not captured in the initial fact-finding, suggesting either: (a) the client commitment was made after the Fact Find was completed, or (b) it was discussed but not formally recorded. Either way, there is no corroborating Fact Find entry. (3) Inheritance and renovations: The £100,000 inheritance is recorded in the Fact Find (P15) as "Incoming funds from sale of Dad's property" valued 06/08/2025. The report asserts "You intend to spend circa £40,000 of your inheritance on renovations" (P24), but the Fact Find does not list £40,000 renovation as a planned expense; neither is it recorded in the Expenditure section. The £40,000 is inferred from the report statement but not evidenced as a separate client commitment in the file. (4) Affordability basis: The Fact Find shows current net monthly income £2,590 and monthly expenditure £2,206.67 with disposable income £383.33 (P2). The Cashflow Income Statement (P13) shows gross regular income £38,800 (Anthony DB Pension £22,000 + Yvette Employment £16,800). The report's affordability narrative does not reconcile these two bases or explain how monthly disposable income of £383.33 supports the recommended £3,600 annual gross contribution (£300 monthly). The ambiguity in the extraction notes ("current earnings are sufficient to support recommended contribution level" without specifying current earnings) reflects this unresolved tension. (5) Emergency fund and liquid reserves: The Fact Find (P2) records Emergency Fund of £10,000 required and held, with no shortfall. Liquid assets total £190,000 (inheritance £100,000 + Chartered £40,000 + two ISAs £20,000 each + emergency fund £10,000). The cashflow projections show liquid assets remaining positive through the modelled period (P3, P4, P7, P8), suggesting affordability is maintained even under stress scenarios (higher inflation, market crash, spouse predeceases). However, the 2030-31 timing discrepancy on Yvette's retirement means the modelled income shock may not align with the stated client intention. (6) State Pension and future income: The report asserts both clients eligible for State Pension in 2037 with combined impact £23,946 gross annually. The cashflow assumes client retirement at age 67 (2037) per P12 timeline. The State Pension age assumption is not challenged in any scenario. (7) Pension IHT change: The report notes the 6 April 2027 IHT rule change but assigns zero financial impact, consistent with the absence of IHT planning in the file. The guards indicate: Expenditure changes already in the monthly budget (£2,206.67) must not be double-counted; the report does not appear to do so. The £40,000 renovation, if aspirational rather than committed, is still a need; however, it is asserted without documentary evidence of the client raising it in formal fact-finding. The timing of Yvette's hours reduction (recorded in narrative but not Fact Find) suggests either a communication lag or a late disclosure not formally captured.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 9 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.3, G6.4, G6.5, G6.6, G6.7, G6.8, G6.9). Rationale references 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but extraction identifies employment status as ambiguous: client retired from factory after 35 years, brief part-time driving work (£890/month net) has stopped. Current employment income appears to be zero. The basis for the £3,600 gross annual contribution is not stated in evidence. Rationale states inheritance spend plan ('You intend to spend circa £40,000 of your inheritance on renovations, but would like to look at investing the remaining £60,000') but no evidence document records the client's stated intention to spend £40k on renovations or to invest £60k. Charge comparison in rationale ('reduced platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%') is stated but the extraction identifies internal inconsistency: existing platform charge is 0.32% yet total recurring charge increases by 0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62%), suggesting total cost of switching is higher despite the platform charge reduction rationale. 18 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts fourteen distinct rationale statements across three recommendations (Pension switch, GIA, and ISA). The rationale includes: (1) risk misalignment (current funds risk 6 vs agreed risk 4); (2) platform charge reduction (0.32% to 0.21%); (3) fund design for risk level 4; (4) professional oversight; (5) pension contribution of £3,600 gross to utilise annual allowance; (6) Aviva Platform features (marked isClientSpecific false); (7) GIA for capital growth and flexibility; (8) GIA enabling CGT allowance utilisation; (9) Bed & ISA facility for tax efficiency; (10) inheritance spending plan (£40k renovations, £60k investment); (11) ISA as tax-efficient investment; (12) ISA tax minimisation and capital growth; (13) ISA flexibility and CGT exemption; (14) GIA and ISA transfer for tax efficiency. Rationale is linked to objectives OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3, OBJ4, OBJ5. The canonical model marks 13 of 14 rationale statements as client-specific (true), with one (Aviva Platform statement) marked false.
    File:     The evidence pack contains research reports, existing holdings data, risk profile documentation, and fee structures, but critically lacks: (1) a documented record of objectives and their prioritised ordering; (2) explicit written alternatives considered and reasons for rejection; (3) documented discussion notes linking rationale to specific stated client needs; (4) confirmation of the £3,600 contribution level and how current earnings support it (extraction flagged this as ambiguous — client is retired, brief part-time work has stopped, current earnings status unclear); (5) explicit client statement of intention to spend £40k on renovations and invest £60k; (6) written rationale linking the Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings (existing arrangement) to the client's prior needs, making the case for change transparent; (7) alternatives research comparing the recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II against other risk-level-4 options; (8) documented consideration and rejection of keeping existing funds, consolidating on existing platform, or other wrapper choices. P1 confirms the client signed that 'financial planning objectives are those identified and prioritised in this document' but does not list them. P7 shows an agreed risk level of 4 but records 'No additional notes were recorded' on how that agreement was reached. P10 shows the same: 'No additional notes were recorded' on Agreed risk level discussion. P14 (the suitability report itself) provides performance analysis showing recommended funds outperformed existing holdings' volatility but does not present written alternatives analysis.
    Discrepancies:
      - Rationale references 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but extraction identifies employment status as ambiguous: client retired from factory after 35 years, brief part-time driving work (£890/month net) has stopped. Current employment income appears to be zero. The basis for the £3,600 gross annual contribution is not stated in evidence.
      - Rationale states inheritance spend plan ('You intend to spend circa £40,000 of your inheritance on renovations, but would like to look at investing the remaining £60,000') but no evidence document records the client's stated intention to spend £40k on renovations or to invest £60k.
      - Charge comparison in rationale ('reduced platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%') is stated but the extraction identifies internal inconsistency: existing platform charge is 0.32% yet total recurring charge increases by 0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62%), suggesting total cost of switching is higher despite the platform charge reduction rationale.
      - Rationale claims pension contribution 'will utilise your annual allowance tax-efficiently' but no evidence confirms whether the client has unused annual allowance or whether £3,600 is the appropriate level for their circumstances.
      - Rationale references 'ongoing professional oversight and our investment monitoring process' but the fee structure (P12, P13) shows ongoing charge of 1% annually and initial charge of 3% (£4,794), yet no evidence links these fees to the specific oversight benefit or justifies their level against alternatives.
      - Existing holdings (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80%) are stated to have combined risk level 6, but no evidence shows these funds were originally selected or justified for the client's prior circumstances, making the 'change' rationale incomplete without understanding the prior suitability.
      - No evidence of alternatives considered: rationale does not reference other risk-level-4 funds researched, other platforms compared, other wrappers evaluated, or other advisers' recommendations obtained.
      - One of fourteen rationale statements (Aviva Platform genericism) is marked isClientSpecific false in the canonical model, yet appears in the rationale output, suggesting internal inconsistency about what counts as client-specific justification.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Documented objectives with stated priorities (P1 references objectives are in 'this document' but the objectives list itself is not in evidence; extraction notes ambiguity over whether OBJ6 is a constraint or objective), Written record of alternatives considered and reasons for rejection for each recommendation, Documented discussion notes recording how agreed risk level 4 was reached (P7, P10 both state 'No additional notes were recorded'), Confirmation of current employment income and how £3,600 annual contribution is supported, Documented client intention regarding inheritance (£40k renovation spend, £60k investment), Basis for selecting Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II over other risk-level-4 alternatives, Evidence of whole-of-market or panel-based comparison for product selection, Research comparing platform charges and features against alternatives, Justification for 1% ongoing fee and 3% initial fee relative to alternatives, Prior suitability record for existing Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings to establish why change is needed
    The requirement tests whether each recommendation carries client-specific rationale linked to stated objectives, supported by evidence of alternatives considered. The report asserts rationale but the comparison cannot be performed fully due to missing foundational evidence. Specifically: (1) Objectives are referenced (OBJ1-OBJ5 appear in linkedObjectiveIds) but the evidence pack does not contain the objectives themselves, their stated wording, or their prioritisation. The extraction notes ambiguity over OBJ6 classification. Without the actual objective text, rationale linking cannot be verified as specific to this client's stated needs. (2) The risk mismatch rationale (current level 6 vs agreed level 4) is concrete and client-specific in form, referring to the client's actual agreed profile. However, it rests on two prior steps: first, that the existing Vanguard holdings were suitable when selected (no evidence of that prior rationale), and second, that the agreed level 4 was reached through discussion (P7, P10 record no discussion notes). The rationale for change is therefore incomplete: it says what changed but not why the prior choice was made or why the change now suits. (3) The charge-reduction rationale ('reduced platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%') contradicts the derived figures: total ongoing charge increases from 0.54% to 0.62% (£63 annual increase on £78,750 fund value). The platform charge does fall but total cost rises. The rationale names only the platform component, not total cost. This is a material inconsistency: a recommendation citing 'reduced costs' via platform charge is weakened when total cost is demonstrably higher. The guard on bases applies here — the rationale compares platform alone while the derived figures show total-cost comparison — but this is precisely the kind of selective basis that undermines client-specific justification. (4) The contribution rationale ('£3,600 gross will utilise your annual allowance tax-efficiently') cannot be verified: no evidence shows the client has unused annual allowance, and the extraction flags that current earnings status is ambiguous (retired, no current income stated). Whether a £3,600 contribution is tax-efficient cannot be assessed without knowing earnings capacity and existing contributions. (5) The Bed & ISA and CGT-planning rationale is templated language (sentences 8, 13 are generic tax-efficiency talk not tied to this client's specific tax position or holdings). (6) No evidence of alternatives: the rationale does not state that other risk-level-4 funds were considered and rejected, other platforms were ruled out, or other advisers' views were sought. The research reports (P2, P3, P5, P7) are present but do not show shortlist comparisons or exclusion reasons in the evidence provided. (7) The inheritance plan rationale ('You intend to spend circa £40,000...investing the remaining £60,000') is specific in amount and purpose but is not evidenced in any document in the pack: no fact find, meeting note, or email records this statement. It appears only in the report's own rationale section. (8) The canonical model marks 13 rationale statements as client-specific (true) and one (Aviva Platform) as false. However, all fourteen appear in the rationale text output. This internal inconsistency suggests the extraction flagged boilerplate language but it remains embedded in the full rationale set. The vulnerability overlay: no indicators of vulnerability, support needs, or communication requirements are flagged in the evidence. The check therefore proceeds on standard grounds. Conclusion on comparisonPerformed: the comparison cannot be fully performed because the objectives themselves (which rationale must link to) are absent from the evidence, the discussion notes supporting the agreed risk level are absent (both P7 and P10 record no notes), and alternatives are not documented in the evidence pack. Where a key element needed for the comparison (the objectives and the discussion that produced them) is missing, the comparison cannot be said to have been performed, even though rationale text is present in the report.

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report justifies the Aviva Platform and Aviva Pension Portfolio (SIPP wrapper) on grounds of: (1) platform excellence with comprehensive investment choices suitable for the client's needs; (2) reduced platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%; (3) the recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II is specifically designed for risk level 4 investors and aligns with the client's risk tolerance; (4) ongoing professional oversight and investment monitoring; (5) tax-efficient £3,600 gross pension contribution; (6) Aviva's financial strength (A Stable by S&P, £407bn AUM); (7) choice of over 6,900 funds; (8) ISA flexibility; (9) online convenience tools. For investment strategy: the multi-asset fund provides diversification across asset classes, blend of active and passive investments keeping costs low, competitive charges, strong peer performance, and is part of the firm's CIP reviewed quarterly by investment committee with external research team oversight.
    File:     The evidence supports several but not all of these claims. Research documents confirm: (1) Aviva Platform offers comprehensive facilities and adviser access [P6, P7]; (2) platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21% is stated [P16]; (3) the recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II is indeed mapped to risk level 4 [P4, P14]; (4) the fund is part of a risk-controlled universe within the firm's CIP [P14]; (5) Aviva's financial strength and fund choice are documented in product research [P3, P7, P16]; (6) the fund provides multi-asset diversification [P4, P14]. However, critical evidence on charges shows discrepancy: the total annual recurring charge is stated as 0.54% for existing holdings but increases to 0.62% post-switch (platform 0.21% + fund costs 0.41%), meaning total charges rise by 0.08%, not fall. The report claims 'Lower Costs' as a switch rationale [extraction notes] but this is contradicted by the arithmetic. Additionally, the report asserts the recommended fund is cheaper and better performing than existing Vanguard holdings [P2 shows 5-year return: Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II 30.12% vs current strategy 45.28%], yet the report also states current funds 'have outperformed the recommended funds' [P2]. No document addresses whether the client's earnings are sufficient to support the £3,600 gross contribution, as the extraction notes flag an ambiguity about current employment income (stated as zero after part-time work ended, yet report claims 'current earnings are sufficient' without specifying amount).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'reduced platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%' but total recurring charge increases from 0.54% to 0.62% (an increase of 0.08%, or £63 annually on a £78,750 fund value)
      - Report asserts the switch delivers 'Lower Costs' and that charges are 'competitive', yet total annual charges rise by 0.08 percentage points post-switch
      - Report states recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II has 'performed well in comparison with their peers' and client's 'current funds have outperformed the recommended funds', but 5-year cumulative performance shows existing strategy at 45.28% vs recommended fund at 30.12% — a 15.16 percentage point gap
      - Report justifies £3,600 gross pension contribution on grounds of 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but extraction notes identify ambiguity: client appears to be fully retired (part-time work ended, employment income stated as zero), making the earnings claim unsupported by the case file
      - Report cites 'competitive charges' and 'blend of active and passive investments, which allows for some active management, whilst keeping costs low', yet fund cost data shows ongoing costs of 0.29% + transaction costs of 0.22% = 0.51% total fund costs, and combined with 0.21% platform charge = 0.72% total before advice fee, not demonstrably competitive or 'low cost' without comparison to alternatives
    The core issue is internal contradiction on costs and performance. The report's primary stated justification for switching is a 'reduced platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%' [cited in extraction], which is accurate as stated narrowly for platform alone. However, this is presented without adequate context: the evidence shows total recurring charges rising from 0.54% to 0.62% due to higher fund costs in the recommended solution (0.41% vs 0.22% previously). The extraction metadata notes this as an internal inconsistency: 'Switch charges table shows Impact of Recommended Alternative as +0.08% increase in charges, suggesting the new solution is more expensive despite the switch rationale citing Lower Costs.' The report's language ('reduced platform charge', 'lower costs', 'competitive charges') is narrowly true on platform fee alone but materially misleading when total cost of ownership increases. This violates the principle of clear, client-specific justification — the client is told costs reduce when they actually rise overall. Performance comparison is similarly problematic: the report asserts the fund 'has performed well in comparison with their peers' and 'The funds have performed well in comparison with their peers' [both generic statements from the investment strategy rationale], yet evidence in [P2] shows the existing strategy (45.28% over 5 years) substantially outperformed the recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II (30.12% over 5 years). The report also states 'Although your current funds have outperformed the recommended funds' [P2], acknowledging the underperformance but burying it after asserting peer competitiveness. The recommendation to contribute £3,600 gross is justified as utilizing 'your annual allowance tax-efficiently' but the case file contains an unresolved ambiguity about the client's current earnings. The extraction notes state: 'Client's employment status and earnings unclear: retired from factory role after 35 years, then briefly worked part-time driving (£890 net/month) which has since stopped. Current employment income appears to be zero, but report states in contributions section that current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level without specifying what those earnings are.' On provider and wrapper selection, the evidence does support the core claims: Aviva Platform is confirmed as comprehensive [P6, P7, P16], the SIPP wrapper is appropriate for a pensioner, and the risk level 4 mapping is correct [P4, P14]. However, the report does not document a comparative evaluation of alternatives. The report states 'I then compared each pension scheme against the most suitable alternative option from the following: [Option 1-5 listed]' [P12] but concludes with '[blank]' — the rationale for rejecting alternatives is not documented. The report relies on firm panel membership (Pension Portfolio - Choice is on TFAS panel [P17]) but does not explain why TFAS panel products are suitable or how they were selected from that panel. The guard states: 'Firm panel or CIP membership is a legitimate justification if the report says so; do not require a fresh whole-of-market search where a documented panel process exists.' The report does identify reliance on CIP and investment committee oversight [P14, extraction], which is a legitimate process-based justification, but does not explain why the client's specific circumstances warrant this particular recommendation from the available options within that process. Summary of core failures: (1) Platform charge reduction is presented without disclosing that total charges increase; (2) Performance comparison is presented generically ('competitive', 'well-performing') while evidence shows underperformance; (3) Contribution justification rests on unstated or absent earnings; (4) Alternative options are listed but rejection rationale is blank; (5) Justification is largely generic (diversification, risk alignment, simplicity, oversight) rather than deeply personalized to this client's unique circumstances.

  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report presents six alternatives to the recommended pension solution: (1) Leave existing pension untouched—rejected because current funds (risk level 6) do not align with client's agreed risk profile of 4, with portfolio drift risk; (2) Leave existing pension in place and switch investment funds—listed but no rejection reason provided; (3) Switch into another existing pension arrangement—listed but no rejection reason provided; (4) Switch into employer's pension arrangement—listed but no rejection reason provided; (5) Set up a new pension plan—listed but no rejection reason provided; (6) Stakeholder pension—rejected because recommended solution is more cost-effective and stakeholder pensions lack flexible access and offer limited fund ranges. The report also considers retaining Moneybox Cash ISA, rejected in favour of recommended ISA funds offering greater growth potential (4.72% over 5 years vs circa 4.5% variable rate). For the ISA recommendation, the report states the solution is more cost-effective than a stakeholder pension.
    File:     Research documents show five pension alternatives were assessed: (1) Leave existing pension untouched—rejected due to risk level 6 not matching agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4; (2) Leave in place and switch funds—no rejection reason documented; (3) Switch into another existing arrangement—no rejection reason documented; (4) Switch into employer's scheme—no rejection reason documented; (5) Set up new pension plan—no rejection reason documented. The research report on Moneybox Cash ISA states: 'While Moneybox offers circa 4.5% interest, this is variable and could go up or down at any time. Recommended ISA funds have provided an average performance of 4.72% over a 5-year period, offering potential for greater growth.' A data comparison table [P10] shows the recommended Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II (5-year cumulative return 36.21%) against three alternatives: CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc (31.28%), HSBC Global Strategy Conservative Portfolio (25.90%), and Vanguard LifeStrategy 40% Equity (21.49%), all with risk rating 4. However, no explicit rejection reasons are provided for why these specific alternatives were rejected in the suitability report itself.
    Discrepancies:
      - Five pension alternatives listed in the report lack any stated rejection reasons (Options 2, 3, 4, 5), whereas the research documents show these options were considered but reasons for rejection are absent from both the report and the research files
      - The report claims stakeholder pension was rejected for being less cost-effective, but provides no cost comparison figures or evidence of how the recommended solution's cost was measured against stakeholder pension maxima (1.5% for 10 years, then 1%)
      - The report states the ISA recommendation offers '4.72% over a 5-year period' but the research passage quotes 'average performance' without specifying the measurement basis (whether this is net of charges, gross, or a projected figure)
      - A ranked comparison of four pension funds (Aviva Multi-asset Plus II returning 36.21% over 5 years vs alternatives at 31.28%, 25.90%, 21.49%) appears in the research [P10] but is not referenced or discussed in the suitability report as justification for fund selection

    The report presents six pension alternatives but provides documented rejection reasons for only two: the do-nothing option (risk misalignment with agreed profile 4 vs existing risk level 6—client-specific and linked to objective OBJ1) and the stakeholder pension (cost and flexibility grounds). Four alternatives (Options 2–5) are named in the report but have no accompanying rejection rationale in either the suitability report or the underlying research files.   [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states one existing arrangement (Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd - Pension Portfolio, SIPP, current value £79,092.32) with adviceAction 'RetainAndSwitchFunds'. Six rationales are provided: (1) Platform quality ('excellent platform with comprehensive investment choices'); (2) Risk misalignment (current funds risk level 6 vs agreed profile 4); (3) Lower costs (platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21%); (4) Risk alignment (new fund designed for risk level 4); (5) Professional oversight (ongoing monitoring); (6) Tax efficiency (£3,600 gross contribution). All rationales are marked isClientSpecific=true and link to stated objectives (OBJ1, OBJ4, OBJ5).
    File:     The file contains multiple corroborating documents. Policy research [P1-P5] confirms the Aviva existing arrangement exists with fund value £79,092.32 and transfer value £79,092.32. Pension switch report [P6, P21] shows platform charge of 0.32% for existing pension. Client report [P10] shows opening value £59,017.42 as at 24/11/2022 growing to closing value £79,092.32, with 34.02% cumulative return. Transaction history [P11-P15] confirms ongoing Aviva charges. Personal Pension Plan research [P20] identifies the recommended fund as 'Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II' rated as risk level 4 with 0.41% weighted AMC. Pension projection [P7] confirms current Aviva pension value. However, the pension switch report [P6, P21] shows the comparison as 'Existing pension: Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited - Current Aviva, Platform: None' versus 'New solution: Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited - Pension Portfolio - Choice, Platform: Aviva Platform'. The switch report also shows ongoing contributions as 'None' in the existing pension illustration [P21], contradicting the report's rationale that contributions of £3,600 gross will be made.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts 'reduced platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%' but pension switch report [P6, P21] shows existing platform charge 0.32% and new platform charge 0.21%, yet the 'Impact of Recommended Alternative' shows a +0.08% increase in total annual recurring charges (0.54% to 0.62%), meaning total costs rise despite the platform charge reduction.
      - Report cites 'Lower Costs' as a rationale but derived figures show the new solution costs £63 more annually (0.08% × £78,750 fund value), contradicting the claimed cost benefit.
      - Report states rationale includes £3,600 gross contribution to utilize annual allowance 'tax-efficiently and boost retirement provision', but pension switch report [P21] specifies 'Ongoing contributions: None' in the existing pension illustration details, creating ambiguity about whether contributions will actually be made.
      - Report claims risk level of current funds is 6, but evidence [P17] shows only one line item '12.34 10 Year Volatility **[2]** **6** Balanced Growth [4]' with unclear mapping between the '6' volatility marker and the actual current fund holdings.
      - The existing arrangement shows current holdings as Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% (35.02%) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% (64.95%) [P6, P21], but the recommended fund is identified as 'Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II' [P20]. The report does not clearly state whether this is a fund switch or whether the Vanguard holdings are being replaced, though 'RetainAndSwitchFunds' action code implies a switch of funds within the retained platform arrangement.
      - Report rationale states 'The recommended new fund is specifically designed for risk level 4 investors' but evidence [P20] shows the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II has no Diamond Rating ('N/A' in both individual and family fields), providing no independent corroboration of its design specifications or suitability for the risk profile.
    The report provides a stated recommended outcome ('RetainAndSwitchFunds') with six discrete rationales, all marked as client-specific. However, material contradictions undermine the stated basis for the recommendation. First, the primary cost rationale is factually contradicted: the report claims 'reduced platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%' as a benefit, and this platform charge reduction is correct [P6, P21]. However, the derived charge comparison shows total annual recurring charges increase from 0.54% to 0.62% (a +0.08% impact, equivalent to £63 additional annual cost on the £78,750 fund value). This increase occurs because fund charges are higher in the new solution. The rationale 'Lower Costs' is therefore unsupported by the evidence and contradicted by the pension switch report's own comparison figures. Second, the contribution rationale creates ambiguity: the report states a £3,600 gross contribution will 'utilise your annual allowance tax-efficiently' but the pension switch report's illustration of the existing pension shows 'Ongoing contributions: None' [P21], and the extraction flagged that current employment income is unclear (client appears fully retired, with past part-time driving income no longer active). The source and certainty of the £3,600 contribution is therefore not established. Third, the risk alignment rationale is stated but not fully corroborated: the current funds' combined risk level is claimed as 6, but evidence of this calculation is sparse (one research document line item [P17] is ambiguous). The new fund's suitability is asserted as 'specifically designed for risk level 4 investors' but the research fund data shows the Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II with no Diamond Rating, providing no independent validation. Fourth, the arrangement action 'RetainAndSwitchFunds' is unclear in its implementation: the report does not explicitly state whether the Aviva platform is being retained (with funds switched within it) or whether 'retain' refers to staying with Aviva as provider. The Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings are shown being replaced by Aviva Multi-Asset Plus, but whether this is a full replacement or partial switch is not stated. The guards permit 'No action required' if a reason is given; this is not that case—the action is 'switch funds', which is a material recommendation requiring clear implementation details. The rationales provided are client-specific in language (naming the client's risk profile and objectives) but rely on cost and risk arguments that are either contradicted by the evidence or rest on unstated assumptions about funding and current employment. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to materially affect this assessment: no vulnerability drivers or support needs identified in the evidence would change the requirement to show that costs reduce (when they increase) or that contributions can be funded (when income is unclear).

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts three recommendations: (1) Switch pension funds from Vanguard LifeStrategy to Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II, reducing platform charges from 0.32% to 0.21%, with intended outcome to 'Align investments with agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile, reduce costs, and utilise annual allowance tax-efficiently'; (2) Invest £57,120 in Aviva Platform Investment Portfolio using CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc, with intended outcome to 'Invest inheritance tax-efficiently with capital growth potential supporting desired retirement income while maintaining flexibility'; (3) Transfer £20,000 from Cash ISA to Stocks & Shares ISA using CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc, with intended outcome to 'Invest inheritance tax-efficiently with capital growth potential supporting desired retirement income while maintaining ISA tax-free status and flexibility'. All three recommendations are marked as 'Recommended', with adviceStatus 'Recommended' and willAdviserImplement 'true', and responsibleParty 'Adviser'. Implementation timescale is stated as '10-15 working days', cancellation rights as '30 days', and next steps include provision of Client Agreement, Expression of Wish form, and cancellation period notification.
    File:     The case file contains product key features documents (Aviva ISA Key Features [P1-P3], Aviva Pension Key Features [P4, P7-P8], Aviva GIA Illustration [P5-P6]), a Risk Profile Report [P9, P13], a Cashflow analysis [P10-P11], a Fact Find [P12], and a Client Agreement to Proceed [P14]. The Client Agreement [P14] states that ongoing advice and review services will be provided 'until either you, or we, consider this service to be no longer appropriate for your needs' and confirms fees are set out in the suitability report. The Aviva GIA Illustration [P5-P6] shows initial adviser charge of £4,794.37 and ongoing adviser charge of 1.00% per annum. The Aviva Pension Key Features [P4] detail how charges are managed, including disinvestment procedures when cash is insufficient. The Risk Profile Report [P9, P13] contains blank sections for investment target notes and capacity for loss notes. No document in the file explicitly corroborates or contradicts the specific recommendations, their advice status, implementation responsibility, or intended outcomes.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states switch will reduce platform charges from 0.32% to 0.21% (an 0.11% reduction), but also states impact as +0.08% increase in total recurring charge, creating an apparent contradiction regarding cost benefit of the recommendation
      - Report asserts pension switch rationale includes 'Lower Costs' but the derived figures show total ongoing charge would increase from 0.54% to 0.62% (0.08% increase), contradicting the cost reduction claim
      - Recommendation 1 intended outcome cites 'reduce costs' but the charge comparison shows costs increase by £63 annually (0.08% of £78,750 fund value)
      - The report does not state whether implementation steps (such as disinvestment procedures detailed in Aviva Key Features [P4]) will be handled by adviser or provider, creating ambiguity about implementation responsibility despite stating 'willAdviserImplement true'
      - Existing arrangements show Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings (60% at risk level 5, 80% at risk level 7, combined weighted approximately risk 6-7) but recommended fund is Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II at risk level 4, representing a material downward shift; the switch rationale does not explain this change in risk profile alignment
    The requirement examines whether the report clearly states (a) that a change is recommended, (b) who will implement it, and (c) the intended outcome. On these core elements, the report largely complies: all three recommendations are explicitly marked 'Recommended', implementation responsibility is assigned to 'Adviser' (willAdviserImplement = true), and intended outcomes are stated for each. Implementation timescale (10-15 working days) and cancellation rights (30 days) are also disclosed. However, material inconsistencies undermine the suitability of the stated recommendations themselves: (1) The pension switch recommendation asserts that costs will be reduced, but the charge comparison shows platform charge falling from 0.32% to 0.21% (saving 0.11%) is outweighed by the fund charge impact, resulting in total recurring charge rising from 0.54% to 0.62% (cost increase of 0.08%), plus an additional 1.00% ongoing adviser charge. This means the stated intended outcome 'reduce costs' directly contradicts the arithmetic of the report's own charge tables. The derived figures confirm this: £63.00 at 0.08% across a £78,750 fund represents a net cost increase. (2) The recommendation to switch from Vanguard LifeStrategy (combined risk approximately 6-7) to Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II (risk 4) represents a significant de-risking, yet the switch rationale names only 'align investments with agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile' without acknowledging this represents a material change from existing holdings. The client's agreed risk level is 4, making the new fund aligned to profile; however, the existing holdings are materially misaligned (at risk 6-7 when client agreed 4), and the report does not explain why the existing Vanguard holdings were not flagged as unsuitable at the outset. (3) The guard 'an implementation step handled by the provider rather than the adviser is still a stated step' applies here: the Aviva Pension Key Features [P4] describe automatic disinvestment procedures for charge payment, but these are provider functions, not adviser functions. The report's assertion that the adviser will implement (willAdviserImplement = true) does not specify the division of labour. The Client Agreement [P14] states fees are 'set out in the suitability report', confirming adviser implementation is stated; however, technical implementation (fund switches, account setup) typically involves provider systems. The report does not clarify this division. The stated intended outcomes are present and client-specific for recommendations 2 and 3 (linking to inheritance, retirement income, flexibility, tax efficiency), though generic in linking to 'capital growth potential' without quantification. For recommendation 1, the intended outcome is similarly generic ('align investments', 'reduce costs', 'tax efficiency') and contradicts the charge data. No document in the file corroborates or contradicts the core statements (that change is recommended, adviser implements, outcomes are as stated), but the cost contradiction is internal to the report's own assertions and represents a material flaw in the suitability reasoning, not in the statement of recommendation status or responsibility.
