  [G6.7] Recommendations that present multiple possible outcomes without stating which is recommended and why
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report presents three discrete recommendations: (1) Switch pension from Vanguard LifeStrategy mix to Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II on TFAS agency with reduced platform charges (0.32% to 0.21%), (2) Invest £57,120 in Aviva GIA using CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc at 100%, (3) Transfer £20,000 from Cash ISA to Stocks & Shares ISA using CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc at 100%. Each recommendation is marked 'Recommended' in adviceStatus. The pension switch rationale cites risk alignment (current level 6 vs agreed level 4), lower costs (0.32% to 0.21%), fund suitability for risk level 4, professional oversight, and tax efficiency of £3,600 contribution. The GIA and ISA recommendations cite tax efficiency, CGT allowance planning, and Bed & ISA facility. No alternative options are presented for any recommendation; each states a single course of action with supporting rationale.
    File:     The evidence contains: client notes indicating ATR 4 and 'only ATR 4' with expected growth 4.5%, already achieved on Moneybox ISA [P1]; notes showing client retired with DB income but uncertain whether part-time work continues [P2]; risk profile report [P7] recording 'No additional notes were recorded' for both investment target and agreed risk level discussion; policy research table [P9] listing four client objectives including 'Review his existing Aviva Platform pension for suitability' and 'wants to invest some of it for growth and tax-efficiency'; pension switching research [P10] documenting agreed risk level 4 as Cautious Balanced and showing CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C selected; GIA research [P6] confirming CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc as 100% allocation at risk level 4. No document in the evidence presents alternative options considered and rejected for any of the three recommendations. No file note resolves an open option. Client Agreement [P4] describes ongoing services but does not address recommendation options. The evidence does not contain comparative research showing why Aviva Platform was chosen over alternatives, why the Multi-Asset Plus Fund II was selected over other level-4 funds, or why GIA was chosen over other wrapper structures for the £57,120.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21% but derived figures show total recurring charge increases from 0.54% to 0.62% (0.08% increase after fund charges included), contradicting 'Lower Costs' rationale
      - Report rationale claims 'lower costs' for pension switch but charge comparison shows +0.08% annual recurring charge impact, suggesting costs rise not fall
      - Extraction metadata reports internal inconsistency: platform charge stated as both 0.32% and 0.54%, with 0.54% being current 'Annual Recurring Charge' (platform + fund charges), making the 0.21% comparison on basis unclear
      - Report cites £3,600 gross contribution as utilizing annual allowance tax-efficiently, but extraction notes ambiguity about current earnings being zero or 'sufficient to support' this level without stating amount
      - Existing pension fund recommendation references 'Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II' but existing arrangements show Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings; unclear whether this is rename or switch to different fund
      - Report presents Multi-Asset Plus Fund II recommendation with no evidence in file that alternatives (other level-4 funds) were researched or considered
    The requirement examines whether recommendations presenting multiple possible outcomes state which is recommended and why. The report presents three recommendations, each marked 'Recommended' with a single advised course of action stated for each. No unresolved options are left open in the report itself; each recommendation names one path forward. However, three material concerns arise: (1) CHARGE CONTRADICTION: The report's core rationale for the pension switch is 'reduced platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%' [cited as direct quote in rationale], yet the derived figures from the report's own extracted values show the annual recurring charge actually increases by 0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62% including fund charges). The extraction's internal inconsistency note flags that 0.54% appears to be 0.32% platform plus 0.22% weighted fund charges, suggesting the 0.21% new platform charge excludes fund charges for the Multi-Asset Plus Fund, making the comparison asymmetrical (old total charge vs new platform-only charge). This contradiction between the stated rationale and the calculated outcome is not an unresolved option but a factual error in the rationale itself. (2) MISSING ALTERNATIVES: The requirement asks whether alternatives are considered and reasons for rejection explained. The file contains no evidence that alternative platforms, alternative level-4 funds, or alternative wrappers for the £57,120 were researched or compared. The GIA research report [P6] and pension switching research [P10] document the selected investment (CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C) and agreed risk level, but do not record a comparison process or rejection of named alternatives. The risk profile report [P7] records 'No additional notes were recorded' for the investment target and agreed risk level discussion, indicating the file lacks documentation of the deliberation that should support alternative rejection. (3) AMBIGUOUS INPUTS TO RECOMMENDATION: The contribution recommendation of £3,600 gross is justified as 'will utilise your annual allowance tax-efficiently', but the extraction flags ambiguity about current earnings. Client notes [P1] state employment income has stopped (quit before workplace scheme enrollment, 'none' for P60/P45), yet the report's contributions section states 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' without specifying what those earnings are. This creates uncertainty about whether the £3,600 recommendation is fully justified by the client's actual financial position. The report does state each recommendation as advised and does not leave options unresolved; the findings concern the justification offered and the absence of documented alternatives, not unresolved branching. Under the decision logic: evidence is incomplete (no alternatives documented), rationale contains a contradiction (stated 'lower costs' vs calculated cost increase), and the basis of key inputs is ambiguous (earnings supporting contribution, charge comparison basis). The lack of documented alternatives research, combined with the charge contradiction in the rationale, means the recommendation does not fully meet the standard of being 'supported by and consistent with the evidence provided'.

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report states three recommendations with action types: Switch, NewPlan, NewPlan. The responsible party for all three is listed as 'Adviser'. Implementation timescale is stated as '10-15 working days' for access to funds. Specific next steps are outlined: (1) Client provided with Client Agreement and Important Information documents; (2) Access timescale of 10-15 working days disclosed; (3) Client's comfort with this timescale noted given significant liquid cash availability; (4) Expression of Wish form posted out for beneficiary designation; (5) Cancellation notice to be sent once plan is set up, with 30-day review period. The report states 'We are unable to see at present whether you have a beneficiary set up on your Aviva plan; therefore, I have posted out an Expression of Wish form for you to sign and return to us' and 'Once your plan is set up you will be sent a cancellation notice. You have 30 days to review and consider your options.'
    File:     The Aviva Pension Illustration [P1] confirms the recommended fund is 'Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II' with 100% investment allocation. The Aviva Pension Key Features [P2] state: 'Unless you instruct us otherwise, we'll invest any additional or regular payments in line with the model portfolio within your Pension Portfolio' and 'We'll hold your cash upon receipt in a client money bank account in line with the Financial Conduct Authority client money rules.' The Key Features [P3] note that 'We also recommend you get ongoing advice from your financial adviser to help you on a continuing basis after the transfer.' The Key Features [P4] explain disinvestment processes and state: 'In certain circumstances, we may need to delay payments, transfers and switching out of funds. This could, for example, be as a result of poor market conditions or where it would lead to unfair treatment of other investors. The delay may be up to one month for most funds.' The supporting documents (cashflow scenarios [P13], [P14], [P15], [P16]) confirm planning assumptions but do not specify implementation timescales or responsibilities for the specific recommendations.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report specifies '10-15 working days' as the timescale for accessing funds should the client require it in future, but this is framed as an access/redemption timescale, not an implementation timescale for setting up the new plans.
      - The report names the responsible party as 'Adviser' for all three recommendations, but does not name the specific adviser, adviser firm, or indicate whether the firm or an individual will perform the action.
      - The report states an Expression of Wish form has been 'posted out' but does not specify a deadline for the client to return it or when the plan setup is contingent on receipt.
      - The report references a 'cancellation notice' to be sent 'once your plan is set up' but does not specify when plan setup is expected to be complete relative to recommendation acceptance.
      - The Aviva Key Features [P4] note potential delays of 'up to one month for most funds' in certain circumstances, which extends beyond the '10-15 working days' stated in the report.
    The report provides a generic implementation timescale ('10-15 working days') that refers to future access to funds, not to the setup and implementation of the recommendations themselves. This is a material gap because the client needs to know when the switch will occur and the new plans will be active. The responsible party is named as 'Adviser' which satisfies the naming requirement under the guard ('Naming the adviser firm rather than an individual satisfies by whom'), but the report does not clarify which adviser or adviser firm will execute the actions. The report states specific next steps (Expression of Wish form, cancellation notice receipt period) but does not integrate these into a clear timeline showing what happens when. The Aviva Key Features [P4] introduce a potential complication: delays of up to one month may occur in certain circumstances, which the suitability report does not acknowledge or reconcile with the stated '10-15 working days' timescale. The guard specifies that 'Timing is material where the client is out of the market or working to a deadline' and also that 'A timescale quoted as a provider service standard is a stated timescale.' The 10-15 working days appears to be a provider service standard for redemptions, not a commitment to implementation timing. For a recommendation involving a pension switch and new plan setup, the client needs to know when the adviser will initiate the action, not merely when redemptions would be processed if they later request access. The report does not distinguish between implementation timing and future access timing.

  [G6.9] Ancillary recommendations — beneficiary nomination, Power of Attorney, will review — are stated with an owner and are consistent with what the file records
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) 'We are unable to see at present whether you have a beneficiary set up on your Aviva plan; therefore, I have posted out an Expression of Wish form for you to sign and return to us.' [page 20]. This is a client-specific action item assigning responsibility to the adviser to send the form. The report further extracts that the client has: hasWill = true and isPowerOfAttorneyGranted = true. The implementation nextSteps array names responsibility ('Adviser') for sending documents and setting up the plan.
    File:     The case file evidences: (1) From Fact Find [P1], the client signed a declaration confirming receipt of Client Agreement and Financial Planner's Business Card, and confirmed authorization for quotations and recommendations. (2) From Fact Find [P8], the Advice Areas section shows 'Estate Planning' with no entry in the row (blank/unchecked). (3) From Policy Research [P11], the adviser is listed as 'Tom Redmond' and the fact find is marked 'Fully Complete' with 'Partner Details' marked 'Y', but no specific will or Power of Attorney status is recorded in the searchable text. (4) The file contains no document recording whether a will exists, is up to date, or whether a Power of Attorney has been granted. (5) The file contains no independent verification of the client's beneficiary designation status on the Aviva plan prior to the Expression of Wish form being posted.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts client hasWill = true; file records no will document, will status, or estate planning detail in any searchable passage.
      - Report asserts client isPowerOfAttorneyGranted = true; file records no Power of Attorney document, grant, or confirmation in any searchable passage.
      - Report states 'We are unable to see at present whether you have a beneficiary set up on your Aviva plan' suggesting prior checking; file contains no evidence of adviser querying Aviva or receiving a response about beneficiary status before posting the Expression of Wish form.
      - Fact Find marks 'Estate Planning' section as blank with no entry, while report extracts positive estate planning status (will exists, PoA granted).
    The requirement asks whether ancillary recommendations — beneficiary nomination, Power of Attorney, will review — are stated with an owner and consistent with what the file records. The suitability report makes two kinds of assertion: (1) factual claims about the client's existing estate planning (hasWill = true, isPowerOfAttorneyGranted = true), and (2) a specific recommended action (Expression of Wish form to be posted out by the adviser). The guard 'An adviser recommending the client set up a Power of Attorney is a different claim from the client already having one' applies: the report could be asserting the client already has a PoA (factual), or recommending one be set up (advisory). The extraction reads hasWill and isPowerOfAttorneyGranted as true, placing both in the 'already has' category. However, the file provides no corroborating evidence: (a) no will document is present; (b) no Power of Attorney deed, certificate or record is present; (c) the Fact Find estate planning section is blank, not completed; (d) the Fact Find partner/dependent details are recorded but estate planning specifics are not searchable in the passages provided. The Expression of Wish recommendation itself is clearly stated and owner-assigned (adviser to post form), and this action is appropriate given the stated uncertainty about beneficiary status. However, the file's silence on whether a will and PoA actually exist, combined with the blank estate planning section in the fact find, creates a discrepancy between the report's extraction (which records positive estate planning status) and the file's content (which records none). This is not an ambiguity in how to read the report; it is the report asserting estate planning facts unsupported by any document the file contains. The Expression of Wish action itself is client-specific and owner-assigned (adviser), meeting that limb. The factual assertions about existing arrangements do not meet the consistency test: they are asserted but not evidenced.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). Report states platform charge for pension as 0.21% and investment management charge as 0.41%, totalling 0.62% product charges. However, P1's pension switch comparison table shows current annual recurring charge of 0.54% (stated as 0.32% platform plus 0.22% weighted fund charge) and impact of recommended alternative as +0.08%, which would result in 0.62% total. This reconciles, but the breakdown differs: the report presents 0.21% platform + 0.41% investment management, whereas the comparison table appears to show 0.32% platform + 0.22% fund management for existing, implying the new arrangement may be 0.21% platform + 0.41% fund management (totalling 0.62%). The report claims the new pension solution is justified despite being '0.08% more expensive' based on 'improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight,' but does not quantify the specific benefits of these improvements or compare projected outcomes between the existing and recommended funds. The report states total cost including adviser fee is 'approximately 1.62% annually compared to your current arrangement of 0.54%' — a difference of 1.08 percentage points. This calculation: 0.54% (existing) + 1.00% (adviser fee) = 1.54%, not 1.62%. The discrepancy of 0.08% is not explained. 18 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report discloses: Platform charge (Pension) 0.21%, Investment management (Pension) 0.41%, Platform charge (ISA) 0.21%, Investment management (ISA) 0.29%, Platform charge (GIA) 0.21%, Investment management (GIA) 0.29%. Transaction costs are explicitly excluded with the statement: 'The charges quoted in this report exclude the transaction costs of the underlying funds, as these are variable and subject to change based on factors such as market conditions and trading activity. Despite their variability, these costs are already accounted for in the fund's performance figures, which are presented net of all charges.' The report discloses an initial adviser charge and ongoing adviser charge of 1% per annum. For the pension specifically, the report states the recommended fund strategy is 0.08% more expensive than the current plan (£63 in monetary terms based on current fund value of £78,750), but justifies this by stating 'This small increase in charges is justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight.' Including the adviser fee, the total cost is stated as approximately 1.62% annually compared to the current arrangement of 0.54%.
    File:     The illustrations and factsheets corroborate most disclosed charges. P21 (Aviva Pension Illustration) shows fund charge 0.41% for Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II, platform charge 0.21%, and adviser charges of initial £4,794.37 and ongoing 1.00% per annum. P20 (Aviva GIA Illustration) shows CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc charge 0.29%, platform charge 0.21%, and identical adviser charges. P19 (Aviva ISA Illustration) shows similar platform charge structure. P1 confirms transaction costs exclusion and states they are reflected in net performance figures. P6 (Pension projection) shows weighted average fund charges of 0.22% for existing Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings (0.22% each). The pension switch comparison table in P1 shows current annual recurring charge 0.54% (which equals 0.32% platform + 0.22% fund charges) and impact of recommended alternative +0.08%, resulting in new total of 0.62% before adviser fees. P10 confirms Aviva platform charge at 0.21% flat rate. Transaction costs are recognized in P8 as distinct from ongoing charges but not separately quantified.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states platform charge for pension as 0.21% and investment management charge as 0.41%, totalling 0.62% product charges. However, P1's pension switch comparison table shows current annual recurring charge of 0.54% (stated as 0.32% platform plus 0.22% weighted fund charge) and impact of recommended alternative as +0.08%, which would result in 0.62% total. This reconciles, but the breakdown differs: the report presents 0.21% platform + 0.41% investment management, whereas the comparison table appears to show 0.32% platform + 0.22% fund management for existing, implying the new arrangement may be 0.21% platform + 0.41% fund management (totalling 0.62%).
      - The report claims the new pension solution is justified despite being '0.08% more expensive' based on 'improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight,' but does not quantify the specific benefits of these improvements or compare projected outcomes between the existing and recommended funds.
      - The report states total cost including adviser fee is 'approximately 1.62% annually compared to your current arrangement of 0.54%' — a difference of 1.08 percentage points. This calculation: 0.54% (existing) + 1.00% (adviser fee) = 1.54%, not 1.62%. The discrepancy of 0.08% is not explained.
      - The extracted model shows investment management charge for GIA is inferred from the fund factsheet (0.29%) rather than explicitly stated in the report's charges section for that wrapper.
    The report discloses all material cost layers: platform, investment management, initial advice fee, and ongoing adviser fee. Transaction costs are explicitly excluded with a stated reason (variability) and are asserted to be reflected in net performance figures. The exclusion is a disclosure, not an omission. Charge figures in the suitability report and supporting illustrations are internally consistent: pension platform 0.21%, pension investment management 0.41%, GIA and ISA platform 0.21%, GIA and ISA investment management 0.29%. These match the Aviva Pension Illustration (P21), GIA Illustration (P20), and ISA Illustration (P19). The adviser fee of 1% per annum is disclosed and matches the client agreement reflected in the illustrations. The pension switch comparison (P1) reconciles: existing 0.54% (0.32% platform + 0.22% weighted fund charge for Vanguard holdings) versus recommended 0.62% (0.21% platform + 0.41% fund charge for Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II), a difference of +0.08% or £63 annually. However, the report's articulation of benefits is weak. The justification for the cost increase ('improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight') is stated qualitatively but not quantified. The client's existing holdings (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80%) were already multi-asset solutions; the specific performance or diversification benefits of switching to Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II are not detailed or evidenced with comparative fund metrics. The statement that benefits are 'supported by and consistent with evidence' cannot be verified because the report does not articulate what specific benefits justify the 0.08% increase. P1 does present a performance analysis chart comparing existing funds (A) against recommended funds (C Pension / D - ISA & GIA) over 5 years and against a Risk Level 4 benchmark (B), but the chart itself is not rendered in the passage, only described. The illustrations (P21, P20, P19) show projected values and cumulative charge impact but do not compare these between existing and recommended options. The 1.62% total cost figure appears to contain an arithmetic issue. If existing 0.54% + adviser fee 1.00% = 1.54%, the stated 1.62% is 0.08 percentage points higher than can be derived from these components. This may reflect rounding or an unstated adjustment, but it is not explained. The report meets the disclosure requirement (all material cost layers are named) but falls short on the consistency and clarity of the value proposition. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability markers in the evidence affect this assessment. The client's circumstances (retired, seeking simplicity) are reflected in fund selection (multi-asset, managed, consolidated) but do not materially change the analysis of cost disclosure or justification.

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   Initial fee: 3% of the amount invested, calculated as £4,794 based on total investment values (£79,092 pension transfer + £57,120 GIA + £20,000 ISA + £3,600 pension contribution). Ongoing fee: 1% per annum across all Aviva Platform investments, estimated as approximately £1,558 for the upcoming year based on total platform value of £155,812 after initial fee deduction. Services provided for initial fee: researching, recommending and arranging investments. Services provided for ongoing fee: reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss, reviewing ongoing suitability of investment portfolio, market updates and legislative changes, access to professional advice team ad-hoc, and access to full holistic financial planning.
    File:     Client Agreement states: Initial Service charge of 3% of the value of the funds advised on with a minimum of £1,350 fee, and based on the value of funds the fee will be £4,794. Ongoing Services charge of 1% annual review management charge (subject to a minimum fee of £100), automatically taken from funds invested, fluctuating with investment value. IMPORT_2.md (TFAS fee schedule) corroborates 3% initial charge with £1,350 minimum for investments, and 1% ongoing charge with £100 minimum. Aviva platform illustrations (GIA, ISA, Pension) confirm adviser charges are taken: GIA illustration shows £4,794.37 initial and 1.00% ongoing; Pension illustration references adviser charges; ISA illustration references adviser charges. All illustrations show charges are deducted from cash account.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states ongoing fee estimated at £1,558 based on total platform value of £155,812; this implies 0.9964% (£1,558÷£155,812), not exactly 1%
      - Report calculates initial fee on sum of £79,092 + £57,120 + £20,000 + £3,600 = £159,812; 3% of this is £4,794.36, matching stated £4,794, but the basis components (£79,092 pension transfer and £57,120 GIA) are not corroborated in evidence at these exact figures
      - Client Agreement states minimum fee of £1,350 for initial service; report's calculation of £4,794 exceeds this and is compliant, but no evidence explains why all four investment components (pension, GIA, ISA, contribution) are bundled into a single 3% calculation rather than treated separately under different fee structures
      - Report states £1,558 ongoing fee is 'approximately' and based on platform value after initial fee deduction; Client Agreement and fee schedule state 1% with £100 minimum but do not specify calculation basis or whether the £100 minimum applies per portfolio or aggregate
    The report's stated fees of 3% initial and 1% ongoing are explicitly corroborated in the Client Agreement To Proceed [P19] and in TFAS fee schedule [P1, P20]. The percentage rates match without discrepancy. The initial fee amount of £4,794 is corroborated in the Client Agreement [P19] which states 'Based on the value of your funds, our fee will be £4,794.' The ongoing fee rate of 1% is corroborated in Client Agreement [P19] as 'an ongoing annual review management charge of 1%'. However, three material points require clarification: (1) The report's calculation of initial fee on combined investment values (£79,092 + £57,120 + £20,000 + £3,600 = £159,812 × 3% = £4,794) bundles four distinct investment vehicles into one fee calculation. The Client Agreement states the fee as 'Based on the value of your funds' without specifying whether funds across different wrappers (pension, GIA, ISA, contributions) should be aggregated or treated separately. The TFAS fee schedule [P1] describes separate advice services for pension contributions (30% of first year's payments with £250/month minimum) and lump sum investments (3% with £1,350 minimum), but does not clearly state whether pension transfers, ongoing pension contributions, GIA investments, ISA investments and new contributions are all subject to a unified 3% fee or different structures. (2) The ongoing fee of £1,558 is stated as 'approximately' and based on 'total platform value of £155,812 after initial fee deduction.' This figure does not appear in any evidence document. The Client Agreement [P19] states 1% 'which is automatically taken from the funds invested' but does not specify whether this applies to the cumulative value across all three Aviva platforms (Pension, GIA, ISA) or each separately, nor does it specify how it accounts for the £100 minimum. The Aviva illustrations confirm 1% adviser charges but cite different basis values: GIA illustration [P4] shows £4,794.37 initial and states monthly amount; Pension illustration [P5] references adviser charges; neither illustration shows the exact £155,812 platform total. (3) The Client Agreement [P19] specifies a £100 minimum for ongoing fees but does not state whether this minimum applies to the aggregate portfolio value or each platform separately. The report does not address whether this minimum affects the calculation. The services described in the report (reviewing attitude to risk, reviewing portfolio suitability, market updates, ad-hoc advice, financial planning) align with language in the TFAS fee schedule [P1, P20] describing ongoing review services, so the service proposition is consistent. The initial fee services (researching, recommending, arranging) match the TFAS fee schedule description [P1] of 'advice we have provided and includes all of the work involved in analysing and researching the options available to you' and 'all of the work involved in setting up your chosen investment solution.' No evidence contradicts the fees charged or the rates applied. The fee structure is consistent with the Client Agreement signed by the client. However, the calculation basis for the initial fee (combining four investment sources into one 3% calculation) and the platform value basis for the ongoing fee (£155,812) lack explicit corroboration in the evidence, making it difficult to verify whether the fees are correctly calculated from the stated investment amounts.

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts Reduction in Yield (RIY) figures at age 60 under three growth scenarios (Low, Mid, High) for both existing and recommended solutions. For the Low growth basis, both existing and recommended show 1.6% RIY. For the Mid growth basis, existing shows 1.7% RIY and recommended shows 1.7% RIY with a critical yield of 2.9%. For the High growth basis, existing shows 1.8% RIY and recommended shows 1.8% RIY with a critical yield of 5.9%. The report states the effect of switching as £0 or 0.0% decrease across all scenarios. The report cites page 31 of the Suitability Report for these figures.
    File:     The Pension switch report [P20] [P21] provides RIY and critical yield figures at age 60. For Mid growth rate (2.94%), the existing pension shows RIY of 0.6% and the new solution shows RIY of 0.7%, with a critical yield of 2.9% and effect of switching of £100 or 0.1% decrease. For High growth rate (5.88%), the existing pension shows RIY of 0.7% and the new solution shows RIY of 0.7%, with a critical yield of 5.9% and effect of switching of £100 or 0.1% decrease. The pension switch report does not provide Low growth scenario figures. The RIY and critical yield figures in the pension switch report differ materially from those asserted in the canonical model.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts existing RIY at Mid growth of 1.7%, but pension switch report [P20] states 0.6%
      - Report asserts recommended RIY at Mid growth of 1.7%, but pension switch report [P20] states 0.7%
      - Report asserts existing RIY at High growth of 1.8%, but pension switch report [P20] states 0.7%
      - Report asserts recommended RIY at High growth of 1.8%, but pension switch report [P20] states 0.7%
      - Report asserts effect of switching as £0 or 0.0% decrease, but pension switch report [P20] states £100 or 0.1% decrease for both Mid and High growth scenarios
      - Report provides RIY figures for Low growth scenario (1.6% both existing and recommended) but pension switch report [P20] does not include Low growth scenario
      - Critical yield values for Mid (2.9%) and High (5.9%) match between report and pension switch report, but the RIY and effect of switching figures do not
    The canonical model asserts RIY figures that are substantially and materially different from those in the pension switch report [P20] [P21]. The most significant discrepancies are: (1) for Mid growth, the report claims existing RIY of 1.7% but the pension switch report shows 0.6% — a difference of 1.1 percentage points; (2) for Mid growth, the report claims recommended RIY of 1.7% but the pension switch report shows 0.7% — a difference of 1.0 percentage point; (3) for High growth, the report claims existing RIY of 1.8% but the pension switch report shows 0.7% — a difference of 1.1 percentage points. The effect of switching is also materially misstated: the report claims £0 or 0.0% decrease but the pension switch report clearly shows £100 or 0.1% decrease for both Mid and High growth scenarios. These are not rounding differences or basis adjustments — they represent fundamentally different calculations. The critical yield figures (2.9% at Mid growth, 5.9% at High growth) do match between the two documents, suggesting the two analyses are measuring the same recommendation but arriving at different RIY conclusions. The Low growth scenario (1.6% RIY) is asserted in the report but no corroborating analysis appears in the pension switch report or any other illustration. The pension switch report [P20] [P21] is the standard switching analysis document and explicitly states its RIY methodology in footnote [1]: 'Reduction in Yield is the impact of product, platform and fund charges on the investment growth rate.' This is the authoritative source for this comparison. The cumulative impact of costs has not been disclosed consistently: the report's RIY figures cannot be reconciled with the evidence, and the effect of switching is understated by a factor of 100 (stating no impact when the evidence shows £100 or 0.1% impact). No explanation is provided in the suitability report for why its RIY figures differ from the pension switch report. The guard regarding prescribed growth rates does not explain this: both documents use the same growth scenarios (Low, Mid, High for the report; 2.94% and 5.88% for the switch report), yet arrive at different RIY outcomes. These are not different bases or different comparison methods — they are contradictory assertions about the same metric for the same recommendation.

      · Pension switch report 16-12-2025 10-06.md [G] (P20)

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts a 'Like for Like' cost comparison showing the recommended plan is +0.08% more expensive than the existing plan, equating to +£63 annually. The existing plan is stated as 0.54% (£427) in recurring charges. The report breaks this down as 0.32% platform charge + 0.22% weighted fund charges. The report explicitly states 'Lower Costs' as a switching rationale, claiming a platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21%. However, the Like for Like comparison shows total recurring charges increasing by +0.08%. The report then states that when the 1% ongoing adviser fee is included, total costs become approximately 1.62% annually compared to the existing 0.54%. The report acknowledges this increase but justifies it by reference to 'improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight.' Transaction costs are excluded with the explanation that they are 'variable and subject to change' but 'already accounted for in the fund's performance figures, which are presented net of all charges.'
    File:     [P17] confirms the platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21%, stating 'Moving to our agency terms will reduce your platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%' and lists 'Lower Costs' as the first switching rationale. [P18] contains the 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' table showing '0.54%' / '£427' for the existing plan and '+0.08%' / '+£63' as the impact of the recommended alternative. [P18] further states 'Based on information supplied by Aviva, our calculations show that the recommended fund strategy is 0.08% more expensive than your current plan. This means that your proposed plan will need to outperform your current plan by £63 in the first year (in monetary terms). However, this small increase in charges is justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight.' [P18] also states 'Including Adviser Fee' section notes total cost approximately 1.62% annually compared to 0.54% existing. [P2] shows the recommended fund (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II) carries an 'Annual Charge' of 0.41%. [P15] confirms the Multi-Asset Plus Fund II annual charge (AMC) as 0.41%. [P23] shows existing fund costs for Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% at 0.22% ongoing and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% at 0.22% ongoing, with transaction costs of 0.04% and 0.03% respectively, totalling 0.26% and 0.25% respectively. [P18] discloses transaction costs are excluded because they are 'variable and subject to change based on factors such as market conditions and trading activity' but 'already accounted for in the fund's performance figures, which are presented net of all charges.' No external provider documentation (e.g., from Aviva, Vanguard) corroborating the 0.32% platform charge, the 0.21% new platform charge, or the 0.54% total recurring charge is present in the evidence pack. Research reports [P15], [P23], [P24] provide fund-level charges but do not validate the platform charges stated in the suitability report.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report claims 'Lower Costs' as a switching rationale yet the Like for Like comparison shows charges increasing by +0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62%). This internal contradiction contradicts the stated rationale.
      - The report states platform charge will reduce from 0.32% to 0.21% (0.11% saving) but the Like for Like charge difference is only +0.08%, implying the new fund charges are 0.19% higher than the old fund charges (0.11% platform saving offset by 0.19% fund charge increase = +0.08% net). However, [P2] shows the recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II at 0.41% while the existing Vanguard funds are stated at 0.22% weighted, a difference of +0.19%, which arithmetically reconciles. But the report does not explicitly disclose the new fund charge (0.41%) or the old weighted fund charge (0.22%) in the main cost comparison section, leaving this reconciliation implicit rather than transparent.
      - The report states transaction costs are excluded from the comparison because they are 'variable' and 'already accounted for in the fund's performance figures,' but [P23] shows transaction costs for the existing Vanguard funds at 0.04% and 0.03%, totalling approximately 0.03–0.04%. These are quantifiable in the research report but not disclosed or estimated in the suitability report's cost comparison, creating a silent omission of a cost layer from the existing arrangement.
      - The adviser fee is presented separately as '+1% per annum,' bringing total cost to 1.62%, but the report does not state whether this 1% fee is being charged across all three investment wrappers (pension, ISA, GIA) as described in [P11] ('1% per annum across all your Aviva Platform investments') or only on the pension. If applied only to the pension, the all-in cost for the pension alone would differ from the all-platform 1.62% figure stated.
    The cost comparison is arithmetically consistent at the line level: 0.54% existing, +0.08% impact, implying 0.62% recommended (before adviser fee). The derived figures from the canonical model confirm this (£427 at 0.54% and £63 at 0.08% are mathematically sound on a fund value of approximately £79,074). However, the requirement to assess consistency is materially breached in three respects: (1) Internal contradiction: the report claims 'Lower Costs' as a switching rationale yet discloses that costs will rise by +0.08%. This is a direct contradiction between the asserted benefit and the quantified impact. The report attempts to reconcile this by stating the increase is 'justified by improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight,' but this does not negate the clash between the rationale stated and the cost outcome measured. (2) Incomplete disclosure of cost layers: the report does not separately disclose the platform charge (0.32% existing, 0.21% new) and fund charge (0.22% existing, 0.41% new) in the Like for Like comparison table. A client reading the comparison table in [P18] sees only the aggregate 0.54% and +0.08%, not the component charges. The fund charge rise from 0.22% to 0.41% (+0.19%) is the primary driver of the +0.08% net increase but is not stated in the cost comparison section; it is only visible in the fund tables elsewhere. This violates the requirement to 'disclose all material charges' transparently. (3) Silent omission of transaction costs: [P18] excludes transaction costs with a general explanation that they are 'variable' and 'already accounted for in fund performance,' but [P23] quantifies transaction costs for the existing funds at 0.03–0.04%. These should either be estimated for the recommended fund and included in the comparison, or explicitly named and quantified in the cost section as a deliberate exclusion, not silently omitted. (4) Adviser fee scope ambiguity: the report states the 1% fee will apply 'across all your Aviva Platform investments' (pension, ISA, GIA) per [P11], but in the cost comparison [P18], the all-in cost of 1.62% is stated without clarifying whether this is platform-wide or pension-only. This creates uncertainty about the true comparator cost. The report's statement that the increase is 'justified' by benefits does not cure the incomplete and contradictory disclosure; COBS 6.1ZA and COBS 9.2.2R require both clear disclosure of costs and clear articulation of benefits. The benefits cited (risk alignment, oversight, tax relief on contributions) are documented but the cost disclosure itself is internally contradictory (Lower Costs claimed vs. higher costs delivered) and incomplete (fund charges not broken out, transaction costs silent, adviser fee scope unclear). No external provider documentation corroborates any of the platform charges or the 0.54% total recurring charge; these are quoted only from the report itself. This is not automatically a failing—the report's own extraction is canonical—but the absence of corroborating documentation from Aviva means the 0.54% figure cannot be verified against the provider's records. The vulnerability overlay does not materially affect this assessment; the requirement is about disclosure of cost figures and their reconciliation with stated benefits, not about communication adjustments for the client.

This means that your proposed plan will need to outperform your current plan by £63 in the first year (in monetary terms).   [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts 'Lower Costs - Moving to our agency terms will reduce your platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%' [P1, page 14]. It also states 'This small increase in charges is justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight' [P1, page 19]. The report claims 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 2.94% per year up to age 60 in order to meet the same value as your current plan. Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I believe this is achievable given the improved risk alignment and professional oversight' [P1, page 20]. Benefits cited include: Lower Costs (platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21%), Risk Alignment (funds at risk level 6 being moved to risk level 4), Professional Oversight, Adviser Charging Facilitation, Tax-Efficient Contribution (£3,600 gross), and Platform Integration. The report presents these as justifying the switch despite acknowledging a 'small increase in charges'.
    File:     Evidence shows existing platform charge of 0.32% [P2, P9] with combined recurring charges of 0.54% including fund charges [internal extraction note]. The recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II has a fund charge of 0.41% (AMC 0.30% + Additional Expenses 0.11%) [P12]. The pension switch research shows 'Impact of Recommended Alternative' as '+0.08%' increase in charges [internal extraction]. The derived figures show: total ongoing charge of £427.00 at 0.54% implies fund value £79,074.07; annual charge difference of £63.00 at 0.08% implies fund value £78,750.00. Existing holdings show past performance: Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity (3M: 6.03%, 6M: 13.74%, 1Y: 13.86%, 3Y: 35.92%, 5Y: 42.45%) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (3M: 7.36%, 6M: 17.61%, 1Y: 17.03%, 3Y: 45.41%, 5Y: 66.42%) [P2, P9]. Recommended fund performance: Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II (3M: 5.58%, 6M: 12.61%, 1Y: 11.92%, 3Y: 33.59%, 5Y: 36.21%) [P8, P11, P12]. The existing arrangement shows combined discrete performance of 12.86% (2021), -9.65% (2022), 11.23% (2023), 11.95% (2024), 13.47% (YTD) [P5]. Risk level of existing portfolio (weighted average of 60%/80% LifeStrategy split at 35.02%/64.95%) approximates risk 6; recommended fund at risk level 4 [P8].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'Lower Costs' by reducing platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%, but total recurring charges increase by 0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62% or higher when adviser fees added)
      - Report asserts required growth rate of 2.94% 'is achievable' based on past performance, but provides no explicit forward-looking fund performance projection or analysis; past performance data shows existing funds outperformed recommended fund over 5 years (existing combined ~58% vs recommended ~36.21%)
      - Report states benefits include Professional Oversight and Adviser Charging Facilitation but does not quantify the cost of 1% ongoing adviser fee in the benefit-cost analysis section
      - Report does not provide aggregated all-in cost illustration showing combined platform charge, fund charge, and adviser fee impact in a single figure for easy comparison
      - Benefits (risk alignment, professional oversight, tax efficiency, platform integration) are stated but not weighted or quantified against the cost increase; the cost justification relies on qualitative assertions rather than numerical analysis
      - Report acknowledges 'small increase in charges' but does not specify the magnitude (£63 per annum at £78,750 fund value = 0.08%) in the suitability section where costs are discussed
    The report presents a contradiction on its face. It opens with 'Lower Costs' as a headline benefit, citing platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21% (0.11 percentage point saving). However, the underlying evidence and derived figures show total recurring charges increase by 0.08 percentage points (£63 annually on a £78,750 fund value). This discrepancy arises because the platform saving (0.11pp) is outweighed by the fund charge difference and, critically, the addition of a 1% ongoing adviser fee is not transparently netted into the headline benefit. The report acknowledges a 'small increase in charges' later [P1, page 19] but does not reconcile this with the 'Lower Costs' claim on page 14. The internal extraction flagged this inconsistency: existing platform 0.32% vs new platform 0.21% (stated saving of 0.11%), but the switch charges table shows total recurring charge impact of +0.08%, indicating the fund cost is higher. On the Risk Alignment benefit: the report correctly identifies the existing portfolio (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60%/80% split) as risk 6 and the client profile as risk 4, and the recommended fund MAP II is correctly stated as risk 4. This benefit is genuine and supported. Performance: the report states the required growth rate of 2.94% 'is achievable given the improved risk alignment and professional oversight' [P1, page 20]. This is presented as justification for accepting higher costs, but it is an assertion without quantitative support. Past performance data shows existing funds materially outperformed the recommended fund over multiple periods (e.g., 5-year: existing ~58% cumulative vs recommended 36.21%), which contradicts the implicit claim that the recommended fund's performance will be adequate to offset higher charges. A client requiring 2.94% growth to break even faces higher hurdle than the existing portfolio's historical performance difference. The Professional Oversight and Adviser Charging Facilitation benefits are stated as client-specific but are not costed or quantified. The 1% ongoing adviser fee is mentioned in the assumptions but not integrated into the benefit-cost narrative in the main suitability findings section. Platform Integration is stated as a benefit of keeping pension, ISA and GIA on same platform, but this is generic, not client-specific, and no evidence is provided that consolidation itself generates sufficient value to justify switching costs or higher platform fees. Tax-Efficient Contribution (£3,600 gross with 20% relief) is legitimate but unrelated to the fund switch decision and thus not a defence of the higher costs. Overall, the report discloses multiple benefits and acknowledges a cost increase but does not aggregate costs or weight benefits quantitatively. The 'Lower Costs' headline contradicts the net cost impact, creating a material inconsistency. The justification for accepting higher costs rests on qualitative assertions (risk alignment is sound, professional oversight is valuable, performance is achievable) rather than on numerical comparison. The basis for claiming 2.94% growth is 'achievable' is not established; past performance of the recommended fund does not support this claim as strongly as the report implies.

  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report states a required outperformance of 0.08% per annum and an additional charge of +0.08% per annum for the recommended solution compared to the existing arrangement. The report presents this as the fund value difference (+0.08%) that the new recommendation must outperform to justify the cost increase.
    File:     No supporting document or passage evidences the benefits, outperformance justification, or value proposition attached to the 0.08% annual cost increase. The requirement is assessed from the report alone and does not draw on external corroboration.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states an additional annual charge of +0.08% but does not disclose what benefits or outperformance the client should expect to receive in exchange for this cost increase.
      - The report presents the required outperformance figure (0.08% pa) without explaining how this threshold was calculated, what assumptions underpin it, or whether it is realistic relative to the investment strategy or fund performance history.
      - The report does not quantify or describe the tangible benefits (consolidation, simpler administration, lower risk alignment, improved governance, etc.) that would justify accepting higher charges.
      - The switch rationale in the canonical model indicates 'Lower Costs' as a stated reason, yet the comparison shows total recurring charges increasing from 0.54% to 0.62%, a contradiction the report does not reconcile.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Expected outperformance or performance benefit of the recommended fund relative to existing holdings to justify the 0.08% additional annual charge., Historical or projected performance comparison between Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II and the existing Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings., Quantified description of non-cost benefits (e.g., consolidation savings, administration improvements, risk alignment to profile) supporting the recommendation., Basis and assumptions underlying the 0.08% required outperformance calculation., Comparison of total cost of ownership (platform + fund + advice fees) against alternatives, aggregated and presented to the client.
    The report asserts a required outperformance of 0.08% per annum but offers no evidence that this threshold is realistic, achievable, or supported by fund performance data, strategy comparison, or documented benefits. The 0.08% figure appears to be the annual charge difference only, calculated from the stated platform and fund charges (0.54% to 0.62%). However, the report does not explain why the client should accept a cost increase that the switch rationale itself characterises as 'Lower Costs' — this is an internal contradiction. The requirement asks whether costs are excessive relative to benefits provided; the report does not articulate what benefits justify the higher cost structure. The absence of any quantified benefit statement, performance comparison, or consolidation advantage means the cost increase stands unsupported by reasoning. The report's silence on the value proposition attached to ongoing adviser fees (1% pa, as noted in the extraction) further weakens the justification. The comparison method (RangeMatch with a guard on higher costs) requires that the stated benefits be examined; none are stated. This is a weakness in justification rather than a data inconsistency, because the report has made a cost comparison but not a cost-benefit comparison.

  [G7.7] Alternatives provide better cost or benefit to the client
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report recommends retaining the pension on the Aviva Platform but moving to TFAS agency terms with a platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21%, and switching underlying funds to match the client's risk profile (from risk level 6 to risk level 4). The report states 'Lower Costs - Moving to our agency terms will reduce your platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%' [P5]. The report justifies the recommendation based on: lower platform costs (0.32% to 0.21%), risk alignment (moving from risk 6 to risk 4), professional oversight, adviser charging facilitation, tax-efficient contribution (£3,600 gross), and platform integration. The pension switch report [P3] shows projected values at age 60 of £74,000 (low growth), £83,300 (mid growth), and £93,400 (high growth), with critical yields of 0.0%, 2.9%, and 5.9% respectively, and an effect of switching of £0 or 0.0% decrease at all growth rates. The report does not extract or present a formal comparison of alternatives considered with their comparative costs.
    File:     The pension switch report [P3] presents a charges comparison table comparing the existing pension with the new solution. The existing pension shows a 'Current Annual Recurring Charge' of 0.54% (which comprises platform 0.32% plus fund charges 0.22% from [P6]), while the new solution shows a total recurring charge of 0.62% (platform 0.21% plus fund charges 0.41%). This represents an increase of 0.08% annually [P3]. The switch analysis shows 'Effect of switching' at mid-growth of £0 or 0.0% decrease and at high growth of £100 or 0.1% decrease [P4], meaning the new solution slightly underperforms or matches the existing solution across growth scenarios. The report states 'From those solutions which were assessed as being suitable for your needs, a selection was made which included a comparison of all costs including Product, Platform and Fund charges. The available solutions were ranked by Maturity Value along with a generic stakeholder pension and the existing pension' [P1], but does not provide the full ranking table or show alternative solutions considered. The fund cost for the existing holdings (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80%) totals 0.25% [P6], while the recommended fund (Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II) costs are stated as 0.41% in the switch comparison.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'Lower Costs' as primary benefit but the new solution's total recurring charge (0.62%) is 0.08 percentage points higher than the existing solution (0.54%), resulting in £63 additional annual cost on a fund value of approximately £78,750
      - Report states platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21% (0.11% saving) but overall charges increase by 0.08%, indicating the fund cost component increases by 0.19 percentage points (from 0.22% to 0.41%)
      - Effect of switching shows £0 decrease at mid-growth and only £100 or 0.1% decrease at high growth, contradicting the 'Lower Costs' justification
      - Report claims comparison of 'all costs including Product, Platform and Fund charges' with solutions 'ranked by Maturity Value' but does not present the ranking table, alternative solutions, or why alternatives with potentially lower total costs were not chosen
      - The suitability report [P5] does not disclose that the new fund carries higher charges (0.41% vs 0.25%) despite the platform charge reduction
    The canonical model contains no entry for alternativesConsidered with comparativeCost values, indicating either the report is silent on formal alternatives or extraction failed. Inspection of the case file reveals the pension switch report [P1] references a ranking of solutions but the actual ranking table is truncated ('The top 10 results are shown below and are based on the medium growth rate pr[uncated]'). The report's primary cost justification—a platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21%—is accurate and supported by [P5] and [P7]. However, this is contradicted by the derived figures and the switch report's own charges comparison, which shows total recurring charges increasing from 0.54% to 0.62%. The fund cost component rises from 0.22% (existing Vanguard holdings per [P6]) to 0.41% (new Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II per the switch comparison in [P3]). The net effect is a cost increase of 0.08% annually, equivalent to £63 per year on a fund value of £78,750. The switch report [P3] itself shows 'Effect of switching' as £0 decrease (or no benefit) at mid and low growth rates and only £100 at high growth, confirming the new solution provides no material cost advantage and a slight disadvantage. The report's claim of 'Lower Costs' is unsupported by its own exhibits and is contradicted by the arithmetic. The non-cost benefits cited—risk alignment, professional oversight, adviser charging facilitation, and platform integration—are not quantified or compared against alternatives. No evidence is provided that alternatives with lower total costs were considered and rejected for documented reasons (e.g., fund range, access, servicing features). The suitability report does not separately disclose the ongoing adviser charge (stated elsewhere as 1% per annum) or explain how this layers on top of the product charges. The comparison lacks aggregated all-in cost presentation across existing and recommended solutions, and the claim of cost benefit is directly contradicted by the charges comparison table within the same documentation.

  [G7.8] Reduction in yield, critical yield and cumulative cost illustrations
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts RIY figures across three growth bases (Low, Mid, High) for both Existing and Recommended solutions at retirement age 60. For Existing: Low 1.6%, Mid 1.7%, High 1.8% pa. For Recommended: Low 1.6%, Mid 1.7%, High 1.7% pa. Critical yields are stated as: Recommended at Low growth 0.0%, Mid growth 2.9%, High growth 5.9% pa. The effect of switching is stated as £0 or 0.0% decrease across all scenarios. All figures are quoted from page 31 of the Suitability Report.
    File:     The Pension switch report [P3] and [P4] contain a comparison table showing: Existing pension RIY at Low 1.6%, Mid 1.7%, High 1.8%. Recommended RIY at Low 1.6%, Mid 1.7%, High 1.7%. Critical yield for Recommended: Low 0.0%, Mid 2.9%, High 5.9%. Effect of switching: £0 or 0.0% decrease across all scenarios. These figures match the report's assertions exactly. The pension switch report also provides detailed charge data: existing platform 0.32%, existing fund charges 0.22% weighted, combined 0.54% recurring charge. New solution platform 0.21%, fund charges 0.41%, combined 0.62% recurring charge. The switch analysis shows an increase of 0.08% in annual charges (£63 on £78,750 fund value). Illustrations [P6] for the new solution show charges reducing fund value from £91,500 to £84,800 over full term, described as a reduction in growth rate of 1.9%. Projection documents [P7], [P8], [P9], [P10], [P11] show product and investment charges reducing fund value by 0.6% growth rate reduction across multiple retirement ages.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Existing High RIY as 1.8% but supporting illustrations show only 1.7% for the mid-to-high growth comparison
      - Report does not explicitly state the charge increase from existing (0.54%) to recommended (0.62%), a net increase of 0.08%, despite asserting 'effect of switching' as £0 or 0.0% decrease
      - Illustration [P6] shows total charge impact (all charges) reducing growth by 1.9% pa, but this does not appear to be reconciled against the stated RIY figures in the switching analysis
      - Effect of switching is stated as £0 or 0.0% decrease, but charge comparison shows £63 annual increase (0.08%) which should result in lower projected values for the recommended solution under identical growth assumptions
    The RIY and critical yield figures extracted from the report align with those in the pension switching report [P3] and [P4], and these match the illustrations. The guard regarding prescribed growth rates applies: the report and evidence both use the FCA-mandated Low (0.00%), Mid (2.94%), and High (5.88%) growth bases. Comparison is valid on matched growth bases. However, a material inconsistency exists in the charge representation. The report asserts the effect of switching as '£0 or 0.0% decrease' in all scenarios, yet the switching charges comparison table shows the recommended solution incurs higher annual recurring charges (0.62% vs 0.54%, a net increase of 0.08%). At £78,750 fund value, this equates to £63 additional annual cost. The illustration [P6] confirms that all charges (including platform, product and fund charges) reduce pension fund value from £91,500 to £84,800, a reduction in growth rate of 1.9% pa. The critical yield figures (0.0% at Low, 2.9% at Mid, 5.9% at High growth) represent the return required from the new solution to match the projected maturity value of the existing pension. These are correctly stated and corroborated. The RIY figures (1.6%, 1.7%, 1.7% for recommended; 1.6%, 1.7%, 1.8% for existing) reflect the impact of all charges on growth. The discrepancy lies not in the numerical accuracy of RIY or critical yield—these are internally consistent—but in the representation of the switching effect. The report claims switching produces no reduction in value (£0 decrease) while the underlying charge data shows higher ongoing costs for the recommended solution. This is not explained by a guard: different growth bases do not explain why a higher-cost solution is presented as producing zero switching impact. The assertion that effect of switching equals £0 contradicts the evidence that charges increase by 0.08% annually.

  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an ongoing adviser charge of 1.0% per annum on all Aviva Platform investments, reviewed annually. The report lists six services included with this charge: (1) Reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss to ensure portfolio remains suitable; (2) Reviewing ongoing suitability of investment portfolio; (3) Market updates and changes in financial legislation; (4) Access to professional, experienced and qualified advisers for ad-hoc advice; (5) Access to full holistic financial planning; (6) Professional fund monitoring and portfolio review services. These services are stated on pages 9, 20, and 26 of the suitability report. The report does not state whether this fee matches the client agreement.
    File:     The Client Agreement To Proceed (P12) specifies: 'Ongoing Services - For most investment funds that we transact on your behalf, we charge an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100), which is automatically taken from the funds invested.' This matches the report's stated 1% ongoing charge. The agreement describes the scope of ongoing services more broadly: 'Should you choose to proceed with our recommendations, we will provide you with our Ongoing advice and review service, as described in the 'Important Information about our Services' document we have supplied to you, until either you, or we, consider this service to be no longer appropriate for your needs.' The IMPORT_2.md document (P2) states under 'On-Going Advice and Review Services': 'In many cases it will be important to regularly review your situation and the ongoing suitability of any plans, particularly for investments. Should you agree, TFAS Wealth Limited aims to revisit and review your plans at least annually. The service includes: Review meeting at least once a year and recommendations which may include the following as part of thi[s]...' The passage is truncated but references a review meeting at least annually. However, neither the Client Agreement nor the IMPORT_2.md document provides a complete, itemised list of what is included with the 1% ongoing charge that matches the six specific service propositions listed in the suitability report.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report lists six specific services attached to the 1% ongoing charge on pages 9, 20, and 26 (risk review, portfolio suitability review, market updates, ad-hoc adviser access, holistic financial planning, and professional fund monitoring). The Client Agreement To Proceed (P12) confirms the 1% charge but does not enumerate these six services. The IMPORT_2.md document (P2) references an annual review meeting and recommendations but is truncated and does not provide the complete itemised service proposition stated in the report. The match between what the report claims is included and what the client agreement and supporting documents detail is incomplete.
      - The report states the ongoing charge is reviewed 'Annually' as the review frequency, but the evidence (P2, P12) references 'at least once a year' and 'at least annually'—terminology that is consistent in substance but the report does not explicitly confirm this is the frequency at which the charge-bearing service is delivered.
      - The report asserts 'Access to full holistic financial planning to suit your needs' as a service for the 1% charge, but neither the Client Agreement nor IMPORT_2.md explicitly promises holistic financial planning as part of the ongoing review service; they focus on investment portfolio review and monitoring.
    The 1% ongoing adviser charge stated in the suitability report is corroborated by the Client Agreement To Proceed (P12), which specifies the same 1% charge on investment funds. The annual review frequency (Annually per the report) aligns with language in P2 and P12 that references 'at least annually' or 'at least once a year'. However, a material gap exists between the six itemised services listed in the suitability report and what appears in the supporting evidence. The Client Agreement and IMPORT_2.md both confirm the review service and annual meeting, but they do not provide a complete, matching enumeration of all six service propositions the report details. The report asserts that the client receives: (1) risk and capacity review, (2) portfolio suitability review, (3) market updates and legislative changes, (4) ad-hoc adviser access, (5) holistic financial planning, and (6) professional fund monitoring. The evidence confirms elements 1, 2, 4, and 6 but does not explicitly itemise element 3 (market updates and legislative changes) or element 5 (holistic financial planning) as part of the ongoing service bundle. The evidence (P2, truncated mid-sentence) is incomplete, and the full service description is cut off. The report does not state that the fee matches the client agreement, though it does match in amount (1%). The report provides no explicit defence of the 1% charge as value for money or as justified relative to alternatives, nor does it compare this charge to competitive offerings or explain why this particular level is appropriate for this client's circumstances. The report discloses the charge and service proposition but does not link the specific benefits to the cost or justify why the client should accept this ongoing fee.

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report states that total ongoing charges will increase by 0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62% annually), generating £63.00 additional cost per year on a fund value of £78,750. The report claims this 'small increase in charges is justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight.' Key benefits cited to justify the higher cost are: (1) Lower Costs — platform charge reduces from 0.32% to 0.21%; (2) Risk Alignment — moving from risk level 6 to risk level 4; (3) Professional Oversight — ongoing fund monitoring and portfolio review; (4) Adviser Charging Facilitation — direct platform deduction of adviser fees; (5) Tax-Efficient Contribution — £3,600 gross contribution with 20% tax relief; (6) Platform Integration — consolidating pension, ISA and GIA on same platform. The report states the required outperformance of 2.94% per annum 'is achievable given the improved risk alignment and professional oversight.'
    File:     Evidence from the case file supports several of the stated benefits: (1) Risk alignment is corroborated — the existing holdings (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% at 35.02% and 80% at 64.95%, combining to risk level 6) do not match the client's agreed Cautious Balanced profile of risk level 4 [P21]. The recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II is stated as risk level 4 [P20], and volatility analysis shows the recommended fund provides 'more consistent performance aligned with your Risk Level 4 profile' with 'lower volatility than your current mix' [P20]. (2) Platform integration is evidenced — the Aviva Platform does support pension, ISA and GIA consolidation, and consolidating arrangements on one platform is a documented feature [P2, P7, P8]. (3) Adviser charging facilitation exists — the platform allows adviser charges to be deducted directly [P10]. (4) Professional oversight is referenced in the suitability report's service proposition [P22], stating 'Reviewing your attitude to risk and capacity for loss' and 'Reviewing the ongoing suitability of your investment portfolio' are included services at 1% per annum. However, (5) the 'Lower Costs' claim is contradicted by the evidence: the report claims the platform charge 'will reduce from 0.32% to 0.21%' but the total recurring charge increases from 0.54% to 0.62%, a net increase of 0.08% (£63 per annum). The 0.54% figure in the switch comparison table is the existing plan's 'Current Annual Recurring Charge' [canonical model internal inconsistency note], comprising 0.32% platform + approximately 0.22% weighted fund charges. The new recommendation increases total charges despite the partial platform fee reduction.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts 'Lower Costs — Moving to our agency terms will reduce your platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%,' which is accurate for the platform charge in isolation, but this benefit is undermined by the fact that total annual recurring charges increase from 0.54% to 0.62%, creating a net cost increase of £63 per annum (0.08% on £78,750).
      - The report states the cost increase is 'small' and 'justified by improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight,' but the justification relies on benefits that either already exist in the current arrangement (professional oversight is described as a core service at 1% per annum, which is being added on top, not part of the switch benefit) or are not quantified in terms of financial value.
      - The report claims platform integration is a benefit of the recommendation, but there is no evidence in the file that the existing arrangement prevents consolidation or that consolidation is a benefit specific to switching rather than a service available on the existing platform.
    The report presents a primary justification ('Lower Costs') that is contradicted by the net figures: the platform charge reduction of 0.11% (from 0.32% to 0.21%) is more than offset by an increase in total recurring charges of 0.08%, resulting in a net cost increase. This is a material inconsistency. The derived figures confirm that £63 per annum (0.08% of £78,750) represents a real increase in charges. The report then justifies this increase by invoking 'improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight.' Risk alignment is supported by evidence: the existing holdings carry risk level 6 (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% weighted), which exceeds the client's agreed risk profile of 4, and the recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II Fund is rated risk level 4 [P20, P21]. However, the quantification of this benefit as worth £63 per annum is not evidenced. Professional oversight is referenced in the service proposition at 1% per annum [P22], but this is presented as the adviser fee, not as an incremental benefit of switching. The report does not clarify whether the client receives materially different oversight post-switch compared to pre-switch, or whether the £63 increase funds additional oversight or other services. The benefits of tax-efficient contribution and platform integration are real but not specific to this switch: tax relief on contributions is available under general pension rules, and platform consolidation is a feature of the Aviva Platform described in the research reports [P2, P7] but not presented as a consequence of switching. The report conflates cost justification (why pay more?) with service benefits (what does the higher cost buy?). The 'Lower Costs' claim is literally true for the platform charge in isolation but misleading in context, as total costs rise. This violates the principle that a claim of cost reduction must be supported by the net cost impact, not a component of it.
      · Suitability Report - Barnes.md [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice] (P20)

The fund demonstrates lower volatility than your current mix of Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% equity funds, which have a combined risk level of 6."
