  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended solution carries higher annual charges: a difference of +0.08% per annum compared to the existing pension. The report states this as 'RecommendedMoreExpensive' with a differencePercentage of 0.08% additional charge pa. The report also states a requiredOutperformancePercentage of 0.08% pa. Despite the higher charges, the projected values at age 60 are identical between the existing and new solution across all three growth scenarios: Low £74,000; Mid growth rate (2.94%) £83,300; High growth rate (5.88%) £93,400. The Reduction in Yield (RIY) figures are also identical or marginally lower in the new solution: Low 1.6% vs 1.6%; Mid 1.7% vs 1.7%; High 1.8% vs 1.7%. Critical Yield figures are stated as 0.0%, 2.9%, and 5.9% respectively for the new solution.
    File:     The pension switch report [P2] and [P7] show a detailed comparison table. At mid growth (2.94%), the existing pension projects £86,800 while the new solution projects £86,700 — a decrease of £100 or 0.1%. At high growth (5.88%), existing projects £97,400 vs new solution £97,300 — a decrease of £100 or 0.1%. The switch report identifies a current annual recurring charge on the existing plan of 0.54% (shown in [P6] as comprising 0.32% platform + 0.22% weighted fund charges). The new solution charges comparison in [P6] shows existing platform 0.32%, new platform 0.21%, but the total recurring charge comparison across passages shows the new solution would carry higher overall charges when the adviser fee is included. The report [P2] states 'Effect of switching' as £0 or 0.0% decrease at low growth, £100 or 0.1% decrease at mid and high growth. The suitability report [P17] discusses the Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II as offering 'cost-efficiency' and [P18] describes it as 'cost-efficient'. However, [P2] and [P7] explicitly quantify a reduction in projected maturity value despite the switch being made.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report canonical model states projected values of £74,000 (Low), £83,300 (Mid), and £93,400 (High) for the recommended solution. The pension switch report [P7] corroborates these exact figures for the new solution. However, [P2] shows the mid-growth new solution projects £86,700 (not £83,300) and high-growth projects £97,300 (not £93,400). This is a material discrepancy in the mid and high growth scenarios.
      - The suitability report states the difference is +0.08% additional charge per annum with identical projected outcomes. However, [P2] quantifies the effect of switching as £100 or 0.1% decrease in projected maturity value at mid and high growth rates, directly contradicting the assertion of identical outcomes.
      - The suitability report does not disclose in its canonical extraction that switching results in lower projected values. The switch report [P2] clearly states 'Effect of switching' as negative values (£100 decrease), yet the suitability report presents the recommendation without highlighting this projected value reduction.
      - The report asserts required outperformance of 0.08% pa to justify the higher charges. However, the pension switch comparison [P2] and [P7] shows that even at identical growth rates, the new solution produces lower maturity values (£100 less at mid and high growth), meaning the 0.08% outperformance requirement is not met by the projections shown in the evidence.
    The requirement is whether higher charges produce lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement that is not explained and justified. The evidence reveals a critical inconsistency. The suitability report's canonical model asserts identical projected outcomes (£83,300 mid, £93,400 high) despite charges being 0.08% higher. However, the authoritative pension switch comparison document [P2] and [P7] — which is part of the case file and derives from the same data — quantifies the effect of switching as a reduction in maturity value of £100 (0.1%) at both mid and high growth rates. This is not a rounding difference; it is a material contradiction. The switch report [P2] explicitly states 'Effect of switching: £100 or 0.1% decrease' under both mid and high growth scenarios. This evidence directly contradicts the suitability report's implicit claim that outcomes are identical. The Reduction in Yield figures in the suitability report match those in [P7], confirming both derive from the same underlying calculation. The critical finding is that the suitability report does not disclose the projected value reduction identified in its own supporting switch analysis. The report states the recommended solution is more expensive (0.08% additional charge pa) and implicitly claims or does not refute that outcomes are identical, but the evidence available in [P2] shows outcomes are lower by £100 at both mid and high growth rates. This is a classic scenario under [G7.11]: higher charges resulting in lower projected outcomes with no explanation or justification provided in the suitability report itself. The 0.08% required outperformance cited by the report is not achieved; the evidence shows a 0.1% underperformance instead. The report's silence on this negative effect of switching, despite its own supporting documents quantifying it, is a material disclosure shortfall. The suitability report describes the recommended fund as offering 'cost-efficiency' [P18] without acknowledging the switch results in measurably lower projected outcomes. The guard about required outperformance under 0.5% pa being within noise does not apply here: the projected reduction is explicit and quantified in the evidence, and the recommendation does not explain or justify accepting lower outcomes for higher costs.

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). Report discloses bond-specific risks (5% withdrawal allowance, chargeable events, tax bills) and inflation risk for cash, but the client's actual recommendations per the evidence pack involve switching existing Vanguard LifeStrategy pension holdings into Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II Fund and making GIA/ISA investments in bonds and cash vehicles. The bond withdrawal tax risk and specific bond disadvantages appear generic rather than matched to the specific products recommended. Report identifies market timing risk on page 14 as a disadvantage of the recommendation but does not quantify the potential impact or illustrate a loss scenario arising from market movement during the switch execution window. Report states all other risks 'are detailed in the product literature provided to you' but does not identify or highlight which of the extensive product literature risks are material to this specific client—e.g., the Multi-Asset Plus II factsheet discloses emerging markets volatility, property valuation judgment risks, and derivatives counterparty risk, none of which are extracted or explained in the report. 15 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states four material disadvantages: (1) AccessDelay - 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' [page 11]; (2) BondWithdrawalTaxRisk - 'Both types of bonds allow cumulative withdrawals of up to 5% of the original investment per policy year without an immediate tax charge. However, exceeding this allowance or surrendering the bond can trigger a chargeable event, leading to potentially significant income tax bills, particularly if not carefully managed' [page 23]; (3) BondCostAndTransparency - 'Bonds are less transparent in terms of underlying fund charges and investment performance and can involve additional setup and ongoing costs compared to ISAs' [page 23]; (4) CashInflationRisk - 'Long-term investment in cash is not recommended due to its inability to keep pace with inflation, leading to erosion of real value over time' [page 24]. The report also lists limitations including CashflowProjectionLimitations and states 'All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature provided to you. You should read this document carefully' [page 11]. A market timing risk is disclosed: 'Whilst the transactions are pending, there could be movements within the stock-market; that is to say that the stock-market could rise or fall in value. If the market rises in value between the date of realising your existing funds and the purchase of your new funds, you could be financially disadvantaged' [page 14].
    File:     The product literature and key features documents provide extensive risk disclosures. The Aviva Pension Key Features [P1, P3, P4, P13, P14, P15] disclose: delayed property sales affecting access (P4), income drawdown risks including means-tested benefits impact and fund depletion risk (P4), tax position variations, FSCS eligibility limits for insured funds investing in third-party funds (P15), and death benefit tax treatment. The Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II Factsheet [P5, P6] discloses: property fund liquidity risks, valuation judgment risks, transaction costs, high yield bond credit risks, alternative investment fund liquidity risks, derivatives counterparty default risk, foreign exchange risk for overseas investments, emerging markets volatility and political instability, smaller companies volatility and liquidity, fixed interest rate and inflation sensitivity, inflation eroding returns, market suspension risks, and structured product loss scenarios. The Aviva ISA Key Features [P2, P8, P11, P12] disclose: ability to make withdrawals, but note value can go down as well as up and may be worth less than invested (P2), transfer risks. The Aviva GIA Key Features [P7, P10] disclose: cash transfer period investment gap, property sale delays, structured products may return significantly less than invested (P7), disinvestment mechanics and charge impact. Research from GIA research report [P20, P23] shows fund costs and features analysis. The Fact Find [P17, P21] establishes client circumstances: retired from factory, briefly worked part-time driving (£890 net/month, now stopped), minimum retirement income need £2,200 net/month, £10,000 emergency fund in place, total disposable monthly income £383.33. The policy research [P9] shows Aviva fund value £79,092.32. No passage explicitly quantifies a reduction in projected retirement value from the switch, nor identifies what benefits or protections are being given up by switching from Vanguard LifeStrategy funds.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report discloses bond-specific risks (5% withdrawal allowance, chargeable events, tax bills) and inflation risk for cash, but the client's actual recommendations per the evidence pack involve switching existing Vanguard LifeStrategy pension holdings into Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II Fund and making GIA/ISA investments in bonds and cash vehicles. The bond withdrawal tax risk and specific bond disadvantages appear generic rather than matched to the specific products recommended.
      - Report identifies market timing risk on page 14 as a disadvantage of the recommendation but does not quantify the potential impact or illustrate a loss scenario arising from market movement during the switch execution window.
      - Report states all other risks 'are detailed in the product literature provided to you' but does not identify or highlight which of the extensive product literature risks are material to this specific client—e.g., the Multi-Asset Plus II factsheet discloses emerging markets volatility, property valuation judgment risks, and derivatives counterparty risk, none of which are extracted or explained in the report.
      - The product literature discloses that property fund valuations 'remain a matter of judgement and opinion' and property transaction costs affect fund value [P5], but the report does not address whether the client's recommended Multi-Asset Plus II fund composition includes property exposure and if so, what the materiality of that risk is for the client.
      - The product literature notes that 'structured products...may get back significantly less than you initially invested' [P7] and elsewhere 'you may get back significantly less than you've paid in' when charges exceed returns [P5], presenting a total loss scenario, but the report does not illustrate any downside or loss scenario for the investments recommended.
      - Report discloses that funds value 'can go down as well as up' in generic boilerplate language found in the product literature, but does not provide a specific quantified projection of what the client's portfolio could fall to under adverse market conditions, despite the client having stated a minimum retirement income need of £2,200 net/month.
      - The fact find [P17] establishes the client is newly retired (stopped part-time work, now fully retired) with modest disposable income of £383.33/month. The report recommends a £3,600 annual pension contribution but states in the evidence 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' without specifying what those earnings are. This ambiguity about affordability and the suitability of a contribution recommendation for a fully retired client is not addressed as a limitation in the disadvantages section.
      - Report cites access delay of '10-15 working days' [page 11] but the product literature for Pension and GIA accounts states that in 'certain circumstances' delays 'could, for example, be as a result of poor market conditions' and 'The delay may be up to one month' [P3, P10], suggesting the report's timeframe may understate the actual maximum delay risk.
      - Report does not disclose that switching from existing Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings carries the specific risk of embedded gains or fund switching costs, nor does it quantify the market timing gap risk in terms of what portfolio value change would trigger concern for this client given their minimum income requirement.
    The report discloses disadvantages and risks, but the disclosures are incomplete, generic, and not clearly linked to the specific client circumstances or recommended products. Four main gaps emerge. First, material product-specific risks identified in the literature are not extracted into the report. The Multi-Asset Plus II Factsheet discloses emerging markets volatility, property valuation judgment risk, derivatives counterparty risk, and structured product downside exposure, but none of these appear in the report's disadvantages list. The report instead delegates full risk disclosure to product literature with the statement 'All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature provided to you' [P11]. Under the decision logic, 'risk disclosure wholly delegated to product literature' is a High severity finding. Second, no loss scenario is quantified or illustrated. The product literature warns that structured products 'may get back significantly less than you initially invested' [P7] and funds can decline below contributions, but the report does not show the client what a 20%, 30%, or 50% market fall would mean for their stated retirement income need of £2,200/month net. The decision logic identifies 'no loss scenario is given' as a Potential Concern. Third, the market timing risk disclosure lacks context. The report states 'If the market rises in value between the date of realising your existing funds and the purchase of your new funds, you could be financially disadvantaged' [page 14], but does not quantify what percentage rise would matter given a fund value of ~£79,000. Fourth, the pension contribution recommendation for a fully retired client lacks transparency. The report states 'current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but the evidence pack shows the client is now fully retired with zero employment income following the end of part-time driving work. The affordability and suitability rationale for a £3,600 contribution is not explained. Fifth, the access delay disclosure appears to understate the product literature. The report states 10-15 working days; the product literature states delays up to one month are possible under poor market conditions [P3, P10]. Sixth, the report does not disclose what protections or benefits are being surrendered by switching from existing Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings. The extraction notes ambiguity about whether Multi-Asset Plus II is a rename or a different fund, suggesting a fund switch is occurring, but the report does not list what is being given up—e.g., any embedded performance, cost savings, or fund-specific guarantees of the Vanguard holdings. These gaps mean that material risks, constraints, and downside scenarios are either absent, delegated to appendix product literature, or stated only in generic boilerplate. The client cannot from the report alone understand the material disadvantages specific to their circumstances, the range of adverse outcomes, or what they are giving up by switching.

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts product-specific risks including: (1) 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider' (page 16); (2) 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement for these funds' (page 11); (3) switching timing risk where markets could rise or fall between disinvestment and reinvestment (page 11); (4) portfolio volatility described as 'moderate volatility and aims to balance growth potential with capital preservation' for the agreed risk profile (page 6); (5) general capital risk: 'unit prices and the value of your pension fund can fall as well as rise and there is no guarantee you will receive a return of your original capital' (page 17); (6) liquidity risk: 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' (page 14); (7) delegated risk disclosure: 'All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature provided to you. You should read this document carefully' (page 14); (8) capital gains tax: 'Capital gains above the annual exemption of £3,000 are taxed according to your tax bracket' (page 28).
    File:     Product literature on file includes: (P2, P4) Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II S14 Factsheet identifies risk rating of 4 (medium volatility) and multiple risk warnings (A - General investment not guaranteed, B - Foreign Exchange Risk, C - Emerging Markets, E - Fixed Interest, F - Derivatives). General risks include value can fall as well as up, losses from derivatives may exceed initial investment (P4), credit risk on fixed interest holdings, emerging markets risks of less regulated markets and political instability, foreign exchange exposure, suspension of trading in certain circumstances (P8). (P3) CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc Factsheet states 'The value of investments and any income derived from them can go down as well as up' and notes specific risks including derivative risk where 'losses can be greater than the initial investment', fixed interest securities risk from interest rate changes, credit risk, smaller companies risk, emerging markets risk, and liquidity risk affecting ability to buy/sell at favourable prices (P7). (P7) CT Universal MAP KIID states fund is 'rated 4 because historically it has shown a medium level of volatility' and recommends this fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years'. (P9, P11) Aviva Pension Key Features discloses 'the value of your pension can go down as well as up and you may get back less than has been paid in', notes pension transfers are complex and require consideration of 'charges, fund ranges, any valuable benefits that could be lost and tax implications', and advises seeking ongoing financial advice post-transfer. (P12) Report itself contains capital gains tax table showing rates by taxpayer band. (P6, P10) Aviva ISA and GIA Key Features detail risk factors including emerging/developing markets carry higher risks, small company investments less easy to sell with greater price fluctuations, overseas investment currency risk, property investment illiquidity, and suspension/deferment of dealing (P6, P10). (P14, P16) Pension projection documents state explicitly 'The growth rates are not guaranteed. They are not minimum or maximum amounts' and 'The value of your pension fund can go up or down and may be worth less than has been paid in' (P14).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the recommended fund is 'Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II' but states this is being switched from 'Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity (35.02%) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (64.95%)' — the extraction notes ambiguity whether this is a rename/restructure or a different fund being recommended as a switch, creating uncertainty about which specific risks apply to the recommended fund versus the existing holding.
      - The report cites switching timing risk (price movements between disinvestment and reinvestment) but product literature and pension key features do not elaborate on this specific switching-timing risk; the risk is asserted by the report without corroboration in the supporting fund or pension documentation.
      - The report states 'All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature provided to you' but this statement delegates substantive risk disclosure to product literature rather than explaining material product-specific risks within the suitability report itself. Product literature (P2, P3, P4, P7, P8) does contain derivative risk (losses can exceed initial investment), emerging markets risk, credit risk, and foreign exchange risk, but the report does not restate or summarize these risks; they appear only in the delegated reference.
      - The report does not explicitly address the CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc fund's specific warning that it 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years' (P7), though the report mentions 10-15 working day processing. The fund literature presents a 5-year withdrawal caution that is not mirrored in the suitability report's liquidity risk disclosure.
      - The report mentions no material disadvantages or features being given up by switching from the existing Vanguard LifeStrategy funds to the recommended fund. Pension key features (P9) highlight risk of losing 'valuable benefits' in a transfer but the report does not address what, if any, features are being foregone.
      - The report discloses capital gains tax at page 28 but this appears disconnected from the pension and ISA recommendations, which carry different tax treatments (pensions tax-deferred, ISAs tax-free). The tax consequence disclosure for each recommended product is not clearly integrated with each specific recommendation.
    The report asserts multiple generic capital-at-risk and performance risks (no guarantee of outperformance, market timing risk, capital losses possible, volatility moderate) which are corroborated by product literature and pension key features. These are reasonably prominent and clear. However, the risk disclosure exhibits material gaps: (1) Specific product risks documented in fund literature — derivative risk (losses can exceed investment), emerging markets risk, credit risk on fixed interest securities, foreign exchange risk, smaller companies risk, trading suspension risk — are mentioned only in product literature (P2-P8) and not restated or highlighted in the suitability report. The report delegates risk explanation via 'All other risks are detailed in the product literature provided' (page 14), which is a limitation on prominence and client-facing clarity; (2) The CT Universal MAP KIID's explicit recommendation that the fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years' is not addressed in the suitability report, despite the report noting 10-15 day processing delays. This specific fund limitation is material and absent from the report's risk disclosure; (3) Pension transfer key features (P9) warn of loss of 'valuable benefits' such as guaranteed income, but the suitability report does not identify what, if anything, the client is giving up from the existing Vanguard holdings — whether those holdings carry any guarantees, protections, or features not present in the replacement fund; (4) Tax consequences are mentioned generically (capital gains tax table at page 28) but are not linked to the specific pension and ISA recommendations. The pension fund operates on a tax-deferred basis (no disclosure of tax-deferred growth compared to taxable alternatives in suitability report itself); ISA recommendation carries tax-free growth benefit (not highlighted as advantage in the risk and disadvantage section, suggesting asymmetric disclosure of benefits vs. risks); (5) The uncertainty over fund identity (Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II vs. restructured Vanguard LifeStrategy) creates ambiguity about which specific risks attach to the recommendation. Without clarity, the suitability report's risk assertions cannot be definitively matched to the product being recommended. The false-positive guards state that standard capital-at-risk wording satisfies general warnings but not product-specific risks, and that delegating risk disclosure to product literature is a limitation on the report. Both apply here. The report covers generic risks adequately but fails to restate or prominently present material product-specific risks (derivative, emerging markets, credit, FX, smaller companies, suspension, withdrawal horizon caution) that appear in fund and product literature. The absence of features-lost analysis, tax-consequence integration, and fund-identity clarity compounds these limitations.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states multiple tax consequences: (1) Pension tax relief: 'A net contribution of £80 effectively becomes £100 gross in your pension' and 'You will receive 20% tax relief on contributions, meaning the government adds £720 to your £2,880 net contribution' [P3]. (2) Contribution limit warning: 'If you contribute more than you earn in this tax year, you'll face a tax charge because you'll have received tax relief beyond your income limit' with acknowledgement 'We are unsure of your earnings this tax year, as you retired and then only worked for a short period of time' [P3]. (3) Assertion of compliance: 'However, your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' [P3]. (4) ISA tax treatment: 'Investment gains within an ISA are tax free. There is no tax to pay on any interest or dividends earned within a cash ISA or Innovative Finance ISA, and there is no Income Tax or Capital Gains Tax to pay on profits made within a stocks and shares ISA' [P3]. (5) GIA tax planning: 'By using a GIA, you can take advantage of your annual Capital Gains Tax (CGT) allowance through careful planning, helping to minimise tax on your investment gains' with capital gains tax rate of 24% for basic rate taxpayers and dividend tax at 8.75% [P3]. (6) Pension death benefits: 'Should you die before age 75, the nominated beneficiaries can receive a lump sum, drawdown an income or purchase a dependents annuity. All these options are paid free of tax. Where you die after the age of 75, the same options are available, but tax is paid at the beneficiary's marginal rate of tax' [P3]. (7) IHT consequences: 'From 6 April 2027, most unused defined contribution pensions and death benefits (such as drawdown funds and uncrystallised pots) will be included in your taxable estate for IHT' [P3]. (8) Advice fee: 'Initial advice fee of 3% (£4,794)...will be paid from the GIA funds to ensure maximum amount invested tax-efficiently in pension and ISA' [P3]. (9) ISA death treatment: 'An ISA does not lose its tax-free status on the death of the investor. It becomes a 'continuing ISA' and will remain so for up to three years' [P3]. (10) Current IHT position: 'Currently, your Inheritance Tax liability will be £0' [P3].
    File:     Supporting evidence confirms: (1) Pension tax relief principles are standard and reflected in product literature [P2]. (2) Death benefit tax treatment for pensions is confirmed: 'lump sum,drawdown' options available for dependants and nominees [P2]. (3) ISA tax exemptions are standard product features [P9]. (4) Pension illustrations provided show projected values at age 65 (£73,100 to £124,000 under different growth scenarios) and age 67 (£71,900 to £137,000) [P14, P23]. (5) Product research shows the recommended Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II with 0.41% weighted AMC [P21]. (6) No document in the file establishes the client's actual current earnings or confirms whether £3,600 gross annual contribution is compliant with contribution limits based on verified income [P4]. The extracted data notes ambiguity: 'Client's employment status and earnings unclear: retired from factory role after 35 years, then briefly worked part-time driving (£890 net/month) which has since stopped. Current employment income appears to be zero' [extraction report]. (7) GIA research report discusses risk profile and asset allocation for this client [P15], but no specific GIA tax planning documentation confirms the stated CGT allowance strategy for this client's circumstances.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but the case file contains no verification of current earnings. The extraction notes explicitly that 'Client's employment status and earnings unclear' and that the client is retired with no ongoing employment income documented. The £3,600 gross contribution (£2,880 net) cannot be verified as compliant with pension contribution limits without knowing current qualifying earnings.
      - The report asserts contribution tax relief consequences ('If you contribute more than you earn...you'll face a tax charge') but immediately acknowledges uncertainty: 'We are unsure of your earnings this tax year.' The claim that current earnings are sufficient contradicts the stated uncertainty.
      - The report states tax relief of 20% on contributions, which applies to basic rate taxpayers, but the file contains no evidence confirming the client's current tax band. The client's income is stated as £1,200 net per month including a defined benefit pension [P4], but no tax band analysis is provided to verify the 20% relief rate is correct for this client.
      - The report references CGT allowance planning and 24% CGT rate for basic rate taxpayers, but provides no detailed illustration of how the client's specific GIA holdings would generate taxable gains or how the £3,000 annual CGT allowance would apply to this particular portfolio.
      - The report mentions dividend income tax at 8.75% for basic rate taxpayers but does not explain whether the recommended portfolio is expected to generate dividend income or at what level, making the relevance of this disclosure uncertain.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Current qualifying earnings or employment income for the current tax year (client is retired; no documentation of ongoing qualifying income to support £3,600 gross contribution), Client's confirmed tax band or marginal rate (needed to verify 20% relief rate and to assess consequences of exceeding contribution limits), Detailed breakdown of income sources to establish whether contribution limit check has been performed correctly, Analysis of whether the recommended portfolio composition will generate dividend income, and if so, at what level, Evidence of GIA tax planning analysis showing projected capital gains and application of CGT allowance to this client's specific circumstances
    The requirement tests whether material tax consequences are fairly, clearly, accurately and prominently explained and are consistent with supporting evidence. The report discloses numerous tax consequences across generic and client-specific categories. However, a material inconsistency exists at the foundation of the tax consequence narrative: contribution limit compliance. The report states the client faces potential tax charges if contributions exceed earnings, acknowledges uncertainty about earnings, but then asserts earnings are sufficient. This is contradictory. No document in the file establishes the client's current qualifying earnings. The extraction notes the ambiguity explicitly. Without this critical fact, the assertion that the contribution is compliant cannot be verified against evidence. The report's tax band is assumed at 20% relief but not stated or verified. While the report extensively discusses generic tax principles (ISA treatment, CGT rates, dividend tax, pension death benefits, IHT from 2027), these are legislative statements not specifically tailored to this client's actual tax position. The client's verified income is £1,200 net per month including DB pension [P4], but no analysis explains whether this places them in basic rate taxpayer category or whether any non-employment income affects the tax relief calculation. The CGT and dividend tax rates are stated generically without illustration of expected portfolio returns. The advice fee of 3% (£4,794) is disclosed and its payment from GIA is explained, but no separate calculation shows the tax consequence of paying this fee (whether it reduces the client's available GIA funds for investment and therefore the after-tax position). The IHT disclosure regarding the 2027 change is prominent and clear. The pension death benefit tax treatment is stated and consistent with product literature [P2]. The ISA tax treatment is standard and not contradicted. The core deficiency is that the most material consequence—whether the recommended contribution complies with tax law—rests on an unverified factual premise (earnings sufficiency). This is not a generic legislative gap but a client-specific compliance assertion without supporting evidence. Under COBS 9.4.7R and COBS 2.1.1R, material tax consequences must be explained; where they depend on a factual assertion, that assertion must be supported or qualified. The qualification exists ('we are unsure') but the ultimate assertion of compliance does not.

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report contains a dedicated section titled 'Other Considerations of Switching' on page 11 that addresses disadvantages and risks. The report states: 'You should remember that unit prices and the value of your pension fund can fall as well as rise and there is no guarantee you will receive a return of your original capital, especially in the early years or if you choose to take an income from it. Past performance is no guarantee of future returns.' The report also states 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider.' The report discusses alternative solutions considered and rejected, including retaining current funds (misaligned with risk profile), retaining Moneybox ISA (lower projected performance), stakeholder pensions (less cost effective), and other vehicles (VCTs, Bonds, Cash Deposits). Tax consequences are addressed in dedicated sections covering ISAs, pensions, GIAs, and capital gains. Loss scenarios are illustrated through cashflow analysis charts and performance projections. The report identifies the client's knowledge and experience as limited and assesses capacity for loss as 'Medium-High' based on guaranteed income and liquid assets. The report discloses the ongoing advisory fee of 1% per annum.
    File:     The pension switch report [P9][P10][P11] shows effect of switching as £0-£100 or 0.0%-0.1% decrease in projected values across low, mid, and high growth scenarios, with critical yields of 0.0%-5.9% required to match existing pension maturity values. The report states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' [P11], indicating no explicit benefits are being surrendered. The GIA illustration [P13] shows charges deducted from projected values over 10 years. The cashflow analysis under market crash scenario [P14] demonstrates resilience with liquid assets remaining positive through age 100. Tax treatment passages [P1][P2] explain ISA tax-free status, pension allowances, GIA taxation on dividends and interest, and capital gains tax brackets. The suitability report [P3][P4][P5][P6] discusses alternative solutions rejected (current funds misaligned with risk, Moneybox ISA lower performance, stakeholder pensions less suitable, VCTs/Bonds/Cash not aligned with objectives), capacity for loss assessment, and cancellation rights. The report [P5] states 'However, please be aware the cancellation notice includes a provision that any previous provider is not obliged to take the plan back.' Risk profile assessment [P6] notes the client describes themselves as having 'very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' but has 'invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years' and has 'very little' knowledge of unit trusts and stocks and shares specifically.
    Discrepancies:
      - The pension switch report [P11] states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products', yet the suitability report does not explicitly address what features or guarantees, if any, are being surrendered in the Vanguard LifeStrategy funds being switched from. The report states these funds are 'not aligned with your risk profile' but does not specify what protections or features (such as capital guarantees, smoothing, or income options) they may offer that will be lost.
      - The switch projections [P9][P10][P11] show negligible difference (£0-£100 or 0.0%-0.1%) between existing and new solutions across growth scenarios, yet the suitability report rationale emphasises switching is 'more cost effective than a typical stakeholder pension' and the 'recommended funds have provided a steadier performance'. The report does not explain why a switch costing £0-£100 or 0.1% (plus 1% ongoing advisory fee) is justified when projections show no material improvement.
      - The report's section 'Other Considerations of Switching' discusses general risks (unit prices can fall, past performance not guaranteed) and alternative solutions rejected, but does not quantify specific loss scenarios for the recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II. The performance charts [P3][P8] show 5-year historical data but the report does not illustrate a stress scenario (e.g., market downturn) showing potential loss from the recommended fund specifically.
      - The report assesses the client's knowledge as 'very little' and mentions they have not previously invested in 'unit trusts' or 'stocks and shares' [P6], yet the recommendation moves their pension and £20,000 ISA into managed funds and equity-based investments. The report does not address how this represents a material shift in investment complexity for a client with limited knowledge, nor does it explain the implications of this knowledge gap in prominent risk language.
      - The report states the client 'has a good level of guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions' but does not quantify the risk that this guaranteed income may not be sufficient if markets perform poorly. The cashflow analysis shows a shortfall during the period when 'Yvette retires, until her State Pension begins' [P6][P8], but no loss scenario explicitly illustrates the impact of poor market performance on this critical period.
    The report does contain a dedicated section 'Other Considerations of Switching' on page 11 and risk content is not confined solely to an appendix, satisfying the basic prominence requirement. However, several material disadvantages and risks identified in the evidence are not prominently or adequately explained in the report. First, the pension switch report documents that no benefits are being surrendered [P11], yet the suitability report does not disclose what, if any, features of the existing Vanguard funds (such as capital preservation, smoothing, or structured income options) the client is giving up. Second, the projections show the switch produces at best no improvement (£0 change) or minor deterioration (£100 or 0.1% decrease) across all growth scenarios [P9][P10][P11], while the report's rationale emphasises cost-effectiveness. The report does not transparently explain that the projected outcome of the switch is equivalent to the existing pension, implying the 1% ongoing advisory fee will be a net cost. Third, the report assesses the client's knowledge and experience as 'very little' for stocks and shares and unit trusts [P6], but does not emphasise this knowledge gap in the risk disclosure. The recommendation represents a material shift to equity-based managed funds, which for a client with limited investment knowledge constitutes a material disadvantage that deserves prominent disclosure. Fourth, the cashflow analysis identifies a critical period of shortfall when 'Yvette retires, until her State Pension begins' [P6][P8], yet no loss scenario illustrates the impact of poor market performance during this period on the client's ability to meet this shortfall. The report provides general statements that 'unit prices and the value of your pension fund can fall as well as rise' and past performance is not guaranteed, but does not quantify illustrative losses or stress scenarios specific to the recommended funds. Fifth, the report does not address the material risk that if market performance is poor in early years, the 1% advisory fee compounds the loss and reduces the fund value available at retirement. The switch projections assume identical growth rates for existing and new solutions, but do not reflect the ongoing 1% advisory charge impact clearly in the main risk disclosure. Sixth, the report notes a cancellation provision stating previous providers are not obliged to take the plan back [P5], but does not explain the practical implication of this (that if the switch is cancelled early, the client may not be able to reverse to the existing arrangement). The vulnerability overlay is relevant: the client has very little knowledge of unit trusts and stocks and shares [P6], and material disadvantages associated with this knowledge gap are not prominently disclosed, which may impair the client's ability to understand the risks of the recommendation.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states: (1) 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement for these funds' [P22, page 10]; (2) existing arrangement has 'Safeguarded Features incl. TFC/GARs/MIG: None' [P22, page 5]; (3) existing arrangement offers 'Full flexi-access drawdown and UFPLS available' [P22, page 5]; (4) existing arrangement is described as 'Good platform with comprehensive facilities, but current holdings do not match agreed risk profile' [P22, page 5]. The report recommends switching from Aviva Personal Pension Plan (no platform) to Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice (SIPP on Aviva Platform).
    File:     Supporting evidence shows: (1) Existing arrangement (Aviva Personal Pension Plan AV2075926) has no guaranteed annuity rates, no guaranteed minimum pension, and no guaranteed growth rates [P2, P4]; (2) Plan details show '25% Tax free cash, 75% Taxable flexible drawdown available' and 'We do offer full and partial encashment from age 55. We offer flexi-access drawdown' [P13]; (3) New arrangement (Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice SIPP) is moving to Aviva Platform with stated reduction in platform charges from 0.32% to 0.21%, but overall charges increase from 0.54% to 0.62% when all costs are included [P5, P6, P8, P10, P11]; (4) Existing holdings are Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% (35.02%) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% (64.95%) with combined fund charges of 0.22% [P11]; (5) New solution invests 100% in Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II [P6]; (6) No safeguarded benefits, GARs, GMPs or guaranteed growth on either arrangement [P2, P13]; (7) Existing arrangement states 'No annuity options' in product information [P13]; (8) Pension switch report confirms 'There is no Market Value Adjustment/Early Transfer Charges applicable to this plan' [P13].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states existing arrangement offers 'Full flexi-access drawdown and UFPLS available' but product information [P13] states 'No annuity options' and 'We do offer full and partial encashment from age 55. We offer flexi-access drawdown' without specifying UFPLS capability for this specific policy
      - Report does not disclose that both arrangements lack guaranteed annuity rates, guaranteed minimum pension, and guaranteed growth rates, treating only performance guarantee as the material loss
      - Report does not identify or discuss the change in underlying fund holdings from Vanguard LifeStrategy funds (passive, lower cost, established holdings) to Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II (active management, different provider, different investment approach)
      - Report does not disclose that net charges are increasing by 0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62%) despite citing 'Lower Costs' as a benefit, meaning client loses the benefit of lower platform charge through higher overall charges
      - Report states platform has 'comprehensive facilities' but does not specify what facilities the new platform adds or what facilities of the existing arrangement are being replaced
    The requirement is to establish what benefits, features or protections the client is giving up. The comparison is between the existing Aviva Personal Pension Plan with Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings and the new Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice SIPP with Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II holdings.
    
    Material features being lost: (1) Existing fund holdings in low-cost passive Vanguard LifeStrategy funds are being replaced entirely with an active Aviva fund. This represents a material change in investment approach and cost structure. The report does not disclose this substitution as a loss of an existing arrangement feature. (2) Charges are increasing on a net basis despite the report's claim of lower costs; the platform charge reduction (0.11%) is more than offset by other cost increases, resulting in a net 0.08% annual increase. This is a material loss of cost efficiency. (3) The report fails to prominently disclose that neither arrangement offers performance guarantees, GARs, GMPs, or guaranteed growth, presenting only the generic statement about no performance guarantee without context that the existing arrangement similarly lacks such protections.
    
    Guards applied: (1) A benefit the client cannot currently access is still a benefit being given up — not applied here as flexi-access drawdown is available at age 55 and client is age 60. (2) A feature described in generic product literature that this policy does not actually carry is not being given up — applied to UFPLS status, which the policy information lists as 'full and partial encashment from age 55' and 'flexi-access drawdown' but does not explicitly confirm UFPLS. (3) Two values on different bases are not contradictory — charges are on the same basis (annual recurring charges as percentage of fund value) and are comparable across both arrangements.
    
    The report's treatment of feature loss is minimal and incomplete. It names one generic feature (no performance guarantee) without explaining what client is giving up or gaining. It fails to disclose the substantial change in fund holdings from established passive Vanguard funds to an active Aviva fund. It does not disclose that net charges are increasing despite the stated rationale of 'Lower Costs'. The assertion that the existing platform has 'comprehensive facilities' is made without specifying what features differentiate the platforms or what the client gains or loses in the move.

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that 'This small increase in charges is justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight' [P20, page 19]. It also states that the recommended plan 'would need to grow by 2.94% per year up to age 60 in order to meet the same value as your current plan' and claims 'this is achievable given the improved risk alignment and professional oversight' [P20, page 20]. The report lists justifications including: risk profile alignment (funds 'managed to remain within this risk tolerance'), diversification and reduced risk, 'blend of active and passive investments' keeping costs low, 'competitive' charges, and 'performed well in comparison with their peers'. The comparison table [P21] shows the existing plan charges 0.54% annually (£427 on current fund value), and the recommended alternative is +0.08% more expensive (+£63), for a total of 0.62%. Including the 1% ongoing adviser fee, total cost reaches 1.62% annually versus the current 0.54%.
    File:     The pension switch report [P4, P10] shows projected values at age 60 under the mid-growth scenario (2.94%): existing pension £86,800 vs new solution £86,700 (effect of switching £0 or 0.1% decrease). Under low growth (0%), both project to £74,000. Under high growth (5.88%), both project to £93,400 with £100 or 0.1% decrease in new solution. The critical yield required from the new solution to match existing pension is 2.9% (mid scenario). The pension switch charges comparison [P4] identifies 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' when switching. The research report [P16] shows the recommended Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II has a 5-year cumulative return of 36.21% and weighted AMC of 0.41%, compared to the existing Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings (60% Equity at 0.22% and 80% Equity at 0.22% weighted average). The GIA illustration [P1, P3] shows adviser charges of 1.00% ongoing, with initial adviser charge of £4,794.37. Projected values show the effect of all charges cumulatively reducing fund growth. The cashflow analysis [P23] indicates 'no shortfall periods' and the 'current financial position and planned investments should support your desired lifestyle through to age 100', showing the client's financial need does not require the switch.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report claims the increased charges are 'small' and 'justified', but no quantified linkage is established between the cost increase (+0.08% or +£63 annually) and the claimed benefits (improved risk alignment, professional oversight, diversification, cost-efficiency). The justifications are largely generic rather than client-specific cost-benefit analysis.
      - The report asserts that the 2.94% required growth rate is 'achievable' based on past performance, but the pension switch report [P4] shows the new solution requires a critical yield of 2.9% to merely match existing pension value—this is a breakeven scenario, not an outperformance scenario, contradicting the claim that the adviser's 1% fee is justified by superior outcomes.
      - The report states the recommended fund 'provides a blend of active and passive investments, which allows for some active management, whilst keeping costs low' [P20, page 22], but the research data [P16] shows the Aviva fund has an AMC of 0.41% versus the Vanguard holdings at 0.22%, representing a fund-level cost increase of 0.19%, yet the pension switch report shows the total recurring charge increasing only 0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62%), an apparent inconsistency in the charge attribution.
      - The report claims benefits including 'greater diversification and reduced risk' through the recommended multi-asset fund, but the asset allocation in the research report [P18] for the recommended Aviva fund shows significant short positions (negative allocations of -5.54%, -64.73%, -5.80%) totalling material negative exposure, which may increase rather than reduce risk in ways not explained to the client.
      - The switch comparison [P4] shows 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' being given up, but the suitability report does not disclose any material guarantees, protections or specific benefits the client is surrendering by switching from the existing Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings (which provide simple, transparent passive exposure) to an actively managed multi-asset fund with complex leverage structures.
    The requirement asks whether the report justifies increased costs with a clear explanation linking cost rises to client-specific benefits. The report does provide justifications, but they fail the consistency and specificity tests. First, on quantification: the report identifies a +0.08% (or +£63 annual) increase in recurring charges (excluding adviser fees), but the justifications given—'improved risk alignment', 'professional oversight', 'diversification', 'cost-efficiency'—are generic marketing statements not quantified against the stated cost. No client-specific analysis shows, for example, that the improved risk alignment to the client's profile of 4 (which the existing holdings at weighted average 6.0 arguably already deviate from [P19]) is worth +£63 per year. Second, on achievability: the report claims the 2.94% growth is 'achievable' and 'justified' by past performance and professional oversight, but the pension switch report [P4] defines 2.94% as the critical yield—the rate at which the new solution merely matches, not outperforms, the existing pension. At this breakeven rate, the additional 1% adviser fee is an unrecovered cost with no offsetting benefit demonstrated in the file. The client's cashflow shows no need for additional management (no shortfall periods through age 100 [P23]), weakening the 'ongoing professional oversight' justification. Third, on cost structure: the report states the recommended fund 'keeps costs low' through its blend of active and passive management, yet research data [P16] shows the recommended Aviva fund at 0.41% AMC versus existing Vanguard at 0.22%, a 0.19% increase at fund level. The reported 0.08% total charge increase does not match this fund-level delta, suggesting unaccounted charge attribution or a basis not clearly disclosed to the client. Fourth, on asset allocation risk: the research [P18] reveals short positions in the Aviva fund not mentioned in the suitability report's 'diversification' and 'reduced risk' claims, and no stress-testing or leverage risk disclosure appears in the report. Finally, on benefits surrendered: the switch report records 'No benefits were listed' [P4], but the report does not disclose what the client gives up—simple passive exposure, lower ongoing management cost, explicit fund mandate—and does not connect these sacrifices to the cost increase. The justifications present are weak: they repeat the benefit without establishing its value relative to cost, and they apply generic principles without client-specific cost-benefit analysis. A justification that repeats the benefit without connecting it to cost is weak per the guard; this is what the report does. The report meets a minimal threshold (it states a justification), but the connection between the increased cost and the stated benefit is not established with sufficient clarity or client specificity to constitute a fair, clear and adequate explanation of why increased charges are acceptable in this client's circumstances.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report discloses the following material disadvantages and risks: (1) Access delay of 10-15 working days for fund administration, disinvestment and banking (page 11, linked to OBJ3); (2) Bond withdrawal tax risk—cumulative withdrawals up to 5% per policy year are tax-free but exceeding this or surrendering triggers chargeable events and potentially significant income tax bills (page 23); (3) Bond cost and transparency—bonds are less transparent in terms of underlying fund charges and performance and involve additional setup and ongoing costs compared to ISAs (page 23); (4) Cash inflation risk—long-term investment in cash is not recommended due to inability to keep pace with inflation leading to erosion of real value (page 24); (5) Projection limitations—charts are based on assumptions and small variances can produce marked differences in outcomes when compounded, and do not consider potential long-term care costs (page 21); (6) Pension switch performance risk—no guarantee the new provider's funds will outperform existing provider (page 16); (7) No performance guarantee on new investment (page 11); (8) Product literature risks—all other risks are detailed in product literature (page 11).
    File:     The evidence includes product literature for the recommended Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II which identifies the following risk warnings: A (General: investment not guaranteed, value can go down, could get back less than paid in), B (Foreign Exchange Risk), C (Emerging Markets), E (Fixed Interest), F (Derivatives). [P2] and [P6] confirm general investment risk. The CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc KIID [P4] contains risk factors including Derivative Risk (losses can exceed initial investment), Fixed Interest Securities Risk (interest rate sensitivity), Credit Risk, Smaller Companies Risk, Emerging Markets Risk, and Liquidity Risk, plus a recommendation that the fund may not be appropriate for investors planning to withdraw within 5 years. Pension switch reports [P3], [P5], [P14], [P15], [P18] show critical yield comparisons indicating the new solution requires market outperformance to match existing pension maturity values (critical yields of 2.9%, 5.9%, 0.0%, 2.9%, 5.9% across scenarios). The transfer value illustration [P15] confirms no transfer penalty but states the transfer value is not guaranteed and is calculated after sale of investment units. The Aviva Key Features document [P10] discloses that during a cash transfer money won't be invested for a period of time and won't be affected by market changes, but there may be charges associated with transferring or re-registering investments. No benefits are listed as lost in the existing products in [P3]. The research reports [P7], [P12], [P13] confirm risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced) and discuss stochastic modelling but do not identify material risks absent from the suitability report.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not explicitly disclose the range of risks identified in the fund literature: Derivative Risk (with potential for losses exceeding initial investment), Fixed Interest Securities Risk, Credit Risk, Smaller Companies Risk, Emerging Markets Risk, and Liquidity Risk are listed in [P4] (CT Universal MAP Cautious C KIID) but not stated in the report's risk disclosures or product-specific risks section.
      - The report does not disclose that during a cash transfer the money will not be invested for a period of time and therefore will not be affected by market conditions, as stated in [P10] Aviva Key Features.
      - The report does not disclose the Defaqto fund rating methodology or lack of rating (N/A) for the CT Universal MAP Cautious fund as shown in [P7], which may be material information about fund performance benchmarking.
      - The report does not explicitly address the critical yield analysis across growth scenarios: the new solution requires the fund to achieve 2.9% critical yield (mid growth) and 5.9% (high growth) to match the existing pension, whereas the existing pension has 0% critical yield at low growth [P5], indicating material additional outperformance burden not made explicit to the client.
      - The report states risks are 'detailed in product literature' but does not provide the client with specific knowledge of which risks apply to the recommended fund beyond generic wording; the fund-specific risks (Derivative, Fixed Interest, Credit, Smaller Companies, Emerging Markets, Liquidity) from [P4] and [P2] are not restated or highlighted in the report's risk section.
    The report satisfies the primary trigger by disclosing multiple material disadvantages and risks across pages 11, 16, 21, 23, and 24. However, the comparison between what the report discloses and what the evidence identifies reveals systematic gaps. (1) Generic versus specific risks: The report delegates detailed risk disclosure to product literature by stating 'All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature provided to you' [from canonical model]. The evidence shows the recommended fund carries five specific risk warnings (Derivative, Fixed Interest, Credit, Smaller Companies, Emerging Markets, Liquidity) plus a 5-year withdrawal recommendation in [P4], none of which are restated in the suitability report. The guard permits delegation to separate documents, but this finding indicates risks specific to the client's fund choice are not highlighted. (2) Cash transfer timing risk: [P10] states that during a cash transfer 'your money won't be invested for a period of time and therefore won't be affected by any changes in market conditions,' but the report does not disclose this operational risk or its implications for the client during the switch period. (3) Critical yield burden: The pension switch analysis in [P3], [P5], [P14], [P15], [P18] shows the new solution requires critical yields of 2.9% (mid) and 5.9% (high growth) to match existing maturity values. While the report states 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater' and discusses switch performance risk, it does not make explicit to the client that achieving even parity with the existing pension requires specific fund performance thresholds. The critical yield of 0.0% for the existing pension at low growth (in one EA) versus 2.9% for the new solution creates an asymmetric performance burden not prominence-highlighted in the disclosure. (4) Defaqto ratings: The research reports [P7] show the CT Universal MAP Cautious fund received 'N/A' for both individual Diamond rating and Family diamond rating, which may be material information about competitive fund positioning absent from the report. (5) Derivation check: The charge arithmetic in the derived figures confirms the new solution's total recurring charge is 0.62% versus the existing 0.54%, representing an 0.08% (£63 on £78,750 fund value) annual cost increase, which aligns with the report's disclosure of 'impact of recommended alternative' as '+0.08%' and appears adequately disclosed. The 1% ongoing advice charge is stated. No contradiction found on stated risks themselves. The report's disclosure of access delay, bond tax risks, inflation erosion, and projection limitations is present and reasonably prominent. The gaps identified are subsidiary risk factors and operational details present in supporting documents but not brought forward into the suitability narrative.

CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.4, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). Existing 5-year cumulative performance stated as 45.28% in report but evidence (P16, P17) shows approximately 36.21% for the combined portfolio. Recommended fund 5-year cumulative performance stated as 30.12% in report but evidence (P19, P20) shows 36.21% for Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II. Charge comparison assessed as 'Worse' (0.54% to 0.62%, +0.08% increase) but pension switch report (P4) shows 'Effect of switching' as £0 or 0.0% decrease at mid growth rate, suggesting no material adverse impact. 20 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts six comparison dimensions: (1) Charges: Existing 0.54% total (0.32% platform + 0.22% fund), Recommended 0.62% total (0.21% platform + 0.41% investment management), assessed as 'Worse'; (2) Risk Alignment: Existing risk level 6 (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity 35.02% and 80% Equity 64.95%), Recommended risk level 4 (Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II), assessed as 'Better'; (3) Fund Range: Both 'Over 6,900 funds available', assessed as 'Equivalent'; (4) Retirement Options: Both 'Full flexi-access drawdown and UFPLS available', assessed as 'Equivalent'; (5) Adviser Servicing: Existing 'Not under adviser agency', Recommended 'Moved to TFAS agency with ongoing professional oversight', assessed as 'Better'; (6) Performance: Existing 5-year cumulative 45.28%, Recommended 30.12% (Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II), assessed as 'Worse'. The report states the switch is recommended despite worse charges and worse past performance, justified by risk alignment and adviser servicing benefits.
    File:     Evidence corroborates most dimensions: (P8, P9, P13) Existing platform charge confirmed as 0.32%. (P8, P9) Existing fund charges (Vanguard 60% and 80%) confirmed as 0.22% weighted average. (P3, P14) Recommended platform charge on Aviva Platform confirmed as 0.21%. (P19) Recommended fund (Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II) charges confirmed as 0.41% total (0.30% AMC + 0.11% Additional Expenses). (P17) Recommended fund risk level confirmed as 4, matching client's agreed risk profile. (P3, P21) Retirement options (flexi-access drawdown and UFPLS) confirmed for both. (P2) Adviser servicing benefit (moving to TFAS agency, reducing platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%) confirmed. (P1, P16, P17) Existing 5-year performance: P16 shows 'Combined Discrete Performance (GBP)' 5-year as approximately 36.21% per P17 table, not 45.28% as report claims. (P19, P20) Recommended fund 5-year performance: 36.21% per P19 and P20, not 30.12% as report claims. (P4) Pension switch report projects 'Effect of switching' as '£0 or 0.0% decrease' at mid growth rate, contradicting the charge comparison showing +0.08% increase.
    Discrepancies:
      - Existing 5-year cumulative performance stated as 45.28% in report but evidence (P16, P17) shows approximately 36.21% for the combined portfolio
      - Recommended fund 5-year cumulative performance stated as 30.12% in report but evidence (P19, P20) shows 36.21% for Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II
      - Charge comparison assessed as 'Worse' (0.54% to 0.62%, +0.08% increase) but pension switch report (P4) shows 'Effect of switching' as £0 or 0.0% decrease at mid growth rate, suggesting no material adverse impact
      - The existing risk level stated as 6 is derived from blended Vanguard holdings (60% at risk 5, 80% at risk 7) but the exact calculation and weighting is not explicitly detailed in evidence
      - Adviser servicing benefit claims platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21% but no evidence confirms this reduction applies only under TFAS agency versus existing arrangements
    The report presents a structured comparison across six dimensions with explicit 'Better/Worse/Equivalent' assessments. Five dimensions are corroborated: (1) Charges breakdown is factually accurate in component terms (0.32% platform + 0.22% fund for existing; 0.21% platform + 0.41% fund for recommended); (2) Risk alignment correctly identifies existing misalignment (risk 6 vs agreed risk 4) and recommended alignment (risk 4); (3) Fund range and retirement options equivalence is reasonable; (4) Adviser servicing benefit correctly identifies TFAS agency advantage. However, three material discrepancies arise: First, the 5-year performance comparison contradicts evidence. The report cites 45.28% for existing and 30.12% for recommended, suggesting existing outperforms by 15.16 percentage points and supporting a switch despite this disadvantage. Evidence shows the existing combined portfolio (P16 discrete performance data, P17 cumulative table) achieved approximately 36.21% over 5 years, and the recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II also achieved 36.21% (P19, P20), making them equivalent, not showing existing substantially better. The 45.28% figure does not appear in the evidence pack; the closest is the report's own historical assertion on page 9 of P1. Second, the charge comparison assesses the switch as 'Worse' (+0.08%) but the pension switch illustration (P4) projects 'Effect of switching' as '£0 or 0.0% decrease', which appears contradictory. This may reflect inclusion/exclusion of advice fees or different time horizons, but the bases are not reconciled. Third, the performance data cited appears to come from FE Analytics (P1, P16, P17, P19) but the two sets of performance figures cannot both be correct from the same source. The 45.28% for existing may reflect a different fund composition at an earlier date, or a different calculation methodology, but this is not disclosed. The comparison is structured and mostly corroborated, but the performance figures contain errors that materially understate the recommended fund's relative merit and overstate the sacrifice the client is making. Where a switch is justified by factors other than performance (risk alignment, adviser servicing), overstating the performance disadvantage is a weak justification component. The charge increase (+0.08% or £63 annually on current fund value) is not adequately justified by the benefits listed, and the evidence that the new fund will perform equivalently to the old one is masked by the incorrect historical performance assertion.

  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts multiple benefits of switching: (1) platform charge reduces from 0.32% to 0.21%; (2) current funds have risk level 6 which exceeds agreed cautious balanced profile of 4, switching ensures alignment; (3) access to ongoing professional fund monitoring; (4) adviser charges deductible directly; (5) £3,600 contribution utilises annual allowance with 20% tax relief; (6) administrative convenience via single Aviva platform; (7) recommended fund provides steadier performance with less severe drops. On costs: exit penalty £0, initial advice fee £4,794 (3% of total invested), annual charge difference +0.08% (£63 more per year), out-of-market exposure 10-15 working days. The report states the proposed plan must outperform current by £63 annually to justify the switch.
    File:     The pension switch report [P3] [P5] shows projected values under different growth scenarios at age 60. At mid growth rate (2.94%), existing pension projects to £83,300 and new solution also £83,300 with effect of switching £0 or 0.0% decrease. At high growth rate (5.88%), existing projects to £93,400, new solution £93,400, also £0 effect. At low growth rate, existing £74,000, new solution £74,000, £0 effect. The comparison shows identical projected values and zero switching effect across all growth scenarios. Personal Pension Plan review [P6] [P7] [P11] confirms existing holdings are Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% (64.95%) and 60% (35.02%), with 10-year volatility of 12.34 mapping to risk level 6, exceeding agreed risk level 4. The new solution is identified as Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II [P4]. No provider documentation in the file confirms absence of guaranteed benefits or minimum returns on existing arrangements. The Advised Pension Information LOA [P8] confirms no guaranteed annuity rates, no guaranteed minimum pension, no Market Value Adjustment or early transfer charges.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims platform charge reduces from 0.32% to 0.21% but no corroborating evidence found in file for these specific rates. Closest figure is 0.1% reduction shown in [P3].
      - Report states annual charge difference is +0.08% (£63 more), but pension switch report [P3] [P5] shows effect of switching as £0 or 0.0% across all three growth scenarios (low, mid, high), with identical projected values between existing and new solution.
      - Report asserts recommended fund provides steadier performance with less severe drops over 5-year period, but pension switch report shows no performance advantage: projected values are identical in all scenarios tested.
      - Report identifies recommended fund as Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II [P4], but existing arrangements show Vanguard LifeStrategy funds [P7]. No documentation explains whether this is a switch to a genuinely different fund or a platform consolidation of equivalent risk-adjusted holdings.
      - Report cites 3% initial advice fee (£4,794) but this combines pension transfer (£79,092), GIA (£57,120), ISA (£20,000), and new contribution (£3,600). The breakdown and justification for this bundled fee across multiple product types is not supported by evidence.
    The requirement tests whether switching benefits are supported by the evidence and whether higher costs are justified. Critical inconsistency: the report's central cost-benefit claim fails internal validation. The report states the proposed plan is 0.08% more expensive (£63 annually) but must outperform to justify switching. However, the pension switch report [P3] [P5] projects identical maturity values (£83,300 mid-growth, £93,400 high-growth, £74,000 low-growth) with zero effect of switching across all scenarios. This contradicts the assertion of a cost disadvantage requiring outperformance. The report's stated platform charge reduction (0.32% to 0.21%) cannot be verified against any passage in the file; closest reference is [P3]'s 0.1% figure. The risk alignment benefit is supported: [P6] [P7] [P11] confirm existing holdings map to risk level 6 against agreed level 4, and the switch to Multi-Asset Plus is stated as risk level 4. However, the claimed performance benefit (steadier with less severe drops) is contradicted by the switch report showing identical projected outcomes. The advice fee of £4,794 (3% across pension, GIA, ISA, and new contribution) is disclosed but the justification for bundling unrelated product switches into one fee, and the breakdown across products, is absent. The no-exit-penalty finding is supported [P8]. The file contains no alternative options analysis or rejected alternatives comparison. Safeguarded benefits: [P8] confirms no guaranteed annuity rates or guaranteed minimum pension exist, so no protected benefits are being surrendered—this is properly addressed. The administrative convenience cited is a valid non-financial benefit of consolidation. The tax efficiency claim (£3,600 contribution, 20% relief) is generic and not product-specific; it is met but not differentiating to the switch. The 10-15 working day out-of-market exposure is disclosed. The fundamental issue is that the report's cost-benefit narrative (higher charges justified by lower risk and better performance) is undermined by the pension switch analysis showing no projected difference in outcomes, making the case for switching rest entirely on non-financial factors (consolidation, adviser access) rather than investment merit. This is not necessarily wrong but is inadequately explained and internally inconsistent.

No material safeguarded benefit, guarantee or valuable feature is being lost that the report has failed to address or that contradicts the evidence.
