  [G9.4] Alternatives to the switch considered
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report states that five alternatives to switching were considered: (1) Leave existing pension untouched - rejected due to risk misalignment (risk level 6 vs agreed profile 4); (2) Leave in place and switch funds only - considered but no rejection reasons stated; (3) Switch into another existing pension - considered but no rejection reasons stated; (4) Switch into employer's scheme - considered but no rejection reasons stated; (5) Set up new pension plan - considered but no rejection reasons stated. Additionally, stakeholder pension rejected for limited flexibility and cost, and investment pathway rejected for lack of personalisation. Report asserts these alternatives were considered and ranked by maturity value.
    File:     Evidence shows that five options were listed in the suitability report at pages 7 and 19. P1 confirms the do-nothing option was rejected citing risk misalignment. P2 lists all five options as considered. P3 (pension switch report) states 'The available solutions were ranked by Maturity Value along with a generic stakeholder pension and the existing pension. The top 10 results are shown below' but the top 10 comparison table referenced is not fully visible in the passages. P3 also shows stakeholder pension and investment pathway were considered with stated rejection reasons. No evidence directly addresses Options 2, 3, or 4's rejection reasoning - the switch report lists them as considered but does not explain why they were rejected.
    Discrepancies:
      - Options 2, 3, and 4 (in-situ switch, alternative product switch, employer scheme) are stated as 'wasConsidered: true' in the canonical model with empty 'rejectionReasons' arrays, but no evidence in the file explains why these options were rejected or ruled out
      - The suitability report lists options at page 7 but does not provide rejection reasoning for Options 2, 3, and 4 beyond mentioning they were considered
      - The pension switch report discusses ranking solutions by maturity value and comparing top 10 results but the actual comparison table showing how Options 2, 3, and 4 performed relative to the recommended solution is not provided in the passages
      - Stakeholder pension rejection is supported (P1, P2 quote 'Stakeholder pensions also typically do not allow for flexible access and have limited fund ranges'), but specific cost comparison claimed ('more cost effective than typical stakeholder pension') lacks numerical evidence
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Detailed rejection reasoning for Option 2 (in-situ fund switch) - why this was considered but ruled out, Detailed rejection reasoning for Option 3 (switch to alternative existing pension) - why this was considered but ruled out, Detailed rejection reasoning for Option 4 (switch to employer scheme) - why this was considered but ruled out, and whether employer contribution loss was addressed given the fact find (P20) confirms employer scheme is available and client is eligible, The top 10 maturity value comparison table referenced in P3 that would show how alternative options ranked against the recommended solution, Numerical cost comparison between recommended solution and typical stakeholder pension to support claim it is 'more cost effective'
    The report identifies five specific alternatives and explicitly states they were considered. However, the evidence supporting this assessment is incomplete. Option 1 (do nothing) rejection is well-supported: P1 and P2 both cite risk misalignment (risk 6 vs agreed profile 4) with direct quotes. Stakeholder pension and investment pathway rejections are also evidenced with stated reasons. However, Options 2, 3, and 4 are presented as 'considered' with no documented rejection reasoning. The canonical extraction shows empty rejectionReasons arrays for these three, and no passage explains why an in-situ fund switch, alternative pension provider, or employer scheme option was ruled out. P3 references a ranked comparison of 'top 10 results' against 'a generic stakeholder pension and the existing pension' but this comparison table is not fully visible in the provided passages. Critically, given that P20 (Fact Find) confirms the client's employer currently operates a pension scheme and the client is not a member but is eligible to join, the failure to document consideration and rejection of Option 4 (employer scheme) is a material gap. COBS 9.2.2R and 9.2.5R require alternatives to be considered and the reasoning documented. The guard 'Options assessed once for the whole case may legitimately cover several plans' does not apply here as there is only one plan being switched. The rejection of Option 1 on grounds of risk misalignment is client-specific and well-reasoned. The lack of reasoning for the other options suggests either they were not properly considered or the adviser's reasoning was not documented. The comparison of charges shows the new solution at 0.62% total ongoing cost (0.21% platform + fund charges + 1% advice fee) versus a stakeholder pension typically 1.5% for 10 years then 1%, but this calculation is inferred rather than explicitly stated. The critical yield analysis in P3 and P4 provides some comparative framing but does not explicitly address why Options 2, 3, and 4 were inferior choices.

  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts switching costs of: exit penalty £0, initial advice fee £4,794 (3% of total invested amounts), and an annual charge difference of +0.08% (£63 per annum). The report states the new solution will need to outperform the existing plan by £63 in the first year. The report identifies seven benefits of switching: lower platform costs (0.32% to 0.21%), risk alignment (from level 6 to level 4), professional oversight, adviser charging facilitation, tax-efficient contribution (£3,600 at 20% relief), platform integration convenience, and steadier performance with less severe volatility over 5 years. The report concludes the recommendation is to switch.
    File:     The pension switch report [P6] confirms the recommendation to switch from 'Current Aviva' (personal pension, no platform) to 'Pension Portfolio - Choice' SIPP on Aviva Platform. The switch report [P2] and [P3] show projected values at age 60 are identical or near-identical between existing and new solution across all growth scenarios (low 0.0% decrease, mid 0.1% decrease, high 0.1% decrease). Critical yield thresholds are 2.9% at mid-growth and 5.9% at high-growth. The switch report [P7] shows an initial adviser charge of £0.00 for the pension switch itself, with an ongoing 1% annual charge. No exit penalties are disclosed. Benefits lost on the existing product are listed as 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' [P7]. The report makes no quantified comparison of the platform cost reduction (0.32% vs 0.21%) in the switch report itself; the cost comparison relies on the suitability report's assertion.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states initial advice fee of £4,794 (3% of invested amounts) but the pension switch report [P7] shows an 'Initial adviser charge of £0.00' for the pension switch itself, creating ambiguity about which costs apply to which transaction
      - Report asserts annual charge difference of +0.08% (£63 annually) but the pension switch report [P2][P3][P7] shows effect of switching as £0 or 0.0% decrease at low growth, £100 or 0.1% decrease at mid and high growth, suggesting the 0.08% increase figure does not appear in the supporting switch analysis
      - Report states platform charge reduces from 0.32% to 0.21% but this specific comparison does not appear in the pension switch report passages; the switch report does not break out platform charges separately
      - Report claims current funds have risk level 6 but risk level is not stated for existing holdings in the pension switch report [P1] which shows the new solution recommendation fund only; the risk level 6 vs level 4 comparison is asserted in the suitability report without corresponding evidence in the switch analysis
      - Report lists 'Professional Oversight', 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', 'Tax-Efficient Contribution' and 'Platform Integration' as benefits but these relate to the advisory relationship and account structure rather than to comparative fund performance or pension features, and are not specifically addressed as switch benefits in the switch report
    The suitability report recommends switching on the grounds that benefits outweigh costs. The costs identified are: (1) exit penalty £0 (corroborated by [P6][P7]), (2) initial advice fee £4,794 asserted but not found in the pension switch report which shows £0.00 initial adviser charge for the switch itself — this ambiguity arises because the £4,794 may cover multiple transactions (pension, GIA, ISA) but is presented as a switching cost, and (3) ongoing annual cost increase of 0.08% (£63) asserted in the report but the switch analysis [P2][P3][P7] projects an effect of switching of £0 or 0.1% decrease in value, not an increase. This is a material discrepancy. The report's projections at all three growth scenarios (low, mid, high) in the supporting switch analysis show identical or marginally lower maturity values for the new solution compared to existing, not higher values. The 0.08% annual increase assertion contradicts the effect-of-switching figures in [P2] and [P3] which show no cost detriment or a small gain. The report cites seven benefits: (a) lower platform costs, (b) risk alignment, (c–f) advisory and administrative benefits, and (g) steadier performance. Of these, (a) risk alignment is addressed in the switch report by the new fund selection, and (g) performance is referenced in [P8] showing 5-year historical analysis. However, (c–f) are not pension-specific benefits but rather platform or adviser relationship benefits, and are not comparable between the 'existing' and 'new' arrangements in a switch analysis sense. The critical yield thresholds [P2] of 2.9% (mid-growth) and 5.9% (high-growth) indicate the new solution requires this growth just to match the existing solution's value; this is a wash, not a benefit. No evidence is provided that these critical yields are likely to be exceeded. The guard permits non-financial benefits to justify net cost increases where argued explicitly, but here the report argues financial benefits (lower costs, risk alignment) yet the switch analysis shows no financial benefit and a slight annual cost increase (0.08%). The non-financial benefits (oversight, facilitation, convenience, integration) are general platform or advice benefits, not switch-specific, and do not constitute a replacement rationale in the COBS sense. The evidence does not support the claim that benefits exceed costs.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states that five main alternatives were considered: (1) Leave existing pension untouched—rejected due to risk misalignment (risk level 6 vs agreed level 4); (2) Leave pension in place and switch funds only—considered but no rejection reasons stated; (3) Switch into another existing pension arrangement—considered but no rejection reasons stated; (4) Switch into employer's pension scheme—considered but no rejection reasons stated; (5) Set up new pension plan—considered but no rejection reasons stated. Additional alternatives include: Stakeholder pension rejected as typically lacking flexible access and having limited fund ranges, with the recommended solution stated as more cost effective; Investment pathway rejected as not reflecting individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile as accurately; Retain Moneybox Cash ISA rejected based on performance comparison (4.5% variable vs 4.72% average over 5 years for recommended ISA funds).
    File:     The evidence shows: (1) Existing pension held Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% (risk 5, 35.02%) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% (risk 7, 64.95%), with weighted average risk approximately 6.6, not matching the agreed risk level 4. The report recommends moving to Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II (risk level 4). (2) Pension switch report [P2], [P6], [P9] show comparison tables of existing vs new solution with projected values at age 60 showing no material difference (£74,000 vs £74,000 low growth; £83,300 vs £83,300 mid growth; £93,400 vs £93,400 high growth at low growth and mid growth scenarios, with £100 or 0.1% decrease at mid-high scenarios). Effect of switching shows £0 or 0.0% decrease at low/mid, and £100 or 0.1% decrease at high growth rates. (3) Critical yield required from new solution ranges from 0.0% to 5.9% depending on growth scenario. (4) Reduction in yield (impact of charges) is 0.6-0.7% for new solution vs 0.6-0.7% for existing at mid-high growth rates. (5) No benefits were listed as being lost when switching [P6]. (6) Research reports [P16] show agreed risk level 4 'Cautious Balanced' was selected based on interactive discussion. (7) Existing holdings performance [P14] shows Vanguard 60% returned 9.93% (2021), -11.22% (2022), 10.14% (2023), 9.65% (2024), 11.99% (YTD); Vanguard 80% returned 14.44%, -8.81%, 11.83%, 13.2%, 14.27% respectively. (8) Recommended fund [P17] (Aviva Multi-asset Plus II) performance shows 5.58% (3M), 12.61% (6M), 11.92% (1YR), 33.59% (3YR), 36.21% (5YR), 70.66% (10YR). (9) No rejection reasons provided in evidence for options 2, 3, 4, 5 despite report claiming they were considered.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims risk level 6 for existing funds but combined weighted average of Vanguard 60% (risk 5) and Vanguard 80% (risk 7) at stated allocations (35.02% and 64.95%) yields approximately 6.6, not 6
      - Report states 'Lower Costs' as reason for switch but pension switch report shows total recurring charges increase by 0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62%) when new platform charge 0.21% plus fund charges are combined, contradicting the cost improvement claim
      - Report provides rejection reasons only for Option 1 (risk misalignment) and the Stakeholder/Investment pathway/ISA alternatives, but states Options 2, 3, 4, 5 were considered without providing any rejection reasoning or comparison analysis in evidence
      - Projected maturity values in switch report show essentially no benefit from switching: £0 or 0.0% difference at low/mid growth, only £100 (0.1%) difference at high growth, yet this is not prominently disclosed as a weakness of the recommendation
      - Report claims recommended solution is more cost effective than stakeholder pension (max 1.5% for 10 years then 1%) but does not provide numerical comparison of actual charges to support this claim
    The requirement asks whether 'other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client.' The analysis must distinguish between: (1) alternatives the report claims to have considered, (2) whether those alternatives were actually compared, and (3) whether the recommended solution represents a better outcome. Report-Side: The report asserts five pension alternatives and three product-level alternatives (Stakeholder, Investment pathway, ISA) were considered. Only one—the 'do nothing' option—receives substantive rejection reasoning (risk misalignment). Options 2-5 are listed as considered but provide no rejection reasons whatsoever. Additional alternatives (Stakeholder, Investment pathway, Moneybox ISA) receive stated reasons. Evidence-Side: The pension switch report provides detailed maturity value projections showing no material benefit from switching (£0 at low/mid growth, £100 at high growth when both grow at same rate). The reduction in yield (charges impact) is essentially identical (0.6-0.7%) for both arrangements. The critical yield required is 0.0-5.9% depending on scenario. Discrepancy 1 – Risk weighting: The report states current funds have 'risk level of 6' but the existing holdings show Vanguard 60% (risk 5, 35.02%) and Vanguard 80% (risk 7, 64.95%), yielding a weighted average of approximately (5×0.3502)+(7×0.6495)=6.45-6.50, not 6. This is immaterial to the risk alignment case but indicates imprecision. Discrepancy 2 – Costs: The report's rationale includes 'Lower Costs' [P5] stating platform charge reduces from 0.32% to 0.21%. However, the pension switch report [P2], [P6] shows the total annual recurring charge increases: existing shows 0.54% (0.32% platform + 0.22% weighted fund charges), new shows 0.62% (0.21% platform + 0.41% fund charges), a difference of +0.08%. This contradicts the stated cost benefit. Additionally, an ongoing 1% adviser charge is added on top [P6]. The switch report table [P6] explicitly states 'Impact of Recommended Alternative: +0.08%' increase. This is a material contradiction: the report claims cost savings as a primary justification, but the switch analysis shows costs increase. Discrepancy 3 – Alternative consideration: Options 2-5 are stated as considered but provide zero rejection analysis in the evidence. The report contains no comparison table, no explanation of why each was rejected, no analysis of their outcomes versus the recommendation. Without this, the claim that 'alternatives provide better outcomes for the client' cannot be tested. The requirement is to demonstrate that the recommended alternative provides better outcomes; silence about why the other options were rejected leaves this unsupported. Discrepancy 4 – Maturity value alignment: The switch report projects identical or near-identical maturity values (£0-£100 difference across all growth scenarios) assuming both arrangements grow at the same rate. This is correct methodology (critical yield analysis) but it means the primary benefit claimed for switching—the recommended fund provides 'better outcomes'—is not supported by projected pension values. The only stated benefit is risk alignment (moving from risk 6 to 4). The report does not address why a switch that yields no additional pension value but adds charges is appropriate. The required benefit to justify switching at these charge levels would require a material outperformance assumption, but the switch report assumes parity ('same investment growth'). The critical yield required is 0.0% at mid growth—meaning zero additional growth is required to break even—yet the recommendation proceeds. Discrepancy 5 – Stakeholder pension comparison: The report states the recommended solution is 'more cost effective than a typical stakeholder pension' [P5, P3] and that stakeholder pensions have max charges of 1.5% for 10 years then 1% [P3]. However, no numerical comparison is provided. The recommended solution charges (0.54% existing + 0.62% new + 1.0% adviser = 1.54-1.62% total) do not clearly undercut a stakeholder at 1.5% for 10 years once adviser charges are included. This claim is unsupported by numerical evidence. Guard Check – Consolidation objective: [P24] Fact Find states employer eligibility: client is eligible to join employer scheme but has not. This is relevant to Option 4 (Switch to employer scheme). No analysis addresses whether this was considered or why it was rejected. Option 4 received no rejection reasoning in the report. This is a material gap: a workplace scheme with employer contributions (if available) would typically provide better retirement outcomes than a personal pension without employer contribution. The fact Find shows the client became eligible but the report provides no analysis of this option. Guard Check – Do-Nothing Option: Option 1 is rejected on the basis that current funds (risk 6) do not match agreed risk profile (4). This is valid reasoning and is client-specific. However, Option 2 (stay in existing plan, switch funds only) would address this risk mismatch at lower cost (avoiding provider switch charges) and should have received detailed comparison. Its absence of rejection reasoning is a material finding. Vulnerability Overlay: The extraction note identifies employment status ambiguity: client reports 'current earnings are sufficient to support recommended contribution level' but actual current employment income appears to be zero (fully retired after factory work, brief part-time driving role ended). The report claims £3,600 gross contribution recommended with tax relief benefit, but if current earnings are zero, the tax relief claim is incorrect and the contribution may not be supported by income. This is not directly a replacement suitability issue but affects whether the recommended solution (which includes a contribution) is appropriate. It does not directly affect the alternatives comparison but does undermine the overall recommendation rationale. Conclusion on Alternatives Comparison: The report fails to provide substantive comparison analysis for four of five pension alternatives (Options 2-5), stating they were considered but providing no reasoning or outcome comparison. The one substantive alternative comparison (Stakeholder pension) lacks numerical support. The primary recommendation justification (Lower Costs) is contradicted by the switch report showing a +0.08% increase in annual charges. Projected maturity values show no material benefit from switching (£0-£100 across scenarios), meaning the primary outcome benefit is risk alignment, not value improvement. The report does not address why alternatives that would achieve the same risk alignment (Option 2: in-situ fund switch within existing plan) were rejected. This represents a material gap in demonstrating that 'other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client'—the evidence does not show they provide better outcomes; it shows parity or worse on pension value, with the primary benefit being risk profile matching.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report claims the recommended arrangement will deliver 'lower costs' by moving to TFAS agency terms reducing platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%, and also asserts 'This small increase in charges is justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight.' The report states total ongoing annual recurring charge will increase by 0.08% (£63 annually), requiring the new solution to 'outperform your current plan by £63 in the first year'. Benefits claimed include: lower platform costs (0.32% to 0.21%), improved risk alignment (current funds risk level 6 vs agreed level 4), professional fund monitoring and portfolio review services, adviser charging facilitation, tax-efficient contributions (£3,600 gross with 20% relief), and platform integration convenience.
    File:     The pension switch report [P1] [P4] confirms existing platform charge of 0.32% and shows the new solution is Aviva Platform SIPP. However, passage [P1] states 'Platform fee: The charge is 0.32% of the fund value' for the existing pension. The switch comparison [P4] shows projections with 'Reduction in yield' of 0.6% for existing and 0.7% for new solution at mid growth rate, but also shows 'Effect of switching' as £0 or 0.0% decrease across all growth scenarios [P2] [P5]. The performance analysis [P17] shows the existing Current Strategy returned 45.28% over 5 years versus the recommended Aviva Multi-asset Plus II at 30.12%, demonstrating outperformance by existing funds despite higher risk. Passage [P9] details existing fund charges: Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% at 0.22% and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% at 0.22%, with weighted average 0.22%. The suitability report [P14] [P16] [P17] confirms risk alignment rationale and acknowledges 'Although your current funds have outperformed the recommended funds, they have suffered much steeper losses'. No evidence appears in the file of any additional benefits such as 'ongoing professional fund monitoring', 'adviser charging facilitation', or specific platform integration advantages beyond what is standard.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'lower costs' moving to TFAS terms reducing platform from 0.32% to 0.21%, but simultaneously states total annual recurring charge increases by 0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62%), contradicting the 'lower costs' benefit claim
      - Report asserts 'this small increase in charges is justified by improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight' but the pension switch report shows 'Effect of switching' as £0 or 0.0% decrease in projected maturity value across all growth scenarios, meaning no outperformance is required or projected
      - Report's 5-year performance data shows existing funds returned 45.28% cumulatively versus recommended fund at 30.12%, directly contradicting the narrative that the switch provides superior outcomes despite accepting higher costs
      - Claimed benefit of 'professional fund monitoring and portfolio review services' is stated in the suitability report but no corroborating evidence of such services appears in category E or G documents
      - Claimed benefit of 'adviser charging facilitation' enabling 'ongoing financial planning support without separate payments' appears unsupported by the pension switch documentation or GIA illustration
      - The report's own charge comparison reveals the platform charge reduction (0.32% to 0.21%, saving 0.11%) is completely negated by increased fund charges, yet 'lower costs' is presented as a primary benefit
    The requirement asks whether evidence supports claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits. The comparison can be performed because both the report's figures and the pension switch report are present. The core contradiction is this: the report asserts both 'lower costs' (platform 0.32% to 0.21%) and that the solution is justified despite 'a small increase in charges' (0.08% total annually). These claims sit in tension. Examining the evidence: (1) Platform charge does fall 0.11%, from 0.32% to 0.21%. (2) Total annual recurring charge rises 0.08%, from 0.54% to 0.62%. This implies fund charges increased by 0.19% (0.08% + 0.11% = 0.19% net increase in fund charges, assuming constant platform charge composition). (3) The pension switch report [P2] [P5] shows projected maturity values identical or higher for the new solution at all growth rates, with 'Effect of switching' uniformly £0 or 0.0% decrease. This is internally inconsistent with requiring '£63 outperformance in the first year'—the illustration suggests no additional return is needed. (4) Risk alignment is evidenced: existing funds show combined risk level 6 (Vanguard 60%=5 + 80%=7), agreed level is 4, recommended fund is risk 4 [P17]. (5) The performance data [P17] shows the existing portfolio significantly outperformed the recommended fund over 5 years (45.28% vs 30.12%), directly undermining any narrative that the switch improves outcomes. (6) Benefits of 'professional oversight', 'adviser charging facilitation', and 'ongoing monitoring services' are asserted by the report but no corroborating evidence appears in categories E, G, or F. The suitability report [P14] [P16] acknowledges that 'a switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement' but does not address the specific problem that accepting higher ongoing costs requires outperformance that the illustrations suggest is not expected. The cost increase is real (0.08% annually = £63/year on a £79k fund), and while risk alignment is genuine and evidenced, the other claimed benefits lack documentation. Under the decision logic, a switch justified on 'lower costs' that the report's own comparison table shows increased costs is a High severity finding. The claimed justification—'improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight'—addresses only one element (risk), while the oversight and adviser services benefits are unsupported by retrievable evidence in the file.

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that switching from the existing Aviva Personal Pension to the new Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice on the Aviva Platform is justified by: (1) lower costs — platform charge reducing from 0.32% to 0.21%; (2) appropriate risk alignment — current funds have risk level 6 which does not match the agreed Cautious Balanced profile of 4, and the recommended fund (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II) is specifically designed for risk level 4 investors; (3) ongoing professional oversight and investment monitoring; and (4) tax-efficient growth through pension contributions. The report concludes: 'By making these changes, you will benefit from lower costs, appropriate risk alignment, ongoing professional oversight, and tax-efficient growth, ensuring your pension remains on track to support your retirement goals.' It also states: 'This small increase in charges is justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight,' acknowledging that total recurring charges increase by 0.08% despite platform charge reduction.
    File:     The pension switch report [P3] recommends switching from Aviva Current Aviva (Personal Pension Plan, no platform) to Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice (SIPP on Aviva Platform). [P4] confirms platform fee of 0.32% on existing pension with transfer value £79,092.32. [P5] shows the new solution investment is 100% in Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II. [P7] presents the projected value at retirement as identical across all growth scenarios (£74,000 low growth, £83,300 mid growth, £93,400 high growth), with 'Effect of switching' shown as '£0 or 0.0% decrease' across all scenarios. The switch report states the critical yield is 0.0% at low growth, 2.9% at mid growth, and 5.9% at high growth. Provider documentation [P13] confirms the existing plan allows partial transfers, flexi-access drawdown, and has no exit penalties. [P2] confirms the existing Aviva plan has no guaranteed annuity rates, no guaranteed minimum pension, and no guaranteed growth rates. No safeguarded benefits or guaranteed features are identified in the evidence as being lost. The charges comparison in [P3] table shows total projected values as equivalent between existing and new solutions.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report claims 'lower costs' as a benefit of switching, but the charges breakdown shows total annual recurring charges increase from 0.54% (existing: 0.32% platform + 0.22% weighted fund charges) to 0.62% (new solution), representing a 0.08% increase in total charges, not a reduction. The platform charge does reduce from 0.32% to 0.21%, but this is offset by fund charges in the new solution.
      - The report states the cost increase is 'small' and 'justified by improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight,' but the pension switch report [P7] projects the Effect of Switching as '£0 or 0.0% decrease' across all growth scenarios, meaning the switch produces no financial advantage to offset any cost increase.
      - The report references ongoing professional oversight and monitoring as a benefit, but this appears to relate to the 1% annual advice fee charged on platform investments [P17], which is separate from the product and platform charges and is not explicitly compared in the switching analysis.
      - The extracted data indicates the existing Aviva plan is described as having 'current contributions' recorded as 'No' and 'transfers into the plan' as 'No' [P11], suggesting no active contributions were being made. However, the switch report [P4] states 'Ongoing contributions: None' for the existing pension but the suitability report recommends a £3,600 gross pension contribution [not shown in rationale but referenced in recommendations]. The basis for this contribution recommendation and how it relates to the switch justification is unclear.
    The comparison can be performed: the report makes specific claims about costs, risk alignment, and benefits, and the evidence includes switching analysis, charges schedules, and projections. However, significant contradictions emerge. First, the cost-saving rationale is contradicted by the data: the report claims 'lower costs' but the arithmetic shows total recurring charges increasing by 0.08%. The platform charge does fall from 0.32% to 0.21% (an 0.11% reduction), but this is more than offset by fund charges, resulting in a net increase. Second, the financial benefit rationale is undermined: the switch report [P7] projects identical maturity values across all growth scenarios, with 'Effect of switching' as £0 or 0.0% decrease, meaning the switch delivers no financial advantage. The critical yield comparison shows breakeven points (0.0%, 2.9%, 5.9%) but these do not translate to projected outperformance. Third, the 'appropriate risk alignment' rationale appears sound in principle (aligning to agreed risk profile of 4), but lacks documentary support: the evidence does not confirm the existing funds are risk level 6, nor does it confirm the new fund is risk level 4 or designed for that profile. Fourth, the 'ongoing professional oversight' benefit conflates two separate items: the ongoing investment monitoring (a service described in [P17]) and the 1% annual advice fee, neither of which appear as a switching cost in the charges comparison, suggesting they are priced separately and do not directly justify the switch. The guard on charge rationale applies: the report rests the switch partly on cost savings that its own comparison contradicts. The maturity value analysis [P7] shows the switching recommendation produces no financial edge, making the cost increase harder to justify. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to apply here as no specific vulnerability characteristics are flagged in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment.

CHK-010 — Vulnerability, Support Needs and Foreseeable Harm Overlay
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 5 requirement(s). 5 raised a concern (G10.1, G10.2, G10.3, G10.4, G10.5). The report asserts a support need for 'ongoing professional oversight and investment monitoring' characterised as 'Inferred' from the recommendations, but this appears to describe the service being offered rather than a support need arising from client vulnerability or capability limitation. The report records Yvette as third-party support with 'Informal' authority, but the Fact Find records no third party present at the meeting and the agreement procedures treat spousal notification as post-death procedure, not ongoing advisory support. The report claims isPowerOfAttorneyGranted=true but the file contains no Power of Attorney documentation, verification, or evidence in category A passages that a PoA is in place or what its scope covers. 7 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G10.1] Details of additional support and needs, including third-party support
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts two support needs: (1) 'Requires ongoing professional oversight and investment monitoring' linked to OBJ4, stated as inferred from the suitability report's recommendations section; (2) 'Requires simplified administration and single point of contact' linked to OBJ4, stated in the Objectives section as a client-expressed desire. The report identifies third-party support: Yvette (Spouse) with 'Informal' authority type. The report also records that a Power of Attorney has been granted (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted = true).
    File:     The risk profile report [P1] records the client's knowledge and experience comprehensively: 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares or EIS/VCT; 'Very little understanding / knowledge' of pension planning and retirement income; but 'Yes, by myself' for cash/savings investments and 'Yes, by myself' for stock investments within last 5 years. The suitability report [P3] confirms 'very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' and describes capacity for loss as 'Medium-High' with good guaranteed income and significant liquid funds providing 'greater resilience to investment volatility'. The Fact Find [P20] records the meeting as 'Telephone - Recorded' with 'Anybody else present at the Meeting? No'. The agreement to proceed [P6] specifies that spouse notification procedures apply on death but does not record Yvette as an active support contact or representative for ongoing advice. The Client Agreement [P23] mentions 'reasonable adjustments' available on request but none are recorded as being requested or implemented. No accessible format requirements or communication adjustments are documented. The Fact Find [P7] records 'Accessible format requirement: No Requirement' and 'Preferred delivery method: No Preference'. No evidence of a Power of Attorney assessment or verification appears in the file; category A passages do not record formal third-party authority documentation.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts a support need for 'ongoing professional oversight and investment monitoring' characterised as 'Inferred' from the recommendations, but this appears to describe the service being offered rather than a support need arising from client vulnerability or capability limitation.
      - The report records Yvette as third-party support with 'Informal' authority, but the Fact Find records no third party present at the meeting and the agreement procedures treat spousal notification as post-death procedure, not ongoing advisory support.
      - The report claims isPowerOfAttorneyGranted=true but the file contains no Power of Attorney documentation, verification, or evidence in category A passages that a PoA is in place or what its scope covers.
      - The client's knowledge profile (very little understanding of investing, stocks, shares, pensions) aligns with a Cautious Balanced risk profile but the report does not explicitly link this knowledge limitation to the support needs identified; no specialist communication, simplified presentation, or additional safeguards are documented as implemented.
      - The Fact Find records 'No Requirement' for accessible format and 'No Preference' for delivery method, but does not record whether these were actively offered or discussed with the client.
    The report records two support needs and identifies Yvette as informal third-party support, but the evidence does not substantiate either at the required level. The first support need ('ongoing professional oversight') describes the service package being sold, not a support need identified through vulnerability assessment. The second ('simplified administration and single point of contact') is correctly captured as a client objective but conflates objective fulfilment with support need. The client's very limited knowledge of investments and pensions (from [P1] and confirmed in [P3]) is a material FG21/1 driver under 'low capability' but the report does not articulate how this drives the support needs, nor does it document measures taken to support understanding—such as simplified language, staged communication, or capacity confirmation. Yvette's role is stated as 'Informal' authority in the third-party support structure, but [P20] records her as absent from the telephone meeting and [P6] treats spousal notification only as a post-death procedure. The claim that isPowerOfAttorneyGranted=true lacks any supporting documentation: no Power of Attorney is cited in category A passages, no verification is on file, and no scope or effectiveness is described. The Fact Find [P7] records that no accessible format was required and no delivery preference was stated, but does not evidence that these were discussed or offered. The report does not cross-reference these capability and knowledge limitations when establishing support needs, nor does it describe specific adjustments made to communication, decision-making process, or ongoing monitoring to compensate for low capability in investment knowledge. The support needs as stated are insufficiently grounded in assessed vulnerability characteristics and are not supported by documented measures or third-party involvement beyond marital status.

  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report (canonical model) contains no extracted values at the path /vulnerability/perClient[]/communicationPreferences. The extraction note confirms this path is absent from the document.
    File:     The case file contains communication preference data in [P7], a Fact Find document.       - The report does not reference or build upon the marketing preference consent recorded on 04/08/2025.

    Communication preferences were recorded in the Fact Find (P7) as 'No Preference' for delivery method and 'No Requirement' for accessible format. These represent standard capture of contact preferences rather than vulnerability-specific adjustments. The guard provided applies here: a standard contact preference is not a vulnerability adjustment; its presence does not evidence that vulnerability was assessed. The suitability report itself contains no discussion of how the client will receive communications, what format will be used, or whether the 'No Preference' and 'No Requirement' answers were revisited in the context of the advice being given. The report is silent on communication preferences altogether. This is not a contradiction (the report does not assert a different preference), but it represents an absence of application or reference to the preferences that were captured. The distinction matters: the file holds a record of preferences being asked, but the advice document does not indicate those preferences were considered in determining how to present complex pension, investment and tax information to this client. The trigger for this check is hasVulnerabilityIndicators=true, indicating the case carries vulnerability drivers; communication preferences should therefore be explicitly considered and reflected in how the advice is structured and delivered.

  [G10.3] Adjustments made to support client comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts three adjustments made to support client comprehension: (1) Recommendation for a single multi-asset fund for pension to maintain simplicity rather than complex fund selection, linked to objective OBJ1 and stated on page 14 under 'Fund recommendation' with the quote 'You want to keep the investment strategy of your pension fairly simple'; (2) Ongoing 1% annual adviser fee to provide continuous professional support and monitoring, linked to objective OBJ4 and stated on page 19 under 'Future contact and ongoing services'; and (3) Consolidated platform providing single point of access for all investments (pension, ISA, GIA), linked to objective OBJ4 and stated on page 8 under 'My Recommendations'. The report also identifies three client characteristics relevant to comprehension: 'Very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' (page 5, Knowledge & Experience section); 'Do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand' (page 5, Your attitude to risk section); and 'May take some time to make investment decisions and tend to be somewhat anxious about those decisions' (page 5, Your attitude to risk section, referencing the Cautious Balanced risk profile).
    File:     The case file contains evidence supporting the identified client characteristics. P9 states: 'you describe yourself as having very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' and 'You have a fair degree of understanding of cash accounts, savings accounts, and Cash ISAs, but no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes.' P12 confirms: 'You have limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand, but you are suited to a Cautious Balanced approach to investing.' P9 also states regarding the risk profile: 'A Cautious Balanced risk profile shows that you may take some time to make investment decisions and tend to be somewhat anxious about those decisions.' Regarding adjustments, P3 confirms the single fund approach with 'Platform Integration - Keeping your pension, ISA and GIA on the same Aviva Platform will provide administrative convenience and a single point of contact for all your investments.' P3 also confirms the 1% adviser fee: 'The costs for this service are 1% per annum across all your Aviva Platform investments.' The Fact Find (P5) documents the advice areas and meeting details. P2 records that 'Client did not have time to complete an ATRQ today, so have sent him an email version to complete himself on Defaqto' and shows marketing preferences ('Related Products/Services Only') and 'Accessible format requirement: No Requirement' and 'Preferred delivery method: No Preference'. No specific support adjustments addressing the identified comprehension difficulties are documented in the case file beyond those already cited from the report itself.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report identifies three significant client characteristics indicating low financial comprehension and decision-making anxiety, yet the case file shows no evidence of specific adjustments designed to address these characteristics beyond the generic simplification of fund selection and platform consolidation. The Fact Find records 'Accessible format requirement: No Requirement' and 'Preferred delivery method: No Preference', suggesting no tailored communication adjustments were identified or implemented despite the documented low knowledge level.
      - The report does not cite or reference any communication strategy, decision-support tools, simplified documentation, plain-language explanations, staged advice delivery, or follow-up support mechanisms that would specifically address the identified characteristics of taking time to decide and anxiety about decisions.
      - P2 shows the client did not complete the ATRQ in person but was sent an email version to complete 'on Defaqto' — a self-service platform — which may be misaligned with a client described as having very little investment knowledge and finding financial matters difficult to understand.
    The requirement tests whether adjustments made to support client comprehension are fully supported by evidence and applied consistently throughout the report. The report correctly identifies material characteristics indicating comprehension and decision-making support needs: very low investment knowledge, difficulty understanding financial matters, and anxiety/time-taking in decision-making. These characteristics are well-evidenced in the file (P9, P12). However, the adjustments asserted in the report are not proportionate to the identified needs. The report cites only three adjustments: (1) single-fund simplification, (2) ongoing adviser support via 1% fee, and (3) platform consolidation for administrative convenience. These are stated as 'client-specific' adjustments but are largely standard platform and advisory features rather than bespoke comprehension supports. For a client with 'very little understanding', 'difficulty understanding', and anxiety about decisions, the lack of documented evidence for tailored communication methods, decision support tools, plain-language materials, simplified illustrations, staged advice, or structured follow-up is material. The Fact Find records show no accessible format requirement or special delivery method was identified, despite the evident comprehension barriers. The assignment of the ATRQ completion to a self-service email platform (Defaqto) appears misaligned with the documented low knowledge level. The 1% ongoing fee asserts 'continuous professional support' but the case file contains no evidence of how this support is structured, what decision-support or comprehension mechanisms it includes, or whether it was explained in a format the client could understand. While the adjustments cited are internally consistent with the recommendation, they do not demonstrate that specific measures were taken to support this particular client's comprehension needs.

  [G10.4] Vulnerability has a material impact on the recommendation (overlay)
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts one impact of vulnerability on the recommendation: 'Limited investment knowledge supported by recommendation for professionally managed multi-asset fund rather than requiring active fund selection' [P24]. The report identifies the client as having 'very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' [P16], describes the agreed risk profile as 'Cautious Balanced' appropriate for those with 'limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand' [P7, P15], and states 'we have recommended a multi-asset fund because it provides a professionally managed, diversified investment solution' [P24]. Capacity for loss is assessed as 'Medium-High' based on 'good level of guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions' and 'significant funds to cover' shortfalls [P16]. The report makes no reference to foreseeable harms, support needs beyond fund selection, communication adjustments, third-party involvement, or enhanced duty of care measures.
    File:     The file contains extensive evidence of vulnerability indicators and circumstances requiring support: (1) Knowledge/experience: Client responses show 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares and 'Very little understanding / knowledge' of pension planning [P2]; (2) Psychometric assessment: Risk profile identified as 'Cautious Balanced' for those who 'may take some time to make investment decisions and tend to be somewhat anxious about those decisions' and 'do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand' [P7, P15]; (3) Capacity for loss: The risk profile report states 'No additional notes were recorded' in both the 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section [P1] and 'Agreed risk level' section [P12], indicating assessment conducted but no specific support needs documented; (4) Communications: Fact Find shows 'No Requirement' for 'Accessible format requirement' and 'No Preference' for 'Preferred delivery method', but records that 'Client did not have time to complete an ATRQ today, so have sent him an email version to complete himself' [P14]; (5) No evidence of Power of Attorney, trusted contacts, family involvement, or enhanced communication protocols; (6) Affordability and life circumstances: Client is transitioning from 35 years in factory work to part-time driving (now ceased), creating employment income uncertainty [P3, P4]; client retired but with DB income and uncertain part-time work end date [P4]; (7) Cashflow resilience: Analysis shows narrow margins with 'Total Monthly Disposable Income' of £383.33 against £2,206.67 expenditure [P13], and multiple stress scenarios modelled (Market Crash, Tony Predeceases, Yvette Predeceases, Higher Inflation) [P8, P9, P10, P17].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report identifies limited investment knowledge as driver for multi-asset fund recommendation but does not reference the client's 'No understanding / knowledge' responses on stocks/shares or 'Very little understanding' on pensions from the investment questionnaire [P2].
      - Report describes risk profile as 'Cautious Balanced' but does not quote or reference the full descriptor that those with such profiles 'do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand' [P7, P15], which is a material FG21/1 driver.
      - Report asserts 'Medium-High' capacity for loss but provides no evidence of how the capacity for loss questionnaire was completed or what notes were recorded, despite the risk profile report showing 'No additional notes were recorded' in that section [P1, P12].
      - Report makes no reference to the narrow disposable income margin (£383.33 monthly) [P13] despite recommending an additional £3,600 annual contribution, nor addresses how this affects vulnerability to market volatility.
      - Report does not reference or address the employment income uncertainty (retirement after 35 years, subsequent part-time work now ceased) [P3, P4] in relation to ability to sustain recommended contributions or resilience to investment losses.
      - Report identifies support need (professionally managed fund instead of active selection) but does not document other potential support requirements around communication, monitoring, or decision review given the limited financial knowledge and anxiety indicators.
      - Report contains no discussion of foreseeable harms, no mention of review triggers, no reference to enhanced monitoring given the Cautious Balanced profile indicators of anxiety about investment decisions.
    The report supports its core recommendation (multi-asset fund for limited knowledge) with evidence that the client demonstrates very limited investment knowledge and anxiety about financial decisions. However, the overlay requirement asks whether vulnerability information is 'fully supported' and whether the recommendation 'accounts for' identified characteristics across support needs, communication, and duty of care considerations. Three material gaps exist: (1) Incompleteness of vulnerability assessment documentation: The risk profile questionnaire responses [P2] show clear FG21/1 drivers (low capability, anxiety indicators), and the risk profile descriptor [P7, P15] explicitly names 'limited experience' and difficulty understanding financial matters as the profile rationale, yet the report does not integrate this language into its reasoning about why multi-asset funds were chosen. The capacity for loss section shows 'No additional notes were recorded' [P1, P12], suggesting the assessment was conducted but not documented beyond the concluded 'Medium-High' rating. (2) Narrow consideration of support needs: The report addresses only fund selection simplicity but does not evidence consideration of other support dimensions—communication accessibility (though 'No Requirement' is recorded [P14]), decision-making support, or review protocols. For a client described as having 'very little understanding' and a profile indicating they 'tend to be somewhat anxious', silence on these dimensions is material. (3) Failure to address material circumstantial vulnerability: The client's employment history shows a 35-year factory career followed by brief part-time work now ceased, leaving employment income status ambiguous [P3, P4]. Combined with monthly disposable income of only £383.33 [P13] and a recommendation to contribute £3,600 annually, the resilience to recommended contributions and investment losses is not evidenced. Multiple stress scenarios are present in the file [P8, P9, P10, P17] but the report does not reference capacity to sustain the plan under adverse conditions given the limited knowledge and narrow income margins. The guards instruct that age alone is not vulnerability and that genuine absence of assessment should be noted rather than manufactured. Here, assessment has occurred (questionnaires completed, risk profile assigned, capacity determined), but the report's treatment of vulnerability in its recommendation is narrow—it justifies one feature (fund simplicity) while leaving other dimensions undocumented. This is not absence of assessment; it is incomplete integration of assessment findings into the recommendation rationale and support design. Under FG21/1 and PRIN 2A.2.1R, where a client presents capability constraints and anxiety indicators, the duty of care requires that the recommendation demonstrate how the firm will support informed decision-making and manage foreseeable harm—not just select an easier product.

  [G10.5] Vulnerabilities recorded in the file and in the report
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client has 'Possible' vulnerability in the category 'Capability'. Three characteristics are recorded: (1) 'Very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' (stated, reported modality); (2) 'Do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand' (stated, actual modality); (3) 'May take some time to make investment decisions and tend to be somewhat anxious about those decisions' (stated, actual modality). All three are quoted from the suitability report itself (document category D_RiskCapacityAndExperience, pages 5). The report discusses a Cautious Balanced risk profile and states capacity for loss as 'Medium-High' based on guaranteed income and significant funds.
    File:     The Fact Find [P1, P2, P7] records client communication preferences and data protection consent but contains no explicit vulnerability assessment or capability diagnosis. Notes [P8, P9] indicate the client was unable to complete an investor questionnaire in person ('Client did not have time to complete an ATRQ today, so have sent him an email version to complete himself on Defaqto') and records informal observations ('Only ATR 4. Expected growth is 4.5%, they already get this on moneybox ISA's'). The Aviva Key Features [P21] notes that accessible formats are available upon request but records no capability concern or accommodation. The Fact Find [P1] shows 'Accessible format requirement: No Requirement', suggesting no access needs were identified at point of capture. The report [P18] itself quotes the ATRQ completed on 9 August 2025 describing the client's investment knowledge, but the Fact Find shows the ATRQ was sent for completion by the client after the initial meeting.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report characterizes the client as having 'Possible' vulnerability and limited understanding of financial matters, but the Fact Find records 'No Requirement' for accessible formats and no explicit vulnerability flag at the time of fact-find completion (06/08/2025). The ATRQ responses relied upon by the report were completed after the initial meeting, not during the face-to-face assessment.
      - The suitability report quotes the ATRQ as evidence of understanding limitations, but the Fact Find notes indicate the ATRQ was sent to the client by email to complete himself on Defaqto, rather than being administered in a controlled setting where the adviser could assess comprehension in real time.
      - The file records no formal vulnerability assessment, capability review, or follow-up from the adviser after receiving the completed ATRQ, despite the report's assertion of limited financial understanding.
      - The Fact Find [P1] records marketing preferences but contains no record of how the vulnerability—once identified—led to adjustments in communication method, document format, or adviser support.
    The suitability report asserts that the client has a capability vulnerability based on questionnaire responses indicating 'very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' and that he 'may take some time to make investment decisions and tend to be somewhat anxious about those decisions.' These statements are drawn directly from the client's own ATRQ responses and the risk profile descriptor, both quoted verbatim in the report [P18]. However, the supporting evidence presents a gap in process rather than a contradiction. The Fact Find [P1] shows that the ATRQ was not completed during the telephone meeting on 06/08/2025; instead, the adviser noted 'Client did not have time to complete an ATRQ today, so have sent him an email version to complete himself on Defaqto.' The ATRQ was completed on 9 August 2025, three days after the fact-find meeting. This means the vulnerability was identified post-meeting, not contemporaneously. The Fact Find itself records 'Accessible format requirement: No Requirement,' suggesting that at the point of first engagement, no capability concern was flagged or acted upon. The report characterizes vulnerability as 'Possible' rather than confirmed, and capacity for loss as 'Medium-High' based on cashflow and income security, which indicates the adviser believes the client's circumstances mitigate the capability concern. The report does not evidence what adjustments or support measures were implemented in response to the identified capability limitation. The file contains no record of a follow-up call or meeting after the ATRQ was returned, no confirmation that the client understood the recommendation, no evidence of simplified documentation provided, and no record of whether the adviser discussed the findings with the client or offered additional support. The cashflow analysis [P13-P20] and pension documentation [P21] are standard industry documents without apparent customization for a client with stated understanding difficulties. The Client Agreement [P11, P22] contains standard terms without apparent plain-language summary or simplified version. This represents a process gap: a capability vulnerability was identified in the ATRQ but the file does not evidence how the adviser then ensured the client's informed understanding of the suitability recommendation or what adjustments were made to support comprehension.

==============================================================================
DOCUMENTS ONE CHECK FOUND AND ANOTHER CALLED MISSING
==============================================================================

One requirement says a kind of document is on file and another says it is not. Both cannot be right, and the difference matters: a document wrongly called missing turns evidence the file holds into a gap the adviser is asked to explain. Check the passage ids — the group reporting the absence may simply not have been given the passage, which is a retrieval problem rather than an advice one.
